#5888. enSights brings AI-powered business management to solar and storage operations

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 16, 2026

As solar and energy storage portfolios grow in both size and complexity, managing performance is just one part of the equation. Owners and operators must also keep track of power purchase agreements (PPAs), warranties, service contracts, billing, compliance requirements and project-level profitability.

enSights is looking to bring those responsibilities together with the launch of its AI Studio, a new addition to its Energy Business Management (EBM) platform. The company announced the launch Nov. 10.

The AI Studio is designed to give solar and storage owners, operators, and asset managers access to a customizable team of AI specialists that can support business and operational workflows. Through no-code tools, users can create AI dashboards and reports using natural-language instructions, while still establishing their own workflows, routines, data-access permissions and approval requirements.

The approach here reflects a broader shift toward renewable energy software, it’s moving beyond monitoring individual assets toward managing the financial and operational performance of entire portfolios.

According to enSights, its EBM platform brings together energy management systems (EMS), production data, billing, PPA information, operations and maintenance, contractual obligations and compliance on a unified data foundation. That integration is central to the company’s AI strategy.

Rather than limiting AI applications to identifying equipment issues or answering generation-related questions, the platform is designed to connect energy production with the contracts and financial obligations associated with each project.

The AI Studio includes an orchestrator that coordinates specialized AI agents focused on areas like monitoring, operations, revenue, warranties, permits, lenders, insurers, and asset management. For portfolio managers, those capabilities could help reduce the time spent reviewing disconnected systems and manually reconciling information across departments. Automated reviews of contractual obligations, revenue performance and operational alerts could also help teams identify issues earlier and direct attention to matters that still require human intervention.

The enSights AI Studio dashboard provides solar and energy storage operators with AI-powered tools for asset management, operational workflows, and financial oversight. Credit: enSights

enSights emphasized that people remain in control of the AI Team. Users determine which workflows the agents handle and where approvals are required. Physical asset controls remain governed by established EMS and site-level systems.

The AI Studio also builds on an expanded financial management offering within the EBM platform. Its Financial Asset Management Module includes revenue assurance, contract, and obligation management, and profit-and-loss reporting.

The module is intended to provide project-level financial visibility, helping companies identify and prevent revenue losses while generating reports for investors.

The platform also includes real-time EMS, weather and tariff forecasting, along with dispatch planning intended to optimize revenue while maintaining grid safety and local compliance.

enSights says its platform is deployed globally across more than 100,000 sites, representing over 5 GW of assets. The company plans to demonstrate the AI Studio and its broader EBM platform at RE+ 2026.

As AI gains traction across the clean energy industry, the next competitive advantage could come from connecting operational intelligence with the financial and contractual decisions that determine how successfully renewable energy portfolios perform.

#5885. The BLUETTI Apex 200 Expandable Portable Power Station — CleanTechnica Tested

BLUETTI continues to innovate, finding new ways to move its portable power station technology into meaningful new formats that provide owners with additional capability, portability, and utility.

They recently reached out about a new stackable portable power station format based around the Apex 200 power station. It packs in a meaningful 2.5 kilowatt-hours (kWh) of storage capacity by itself, but the real magic is that it was built to be modular and can be linked up with additional storage modules to be stacked underneath, connecting directly to the main unit.

Disclaimer: BLUETTI provided the Apex 200, B300K, and a pair of solar panels to the author for the purposes of this review.

This modular format is a game changer because it means you don’t need an installer to bolt 300 pounds of batteries to the wall of your house. Instead, you can order them in manageable formats and add more as you need them. Among other things, this capability allows the owner to assemble and disassemble the battery pack that meets their needs when they need it.

If you’re heading out to camp for a week in your RV, you can grab an Apex 200 and one of BLUETTI’s B300K expansion batteries and bring 5.5 kWh of capacity with you on the road. When you get home from the trip, you can put them back into the garage and add that back into the battery capacity for your home.

The BLUETTI Apex 200 serves as a head unit or master controller for the battery stack, while the other battery modules are effectively servants to it. For example, when you’re setting the unit up to charge from a wall outlet or from a set of BLUETTI’s solar panels, which can wire directly into the Apex 200 with MC4 connectors, you simply plug one set of connectors or one AC charging cable into the Apex 200 and it automatically balances the energy storage across however many additional battery packs are plugged into the unit.

That really simplifies the charging structure and allows you to use the battery exactly how you want to. If you want to just head to the park with two solar panels that are portable, you can do that and have a much more manageable power system, effectively bringing a mini-grid with you on the go.

We tested the BLUETTI Apex 200 out on a wall charger and, much like most of the other portable power stations on the market, it effectively maxes out the capacity you can actually draw from a wall outlet, which is around 1,600 watts continuous. Even with the best charging cables, you’re going to notice some heat building up in the cable, and BLUETTI has made some concessions for this. The Apex 200 has a switch that allows you to select from different charging modes:

When it comes to solar, the BLUETTI Apex 200 can plug in to solar arrays through either an XT60 connector or the MC4 connectors that wire directly into the unit on the side. Between the MC4 connectors and the XT60 solar input, the unit can pull down 3,000 watts of solar, though you will need to make sure the voltage on your panels lines up with what each of the ports on the unit can handle. When we initially wired our two Bluetti 100-watt solar panels into the unit, we had them wired in parallel, which resulted in just 40 volts being pushed into the unit.

Stacking them in series gave us 80 volts, which pushed it over the minimum 60 volts for the MC4 connectors and allowed the BLUETTI Apex 200 to start charging from the panels. Pushing 2,000 watts maximum of solar power into a 5.5 kWh battery stack is going to take some time to get it charged up, but it’s easy to manage scaling the input power system up to an impressive 2,000 watts to really deliver some meaningful power.

The flexibility is pretty impressive, because it means you can dial the system in to exactly what you need, whether that’s bolting an array to the top of your RV or installing solar panels on the side of your house and then wiring them in a way that’s the most convenient for you.

BLUETTI also makes a larger B500K battery, so you can really pick and choose exactly the right level of storage that you need for your house and the right size battery packs that you want to be able to move around.

The MC4 connectors for solar panels are a little bit troublesome on the BLUETTI Apex 200 because they require a special tool to release the locks that connect the male and female ends of MC4 connectors together. That means if you have your solar panels wired up and plugged into the unit, you’ll need an MC4 connector release tool to disconnect the solar panels from the Apex 200 before you move on. Alternately, you can wire the solar panels up through an XT60 connector, which is a simple plug-and-play adapter for lower voltage solar setups.

When it came time to use the stored power from the BLUETTI Apex 200 and the B300K battery pack, we had to pick up some more high-draw appliances to really put them the the test. We bought a 1,500-watt electric fireplace and 1,500-watt portable heater for a total draw of around 3,000 watts by. We also grabbed an an Instant Pot from the kitchen to pull a bit more juice from the battery stack.

The BLUETTI Apex 200 handled both of the heaters running full blast without a problem. It shared a continuous power output capacity of 3,200 watts. This gets bumped to 3,600 watts when you attach a B300K extension battery. Beyond that, you can even pull a little bit more power, pulling peak usage up to 4,800 watts for a few seconds, because of its peak power handling capacity.

Keeping the larger heater unit attached, we added an Instapot to the the mix, and again the unit had no issues no matter what power levels we used. It’s a solid stack with impressive stats that actually deliver when you need them to the most.

BLUETTI has really built an impressive system here with the Apex 200 and its different expansion battery options. A single BLUETTI Apex 200 can be expanded up to 33.3 kWh, and they can even go bigger than that if you need something with more storage.

BLUETTI allows you to link three Apex 200 battery stacks together through their Hub 1 accessory for a total of just under 100 kWh of storage capacity. That can be accomplished by stacking three Apex 200s with a bunch of BLUETTI’s 5kWh B500Ks and then linking them all together through the Hub 1.

It’s a massive system that shows BLUETTI isn’t just building products for a quick sale, but building a much more comprehensive, flexible storage system that you can build at home into the right configuration for your needs with components that act more like LEGO building blocks.

The ability to wire in solar via XT60 connectors and MC4 connectors is exciting, giving homeowners even more options to produce power from the sun for them to run their lives on. The BLUETTI Apex 200 retails for $1,399 USD.

For more information about the BLUETTI Apex 200, the B300K, B500K expansion packs, or their solar panels, head over to their official website.

#5879. NEW: Sierra Club Report Shows Data Centers’ Potential to Erase 15 Years of Climate Progress

Despite Americans’ Disapproval, Data Centers Rush to Build Climate-Threatening Projects

Washington, D.C. — Sierra Club has released a new report that shows that without swift, critical guardrails, artificial intelligence data centers in the United States could undo 15 years of climate progress.

The power demand brought on by data centers combined with utility companies’ rush to fossil fuels has severe and threatening climate impacts. The Artificial Intelligence, Real Pollution report shows that between data centers connected to the electrical grid and those that are building their own on-site power supply, these projects could add 680 million tons of greenhouse gas emissions into the atmosphere.

Sierra Club’s Dirty Truth Report, released in September 2026, reflected the alarming rate at which utility companies are building new methane gas power plants to power artificial intelligence data centers. Today’s report shows that additionally, data center projects are building massive amounts of on-site power sources that are subject to more lenient guardrails and reporting standards.

This report comes on the heels of the 2026 midterm elections, in which voters across the country overwhelmingly supported candidates who promised more guardrails and accountability on data center projects. Voters also passed nearly every ballot measure that would slow the breakneck speed at which data centers are developing. Artificial Intelligence, Real Pollution does not just outline the real climate risk that the industry’s current growth trajectory poses, it shares substantive and impactful strategies that states can implement right now to prevent harmful emissions from gas plants while protecting ratepayers from rising costs caused by data center demand.

In response, Sierra Club Chief Program Officer Holly Bender issued the following response:

“For twenty years, the Sierra Club has led the environmental movement’s most successful campaign—the Beyond Coal Campaign—with a clear-eyed vision that a clean power grid is the backbone of any climate strategy. Millions of people have contributed to this work, advocating for clean air, clean water, and investments in clean energy across the country. We face an unprecedented risk to our hard-won progress, as the picture of the climate impact of artificial intelligence data centers and their power demand becomes clear. We now know that as currently planned, this industry could put all of our progress at risk.

“Artificial Intelligence, Real Pollution shows the staggering amount of greenhouse gases that would be emitted into our air if we allow data centers and utility companies to continue their rush to fossil fuels. More pollution will worsen the extreme weather families are facing, cause illnesses, and drive up the cost of healthcare and disaster recovery. Just two weeks ago, Americans unequivocally demanded more accountability, more transparency, and more guardrails on AI data centers. With this report, the Sierra Club has not only clarified what’s at stake, but shared a menu of strategies that can be implemented right now to protect consumers, communities, and the climate.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5877. When Less Is More: Rewilding as a Strategy for Nature Recovery

Can we bring nature back from the brink of collapse? That may sound melodramatic, but the evidence is sobering: the Planetary Health Check 2026 shows that we have crossed 7 of the 9 science-based planetary boundaries;[1] the Living Planet Report 2026 finds that wildlife populations have declined 73% on average since 1970;[2] and around 1 million animal and plant species are threatened with extinction, with 75% of the land surface having been significantly altered by human activity, according to the IPBES, the leading intergovernmental body for assessing the state of biodiversity.[3]

In fact, with vertebrate extinction rates up to 100 times the natural background rate, some scientists believe we are at the onset of a sixth mass extinction.[4] But perhaps we should not be surprised at this precipitous decline in the health of our living planet. After all, around $7.3 trillion is spent every year on activities that harm nature ($4.9 trillion by companies and $2.4 trillion in government subsidies), while only $220 billion goes towards protecting or restoring ecosystems (2023 figures).[5] In other words, for every $1 we spend on taking care of nature, $33 is spent on activities that degrade the environment.

The drivers of nature loss are many, including climate change, overexploitation of resources, pollution and invasive alien species, but the largest is land-use change, primarily for agriculture.[6] Research by UNEP and Chatham House goes so far as to identify agriculture as a threat to 86% of species at risk of extinction.[7] So, is regenerative agriculture the answer? That is certainly one approach that would help, as I explored in a previous CleanTechnica article. But there is another, more direct—and some would say more radical—solution that is gaining popularity: rewilding.

As with regenerative agriculture, rewilding is more a collection of ideas and practices than a well-defined science. Nevertheless, experts convened by the IUCN define rewilding as the process of rebuilding a natural ecosystem, following major human disturbance, by restoring natural processes and food webs.[8] The Global Rewilding Alliance puts it even more simply, as “helping nature heal”.[9] In practice, this can mean anything from leaving areas of nature undisturbed to actively introducing keystone species such as beavers, bison and wild boar.

Perhaps the most cited rewilding experiment is the reintroduction of wolves into Yellowstone National Park in 1995–96, after they had been eradicated there by 1926. This resulted in a so-called trophic cascade, whereby elk numbers fell, scavengers such as eagles, ravens and bears gained a year-round food supply, and willow and aspen began to recover in places.[10] The strength of that recovery, and the extent to which the wolves were the primary driver, has been contested,[11] but few dispute the overall positive effect on biodiversity and the ecosystem.

The Yellowstone case is rewilding on a large scale, but it is still dwarfed by the rewilding work of Tompkins Conservation, created by the late Doug Tompkins, co-founder of The North Face and Esprit, and his wife Kristine McDivitt Tompkins, former CEO of Patagonia. Working primarily in Chile and Argentina, they have helped create or expand 16 parks protecting over 45 million acres of land and sea, and reintroduced 14 species, including around 50 wild jaguars.[12] Besides the ecological benefits, ecotourism is boosting local economies.[13] In Chilean Patagonia, every dollar the state invested in the national parks along Chile’s Route of Parks in 2024 returned US$6 to local communities,[14] while visitors to Argentina’s Iberá Park, partly degraded by cattle ranching in the past, have doubled in a decade to 60,000 a year.[15]

At a more local scale, the Knepp Estate in the UK has become another well documented case of rewilding. In her book Wilding: The Return of Nature to a British Farm, Isabella Tree tells the story of how she and her husband, Charlie Burrell, converted their 1,400-hectare farm from intensive dairy and crop agriculture, which was losing money, into a thriving “wild farm,” with free-roaming herbivores such as cattle, ponies, pigs, and deer.[16] A 20-year review shows an incredible rejuvenation of biodiversity, including a 916% increase in breeding bird abundance in the southern block, a 132% rise in bird species richness, a near-ninefold increase in dragonflies and damselflies, and a doubling of butterfly species in some areas.[17] There are climate benefits too, with 5.2 tCO2e/ha/yr sequestration, comparable to new native woodland.[18]

Critics of rewilding often frame it as a trade-off: more nature means less food; more wildlife means fewer jobs; more biodiversity means lower economic growth. These arguments can (and should) be tested, for in many (but not all) cases they will prove to be a false choice. My view is that it should be about balance. I saw this pragmatic approach at a rewilding site in Norfolk, England, called Wild Ken Hill, where they have designated separate areas for regenerative farming, coastal conservation, and rewilding—including 30 or so wild Exmoor ponies, 6 very inquisitive and vocal Tamworth pigs, and a small herd of Red Poll cattle.

My conclusion on rewilding is that it can certainly help us to reach the global goal of reversing biodiversity loss and becoming nature positive. It is by no means the only strategy, but it is one of the most direct and impactful. As I noted in the chapter on nature restoration in my book Thriving: “We know that nature is on the ropes, bruised and battered by dumb agriculture and dumber economics. But another thing we know is that, given half a chance, nature bounces back. Ecosystems are resilient and designed to flourish. The most powerful and effective action we can take is to step back and give nature space and time to recover.”[19]

This article is adapted and updated from content in Wayne Visser’s book “Thriving: The Breakthrough Movement to Regenerate Nature, Society, and the Economy (New York: Fast Company Press). For more information, see waynevisser.com.

#5876. Soneil Spark introduces its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System

This week, Soneil Spark rolls out its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System (Mobile BESS), the portable, modular battery storage built into a towable trailer.

The company, offering bespoke, modular energy solutions, is introducing Mobile BESS at RE+ 2026. It delivers temporary backup and off-grid power, working with what’s available by taking power from the grid, a generator, or solar.

“Wherever your business goes, the power goes with it. Simply tow it where energy demand grows,” said Sonya Jain, CEO of Soneil Spark, in a statement. “Start with one unit and add more as your needs change.”

It’s a simple set up, the company said. Mobile BESS works with no crane, no trenching, and no utility wait. That makes it a natural fit for a job site which may be months away from permanent service, a film set or event that can’t tolerate generator noise and fumes, a utility crew restoring power after a storm, or a remote mine or farm beyond the grid’s reach.

On sites that already run generators, the Mobile BESS syncs with them to cover peaks and start-up gaps, so the generators run fewer hours. Where diesel is restricted, it offers a quiet alternative.

Where containerized storage is built to be placed once, the Mobile BESS is built to be towed and to work in any weather. It includes:

The Mobile BESS is built for generator and equipment rental, construction, emergency and backup power, utilities, electrical contractors, events, film and entertainment, and off-grid sites such as mining and agriculture. A hybrid option adds EV charging.

#5873. China’s Electric Heavy Trucks Are Taking Sales from Diesel and LNG

In September 2026, battery-electric trucks captured 49.8% of China’s new domestic heavy-truck registrations. Of 65,800 trucks registered, 32,800 ran on batteries. Another 36 used hydrogen fuel cells. Battery electric accounted for more than 99% of the broader new-energy category. China’s freight operators are buying batteries at a scale that puts diesel and LNG suppliers under pressure.

The shift has gathered speed. Battery electric accounted for about 13% of domestic heavy-truck registrations in 2024, 28% in 2025 and nearly 38% in the first nine months of 2026. The figures come from compulsory-insurance registrations for trucks sold into the domestic market, excluding exports. China’s total domestic truck market remains below its pre-pandemic levels. Battery trucks are gaining share while the market for new heavy trucks remains constrained.

From May through September, new-energy heavy trucks exceeded 40% of new registrations for five straight months. The category reached approximately 226,400 vehicles in the first nine months, up 64% from the same period in 2025, and nearly 99% were battery electric. Tractor units that pull semi-trailers are leading the change. In September, 64.48% of new domestic tractor registrations belonged to the new-energy category. Electric trucks are moving freight on ordinary commercial routes, including work beyond ports and industrial yards.

My full TFIE Strategy Briefing traces China’s heavy-truck sales from 2015 through 2026, examines real freight costs and charging choices, and assesses the consequences for manufacturers, logistics companies and fuel demand.

The operating economics explain much of the appeal. Trucks earn money by carrying cargo, so annual kilometres, payload, electricity prices, charging stops and vehicle availability matter as much as the price on the invoice. High-mileage routes make electricity and maintenance savings more valuable. A costly grid connection or charging queue can eat into those savings. Fleet operators need to evaluate the truck and its route together.

Recent ICCT case studies document how those economics play out. A Guangdong warehouse fleet of 28 electric trucks had estimated five-year ownership costs 16% below comparable diesel trucks. In Yangtze River Delta express delivery, 50 electric tractor-trailers working routes of 300–500 km achieved a narrower 3% advantage over six years without purchase incentives. Another operation covering 900 km a day with strategically placed high-power charging showed estimated ownership costs 19% below diesel. The differences reflect route, utilization and charging arrangements.

Those three operations offer a more useful procurement lesson than a single quoted range figure. The operator starts with cargo, schedules and energy access, then selects equipment. The narrow 3% margin in express delivery also shows why financing and charging costs can determine whether a viable electric route makes money.

Battery swapping has carved out a sizable part of this market. In September, 20.09% of newly registered battery-electric heavy trucks were classified as swap-capable. That compares with 22.92% across January–September 2026 and 30.39% for all of 2025. The number of swap-classified trucks continued to rise, while conventional charging gained a larger share of the expanding electric market. China’s registration classification also includes some trucks with leased batteries that are recharged rather than exchanged.

Swapping fits dense, repeatable freight operations such as steelworks, ports and bulk-material haulage. Fleet operators and station owners can standardize vehicles and use stations often enough to pay for batteries held in inventory. Conventional charging serves a wider range of routes and can fit scheduled stops, overnight parking and depot operations. Both approaches depend on predictable truck traffic and reliable electricity connections.

China is building infrastructure around those freight patterns. Yunnan commissioned 3,350 km of heavy-truck charging corridors in 2025, with 113 stations and 869 connectors. Fujian opened a 649 km coastal corridor in October 2026, including 15 charging stations and eight swapping stations. The IEA’s 2026 charging review describes the concentration of heavy-vehicle charging around freight corridors and industrial centres. Long-haul coverage still has gaps, but the network is expanding where trucks already have work to do.

Hydrogen has found little traction alongside this expansion. China registered 689 fuel-cell heavy trucks in the first nine months of 2026, compared with more than 223,000 battery-electric trucks. LNG has a much larger installed customer base, although its advantage over diesel has narrowed as gas prices change. Battery trucks offer a different cost structure built around drivetrain efficiency and lower maintenance needs. That makes the competition increasingly difficult for equipment and fuel suppliers dependent on combustion engines.

Sales changes take years to work through the operating fleet. Kpler projects that new-energy trucks, predominantly battery electric, will account for about 8% of Chinese heavy trucks in service at the end of 2026. It also estimates they will displace about 230,000 barrels of diesel per day compared with moving the same freight using diesel trucks. Freight vehicles with high daily utilization can have a large effect on fuel demand before they become a majority of vehicles on the road.

China’s June 2026 heavy-truck programme joins purchasing incentives, grid access, charging corridors, financing and logistics planning. Its 2030 targets include a 40% share of new-energy heavy-truck sales, a 20% share of the operating fleet and 30,000 km of zero-carbon freight corridors. Monthly new sales have already surpassed the sales-share target. Building and replacing enough vehicles to reach the fleet target will take longer.

Other countries have different electricity tariffs, road networks and freight routes, but the procurement method travels. Start with the actual jobs trucks perform. Identify routes with strong electric economics, plan the charging and grid connections those routes require, and finance vehicles and infrastructure as one programme. Other drivetrains can compete where their operating costs or performance are better. The evidence from China’s largest commercial freight applications increasingly favours batteries.

The complete TFIE Strategy Briefing includes the historical registration series, the ICCT ownership-cost cases, charging and swapping corridors, and fleet-turnover analysis. TFIE Strategy works with fleets, utilities and policymakers on freight-electrification scenarios and investment decisions.

#5869. The Rising Tide Of Distributed Energy Resources

Centralized power plants can trip widespread brownouts or blackouts when under stress. They make fat targets for enemy weapons, too. In contrast, distributed energy resources (DERs for short) can improve overall resiliency in the face of both natural and human-made disasters, while also enabling ratepayers to take advantage of utility discounts, and even earn revenue from their personal energy assets. The DERs field includes — but is not limited to — rooftop solar, EV charging stations, and home batteries. And, we ain’t seen nothing yet. DERs stakeholders have also begun moving into the distributed computing field as well.

DERs first surfaced on the CleanTechnica radar in the early 2000’s during the Obama administration, when the US Department of Energy began promoting decentralized, home-and community-based energy resources as the leading characteristic of a modernized and more resilient electricity grid. Made possible by the falling cost of solar, wind, and energy storage, the agency’s embrace of small-scale, localized electricity generation was a sharp contrast with the traditional emphasis on large centralized power plants for energy security (see more DERs background here).

Nowadays the DERs field embraces practically any non-centralized power generation resource, regardless of size or type. The DERs field can include, for example, a new gas power plant commissioned to run an adjacent AI data center.

To get some perspective on the journey of DERs since the early 2000’s, consider the case of the leading rooftop solar installer Sunrun. After launching in 2007, Sunrun segued into energy storage and virtual power plants, with “virtual” referring to smart grid technology that coordinates thousands of far-flung energy producing (and consuming) devices to balance supply and demand at peak periods of consumption.

For the record, Sunrun now presents itself as a storage-first company. The company’s focus remains on renewable energy but the storage field is source-agnostic, embracing practically every energy resource on the planet.

Sunrun CEO Mary Powell also prefers to use “distributed” instead of “virtual” in reference to decentralized power systems, to emphasize that hardware is the foundational element. As of this month, Sunrun ranks as the biggest distributed power plant in the US, with solar and storage assets spread among 164,000 enrolled households.

Back in 2021, Sunrun entered into an installation partnership with the home energy management startup SPAN, known for its next-generation home electrical panels among other products, and that layed the ground work for a partnership on distributed computing systems. Earlier this year, SPAN introduced XFRA, a new product consisting of computer nodes to be staged at homes and smaller commercial spaces to form a distributed data center. The model is similar in concept to the familiar Internet-enabled citizen scientist platform, except that XFRA deploys its own nodes instead of using a participant’s personal computer.

This past September, Sunrun announced its selection as an authorized installation partner for XFRA with a focus on new home developments, aiming for gigawatt-scale distributed computing networks beginning in Texas and other selected markets. “Sunrun’s battery-plus-solar systems will maintain reliable electricity for both the homes and the XFRA compute nodes, while preventing the overloading of local distribution infrastructure—allowing SPAN to maximize node deployment.,” Sunrun explains.

“By pairing home energy generation and storage with XFRA compute nodes, we are unlocking new economic value for our customers and for Sunrun, while also solving certain speed-to-power constraints that would otherwise limit AI deployment,” Sunrun CEO Mary Powell elaborated in a press statement.

Arch Rao, founder and CEO of SPAN, also chipped in his two cents. “We can now drastically increase our penetration of XFRA compute nodes by pairing them with Sunrun energy systems to deliver cost-effective AI compute capacity at unprecedented speed while also reducing compute cost for certain AI applications,” Rao said.

Back when the Energy Department initially began promoting DERs as a function of the energy transition, the agency used the term “prosumer” to describe ratepayers who are empowered to engage with their local power grid as a renewable energy producer, not simply as a passive consumer. The distributed data center adds another layer of engagement to the prosumer mix.

At a personal appearance during Climate Week in New York City in September, Powell referenced distributed computing as the latest demonstration of the company’s ongoing dialog with its customers and other stakeholders, leading to innovative new products that put more power management tools in the hands of ratepayers.

Powell reiterated the point during an appearance at the 2026 “DERVOS” summit at the Brooklyn Navy Yard on November 5, and the DERVOS gathering itself provides further insights into the widening scope of DERs activity.

“DERVOS” is a play on Davos, which is shorthand for the annual meeting of the World Economic Forum. Like the World Economic Forum, DERs stakeholders have emerged in force upon the global economy as well as here in the US, stretching far from its do-it-yourself roots in the off-gridder movement of the 1970’s.

The event itself is the brainchild of the collaborative trade organization DER Task Force. The group formally launched in 2019 but it claims roots much earlier, in the 1970’s-era New York City activist organization Energy Task Force, whose activities were highlighted by the installation of a wind turbine in the city — illegally, of course.

By the time DERVOS 2026 rolled around, the DER Task Force had become an organization with a reach far beyond New York, and it has also embraced the size-doesn’t-matter ethos  under the slogan, “Everything is DER.”

“That’s what we mean by Everything is DER: not that everything is small, or even literally ‘distributed,’ but that the center of gravity is shifting toward energy resources that are closer to load and controlled by those who need the power,” the Task Force explains (emphasis theirs).

“Sometimes that shift is bottoms-up, sometimes it’s centrally planned, but either way, DERs aren’t the sideshow anymore. They’re the main event,” they add.

I’ll have much more to report from DERVOS 2026 in the coming days and weeks, but for the time being let’s go back to the foundational idea of improving grid resiliency and security. Climate change aside, in recent years Russia and the US have both provided textbook cases for transitioning out of energy systems that involve building fat, juicy infrastructure targets for attack, alongside precariously long energy transportation systems. If you have any thoughts about that, drop a note in the comment thread.

Image: The renewable energy “prosumer” envisioned by the US Department of Energy in the early 2000’s has become the sophisticated distributed energy resources manager of today (graphic by Sara Harman courtesy of US DOE).

Tina has been covering advanced energy technology, military sustainability, emerging materials, biofuels, ESG and related policy and political matters for CleanTechnica since 2009. Follow her @tinamcasey on LinkedIn, Mastodon or Bluesky.

#5866. BYD E6Y Odyssey

A couple of friends embark on a road trip to pick their EV purchase, and it turns into an Odyssey.

The BYD E6 is a BEV classic. Globe-trotting friends have ridden in them as taxis in Singapore. Majella and I even took one for a test drive (it reminded me of a ’70s Chrysler Valiant). It was the best selling pure electric car in China in 2016, with about 600,000 sold globally, and it is viewed by many as ahead of its time. So, when one showed up at our coffee and cake morning tea, I had to interview the owner, Joanne, and her road trip friend, Meredith. What they shared with me was truly an odyssey of determination to get Jo’s car home. Please note: this story took place 6 years ago. Charging availability has improved and so has Joanne’s knowledge of living with an electric vehicle.

Joanne told me that she had been looking for an EV for two years when a search on carsales.com found an ad for an E6Y (the taxi version of the BYD E6). It was a pre-COVID import and had been mothballed at Sydney airport after the company importing the vehicles had decided not to go ahead with taxis. Photos of all the BYDs at the airport were widely circulated as evidence that no one wanted to buy an EV. The cars were being “sold by a mate out of the back of the factory.” At the low cost of AU$32,000, claiming a 400 km range from its 83-kWh battery and only 100 km on the odometer, Joanne saw it as a bargain.

There was some consideration of putting the car on a flatbed and having it shipped, but in the end, the two friends decided on a road trip.

Jo informs me that: “They only brought 120 of the BYD E6Y taxi versions into the country. BYD sold some GLX versions, which had more bells and whistles.”

She was soon to find out that all is not as it seems. Her lack of knowledge of EVs meant that her trip in the car from Sydney back to Brisbane would be an odyssey of Greek tragedy proportions. Jo reminisces: “Meredith reminded me our original plan had been to spend just two days driving back to Brisbane with the one overnight stay. I thought I could ‘fast charge’ the car and make it back to Brisvegas on the second day. Hahahaha. Lesson learned the hard way.” They expected it to be just a twelve-hour trip. Meredith adds, “I told her we would laugh about it one day.”

Joanne contacted the seller and planned her flight to Sydney. Meredith got excited and joined the “road trip” in Newcastle. Jo paid the money and took ownership of the car. “It took two hours to get out of Sydney.” To be fair, that wasn’t the car’s fault — just Sydney’s congested traffic. However, the fact that the previous owner had only charged the car to 50% didn’t help. She was able to charge the car to 80% on the outskirts of Sydney. “But I had no idea that I wouldn’t get the expected range at highway speeds.”

Another learning curve was the state of charging points — sure, there were plenty listed on PlugShare, but many were unavailable or broken. Again, dear reader, I must emphasize this was 6 years ago.

Her next planned stop was accommodation she had booked at Newcastle. She got to within 2 km when the battery readout said “zero.” She was unable to find a place to charge — the car had not come with a granny charger. Jo clarified her Newcastle experience by email: “the Uni in Newcastle was the first I tried to charge. My friend Meredith and I tried three other places to charge all with no luck. I found out later that the Newcastle chargers were notorious for not working.” She confided to me that she now knows that the car can go a little further even when it says zero battery. She also had to learn the difference between AC and DC charging. The BYD she had bought could only handle AC charging.

With the battery reading zero, she felt she had no choice but to leave the car at a nearby garage. The owner of the accommodation she had booked kindly came and picked her and her friend up. The next day, the BNB owner dropped them back to the garage and a tow truck was ordered. The tow truck took them and their car to a local winery where they trickle charged next to the golf carts. The winery also had a Tesla level 2 charger for customer use. It was charging when they arrived. “We left a note on the windscreen of the Tesla that was using the level 2. During breakfast the Tesla owner rang and said the charger was available. We left the winery fully charged and off up the highway.” Not a bad place to get stuck, methinks.

280 km up the road to Kempsie, there was access to free charging in the council carpark, but no available accommodation! That’s what happens when you travel on the Queen’s Birthday long weekend. “We were checking out the back seat of the car as a place to sleep when a friend of a friend told them he had a farm in area. ‘Have I got a place for you!’ he told them. After we finished charging, we had to drive down this dark farm track. We couldn’t plug the car in because we still didn’t have a granny charger!” They made themselves comfortable in the available beds in the barn. The next morning in the light of day they found they had driven alongside of a lagoon! “We could have easily driven into that!”

Meredith adds: “I didn’t think I’d be sleeping in a shed with a tractor and then I couldn’t sleep because of the smell of diesel.” The trip was not just a challenge, but torture.

Their next adventure was just a few kilometers up the road at Gladstone NSW. Low on juice, they contacted people through PlugShare and got a message from a local café owner. You can check out the café here. He already owned a Hyundai Ioniq, and so had a granny charger to lend. The café owner knew a fellow who was visiting his mum in town. This friend had ordered an Atto 3 from BYD and was waiting on this first shipment of BYDs to arrive. Jo took him for a ride around town in her car. “This was his first ride in a BYD.”

He arranged for them to leave their car at his mother’s home in the next town (Smithtown). Jo and her friend were now in a hurry, as both had to get back to Brisbane to go to work the next day. Leaving the car charging, “We belted back to Brisbane in this horrible little roller skate hire car.” Home in Brisbane, Jo had to plan how to retrieve her BYD purchase. Meredith did not want another “road trip.”

This time, Jo travelled south by train. Even this trip was not without drama. She missed her stop. Thankfully, she was able to get off at the next stop and change to the northbound train to make it back to where her car waited faithfully for her. She took a taxi to her car, returned the borrowed granny charger, and began her journey home. She was so keen that she ended up with a speeding ticket.

Joanne arrived at her motel in Balina with 10 km of range showing on the dashboard. But the available ocular charger soon filled up the car. By 9:00 pm, with a full battery, she couldn’t wait to go home. The fireworks display in town was a fitting sendoff for the final leg of her journey.

Jo would like to acknowledge all the good advice and offers of help she received from fellow users of PlugShare, as well as all those referred to above. She is now a confident and competent driver of her BYD E6Y and has made many trips back and forth to Sydney from Brisbane with less drama. The BYD E6Y Facebook group has been a great help. She says she would not part with her green E6Y Singapore taxi — “a comfortable and solid basic car.” Rumour has it that there are about 250 on Australian roads, with the record distance travelled over 1 million km.

Another owner commented: “This unstoppable tank will outlive me I’m sure, best of all its super basic … no internet, no lane assist, no self-driving and plenty of new and used cheap parts available. This is why it seems like there are so few … they are so good NO ONE who has one is selling them.”

Jo has even taken her car camping. “The camping trip was to Yandina up the coast. I did sleep in the back of the car on that trip very comfortably. But that time I had a mattress and bedding. I did take out the bottom half of the back seat from the car so I could get a completely flat rear area for the mattress. When I returned from the camping trip, I popped the rear seat bottom back in the car. It’s just two bolts and a couple of plastic clips that hold it in.”

And in the future, I asked? “It will be hard to part with my E6. I am waiting on the first solid state 1000km BEVs before I would consider parting with it. I just love the two-tone paint job.”

Jo is another happy battery electric vehicle owner, sharing her learning experience “odyssey” and having fun. “Thanks for the opportunity to immortalise the odyssey,” she told me in her latest email. The future is bright and electric as we learn to live with our new vehicles.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

#5859. Senate Republicans Seek to Again Raid States’ Coffers & Threaten EV Charging

FY 2027 Transportation Proposal Puts EV Charging Investments at Risk

WASHINGTON, D.C. — On Monday, Senate Republicans released an Appropriations package that proposes to take back nearly $2 billion in federal funding already allocated to states to build out electric vehicle (EV) charging infrastructure under the Infrastructure Investment and Jobs Act and repurpose it for general transportation uses, putting critical clean transportation investments at risk.

Republicans already clawed back roughly $500 million from the National Electric Vehicle Infrastructure (NEVI) Formula Program in the FY 2026 appropriations bill. Now, they are proposing to further divert these funds, even as all 50 states, Washington, D.C., and Puerto Rico have approved plans for their use and are working to deliver critical charging infrastructure to their communities. More than half of available NEVI funding has already been committed to projects, and the proposal threatens to upend additional awards that states continue to make to charging developers.

For example, Texas recently made an announcement of $226 million in awards that will support 321 stations. The Senate proposal would threaten Texas’s remaining NEVI funding, along with funding for states like Ohio, which has one of the largest numbers of operational NEVI-funded stations in the country.

In response, Clean Transportation for All Director Katherine García released the following statement:

“It is unacceptable for Senate Republicans to once again seek to claw back critical EV charging investments. Trump’s day-one freeze of NEVI set states back, and they are only now back on track after we successfully challenged that freeze in court. This latest proposal threatens affordable transportation for American families, clean air for communities, and states’ efforts to build the infrastructure of the future. We have successfully defended NEVI against the Trump administration’s attacks, and we will continue fighting to protect the program and ensure these investments reach communities across the country.”

The NEVI program, established through the Bipartisan Infrastructure Law in 2021, provides funding to states to build a nationwide network of EV fast-charging stations. Protecting that investment is essential to expanding charging access, supporting EV adoption, reducing dependence on volatile gasoline prices, and cutting transportation pollution.

Since the Trump administration’s unlawful freeze of the NEVI program was lifted following successful litigation by Sierra Club, its partners, and a coalition of states, hundreds of millions of dollars in NEVI funding have been committed to charging projects.

Congress must preserve funding for EV charging infrastructure while providing dedicated resources for passenger rail, public transit, and other clean transportation priorities.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5857. OMCO Solar offers solar-powered tracker control system

OMCO Solar now offers a version of its OMCO Star Tracker Control System powered entirely by solar energy, allowing tracker controls to operate without AC power at the site.

The system, first launched last year, is designed for OMCO Origin Factory-Direct Trackers. Its tracker controllers, array controllers and sensor bridges use all-weather battery packs charged by solar modules.

According to OMCO, the technology eliminates the need for AC power to tracker control components and an uninterruptible power supply. It also allows tracker controls to operate and protects modules against wind, snow and hail before AC power is available.

“Our industry has solved so many problems as we have grown – we’re now the leading source for new electric energy capacity in the US. This addition to the OMCO Star product line solves one more – how to protect modules if there’s no AC power to the site even for a short time. We’re excited to offer this solution to our existing customers and to new partners,” said Matt Kesler, vice president of solar technology at OMCO Solar, in a statement.

The OMCO Star system uses LoRa, or long-range, wireless technology with a communication range greater than 1 mile. According to OMCO, this reduces the number of network controllers required and allows direct communication with every tracker without relying on a mesh network.

The company says LoRa is 100 times less susceptible to electromagnetic noise than Zigbee and better able to connect to distant trackers despite obstacles.

OMCO says its all-weather battery packs allow the solar-powered controls to operate reliably in cold climates. The system includes field-replaceable batteries, a phone app that eliminates the need for buttons on controllers and a remote monitoring and control portal.

The portal allows operators to move trackers for activities such as mowing, snow plowing and module cleaning. The system is developed, manufactured and supported by OMCO Solar in the United States.

“We are excited about bringing this innovation to the Utility Scale and DG tracker markets. Over the past few years, OMCO has expanded its capabilities beyond just being a US steel manufacturer to now being a true technology leader in our space. We look forward to supporting our customers/partners with the OMCO Star Tracker Control System, improving reliability, and reducing the labor and materials needed to build their projects,” said Eric Goodwin, vice president of business development at OMCO Solar.

OMCO Solar will display the all-DC tracker control system at RE+ in Las Vegas, November 17–19, in the North Hall, booth N516.

#5852. Zoox Partnering with Uber to Bring Autonomous Mobility to More Riders

Now live in Las Vegas, with Los Angeles to follow

In March 2026, we announced a multi-year partnership between Zoox and Uber to bring our robotaxi experience to Uber’s growing autonomy platform in select cities.

Now, beginning today, Uber riders in Las Vegas will have the opportunity to be matched with a Zoox robotaxi on eligible trips. Zoox will also continue to offer rides through the Zoox app. In addition, Zoox and Uber are the official robotaxi and rideshare partners of MGM Resorts’ Las Vegas properties. Together, they’re working with MGM Resorts to make pickups and drop-offs easier and more intuitive, with improved on-property wayfinding, in-app guidance, and infrastructure improvements designed to support smoother traffic flow.

The Zoox purpose-built robotaxi gives riders the agency to create a space all their own, with design elements for enhanced socialization and safety not possible in a traditional car. Bringing this experience to Uber will help introduce the benefits of purpose-built autonomy to new, curious riders.

Within the Zoox and Uber apps, riders can take a Zoox to popular resorts and venues on and around the Strip, as well as rides in the Downtown & Arts District. More destinations will be added soon.

We will continue to offer our service through the Zoox app, in addition to rides being available through Uber in those cities.

Zoox was founded in 2014 on a bold belief: if you’re going to reinvent mobility, you have to start from scratch. Instead of retrofitting cars with autonomous technology, we designed the world’s first purpose-built robotaxi: fully autonomous, all-electric, bidirectional, and intentionally engineered around the rider experience. Since the very beginning, we’ve been focused on our vision of delivering safe and enjoyable mobility.

Like Zoox, Uber was founded in San Francisco with a vision to improve urban transportation. Uber started in 2010 as a ridehail app connecting riders with cars, but quickly evolved. Over the past decade, it expanded into shared rides, food delivery, freight logistics, micromobility, and is now building an autonomous vehicle network, consistently redefining how people and goods move through the world. By leveraging smartphone technology, dynamic pricing, and real-time data, Uber helped catalyze the on-demand economy and reshape how people get from A to B on a global scale. This partnership is a testament to Uber’s dedication to advancing transportation within the ridehail space.

Zoox has always taken a measured, step-by-step approach by starting small, learning quickly, and scaling responsibly. This launch with Uber mirrors that approach. We’re beginning with a small fleet with the potential to expand as we refine our operations, technology, and customer experience.

Uber and Zoox will launch in Los Angeles next year, after Zoox opens service on the Zoox app.

#5847. REPORT: U.S. Exceeds 200 GWh of Energy Storage Capacity Installed to Date

Rapid Growth in Energy Storage Is Helping Stabilize Electricity Prices and Strengthen Grid Reliability; Industry Urges Congress to Pass Permitting Reform to Accelerate Deployment

LAS VEGAS — The U.S. energy storage industry installed 14.3 gigawatt-hours (GWh) of new capacity in Q3, bringing total installations in the first nine months of 2026 to 48.3 GWh, according to the U.S. Energy Storage Market Outlook Q4 2026 (ESMO) released today by the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA) and Benchmark Mineral Intelligence.

U.S. energy storage capacity installed has now exceeded 200 GWh to date as grid operators, utilities, and energy buyers turn to storage to lower energy costs and strengthen grid reliability. After voters sent a clear message in the midterm elections that lowering energy costs must be a priority, the U.S. solar and storage industry is urging lawmakers to send the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act (BAAJA) to the President’s desk.

Last week, more than 300 solar and storage companies sent a letter to Congressional leadership urging swift passage of BAAJA. The legislation will accelerate energy storage deployment, reduce project costs, and expand access to lower-cost, reliable electricity for households and businesses.

“Voters made it clear that they want lower prices, and the growing deployment of energy storage is helping make that happen. We’re urging Congress to pass BAAJA to build out America’s energy infrastructure and put downward pressure on prices,” said SEIA CEO Tim Pawlenty.

The outlook for energy storage continues to strengthen, with the report increasing its 2030 cumulative U.S. deployment forecast by 3.7% to 708 GWh across grid and BTM. The revised forecast reflects sustained industry momentum, with U.S. installations up 20% year over year through the first nine months of 2026.

Texas led the nation in utility-scale storage deployment through Q3, adding 12.3 GWh of capacity. Arizona and California followed with 7.7 GWh and 6.8 GWh, respectively. Recent SEIA analysis shows that rapid growth in solar and energy storage helped drive down average wholesale electricity prices by 42% in Texas and 51% in California from summer 2021 to summer 2026.

11 states surpassed their previous annual energy storage installation records in Q3, even with three months remaining in 2026: Colorado, Massachusetts, Utah, Michigan, Georgia, Mississippi, Virginia, Iowa, Montana, Alabama, and Illinois.

“States are moving from targets to centralized procurements,” said Pete Tillotson, Senior Analyst at Benchmark Minerals. “Across utility mandates and bulk capacity contracts, energy storage pipelines are growing as a result of both investment appetite and flexibility requirements.”

U.S. storage manufacturing continues to accelerate, ensuring that more of the batteries being deployed to strengthen the grid are made in the United States. In July, a new battery cell manufacturing facility opened in Tennessee. In October, a new cell manufacturing facility opened in Indiana, bringing total cell manufacturing capacity to 92.4 GWh, a 50% increase.

Utility-scale projects accounted for the vast majority of Q3 installations, with 12.2 GWh deployed, followed by 1.3 GWh of commercial and industrial storage, and 746 MWh of residential storage.

#5840. Third Pillar Constructing Largest Floating Solar Complex in New York

Crown City Solar will be North America’s first floating community solar project. Phases 1 and 2 are under construction, and the three-phase, 22.5 MW DC complex is expected to be fully operational in 2027.

CORTLANDVILLE, N.Y. — Third Pillar Power today announced it is constructing Crown City Solar, North America’s first floating community solar project, at Suit-Kote Corporation’s Polkville Crushed Stone Facility, an active sand and gravel mining operation co-located with an asphalt plant and storage terminal in Cortlandville, N.Y.

Crown City Solar 1 is the first of three 7.5 MW-dc phases and is expected to begin commercial operation in February 2027. Crown City Solar 2 and Crown City Solar 3 are scheduled to begin commercial operation in October 2027, bringing the site to 22.5 MW-dc in total and making Crown City Solar North America’s largest floating solar complex. The complex takes its name from nearby Cortland, known locally as the Crown City because it sits on an elevated plain over 1,000 feet above sea level.

“Every quarry and reservoir in this country already has a job to do. Crown City Solar shows that those sites can take on a second one: powering their neighbors through a Central New York winter, at a community scale, on a working industrial site. The question is no longer whether floating solar works here. It’s how quickly we can bring it to the next site, like Polkville, and the one after that,” said Jaimeet Gulati, CEO of Third Pillar Power.

Third Pillar identified the approximately 120-acre flooded mine pit as an ideal host for the project in early 2024. Thanks to the collaborative efforts of Suit-Kote, the Town of Cortlandville, and Cortland County, the project team brought this first-of-its-kind project to construction less than two years after securing site control and applying for interconnection. Suit-Kote was acquired by Colas USA, the U.S. subsidiary of Colas, in October 2026.

Crown City Solar also represents a series of technical firsts. It is engineered for the conditions of a Central New York winter, including a ground snow load of 69 pounds per square foot, the highest of any floating solar project in the country. Additionally, the project will float its medium-voltage transformers and switchgear on custom-designed barges—another first for a U.S. floating solar project. Floating this equipment further reduces the use of agricultural land, addresses floodplain siting concerns, and improves electrical efficiency. These firsts reflect Third Pillar’s specialization in floating solar, from identifying and engineering projects on working industrial water bodies to securing the permits and interconnection needed to bring them online.

D3Energy is serving as the engineering, procurement, and construction contractor. The floating system uses Alps’ Solar PV modules, Hazelett Marine’s specialized elastic mooring systems, and Profloating’s floats.

“With more than a decade of building floating solar projects across the country, D3Energy is proud to bring that experience to a project of this magnitude. Crown City Solar demonstrates what is possible with floating solar. We’re grateful for our partnership with Third Pillar Power and the trust they’ve placed in D3Energy to help bring North America’s largest floating solar complex to life,” said Stetson Tchividjian, Managing Director of D3Energy.

Crown City Solar 1 is expected to achieve mechanical completion in December 2026.

#5836. Nextnova unveils solar cell manufacturing facility

The new state-of-the-art factory in Oklahoma City will create about 300 jobs, strengthen the domestic solar supply chain, and establish a new benchmark for transparency and quality.

This month, Nextnova celebrated the official inauguration of its new solar cell manufacturing facility in Oklahoma City, marking an important milestone in strengthening America’s domestic solar manufacturing industry.

The facility will manufacture high-efficiency solar cells using advanced automation, Industry 4.0 technologies, and comprehensive digital traceability.

Production is scheduled to begin in March 2027 with an initial annual capacity of 2 GW, designed to expand to 5 GW as market demand grows.

The new factory is expected to create approximately 300 skilled jobs in engineering, manufacturing, quality assurance, and operations, supporting economic growth while reinforcing the U.S. clean energy supply chain.

Nextnova celebrates the inauguration of its new solar cell manufacturing facility in Oklahoma City. Credit: Nextnova

“Today represents an exciting milestone for Nextnova and for American solar manufacturing,” said Nathan Kii Miller, Nextnova’s chief strategy officer, in a statement. “Our Oklahoma City facility combines advanced manufacturing technology, complete supply chain transparency, and rigorous quality standards to deliver premium solar cells while strengthening a resilient domestic solar industry.”

Designed for maximum quality and reliability, the facility features fully integrated manufacturing execution systems (MES), automated inspection technologies, advanced process control, and a dedicated analytical laboratory supporting continuous quality verification throughout production.

Nextnova notes that its commitment to transparent sourcing, manufacturing excellence, and scalable production provides customers with high-performance solar cells that support the continued growth of a secure and competitive U.S. solar industry.

#5834. The Harm From Plastic Is Happening In Plain Sight

It is known. Plastic is a major cause of the climate crisis, yet governments continue to ignore it.

The Environmental Justice Foundation (EJF) has issued a new briefing published to coincide with the UN Climate Change Conference, COP31, in Antalya, Türkiye. The briefing, “Reducing plastic production in a post-1.5°C world: A test of resolve for the international community,” calls on governments to reduce global plastic production.

Plastic already emits more than two billion tons of CO₂e a year, about twice as much as aviation, and under current policies this would more than double as the fossil fuel industry turns to plastic as its hedge against electrification.

“There’s only so much carbon the world can still emit if we want to avoid blasting past the Paris Agreement targets. Plastic’s harm to our climate is happening in plain sight, and governments are choosing to look away. Every new plastics plant locks in decades of emissions the planet simply cannot afford. As leaders gather at COP31, they must recognise plastic as a major cause of carbon pollution that is rapidly heating our world.” — Steve Trent, CEO, Environmental Justice Foundation

Over 90% of plastic emissions come before a plastic product is even used, during extraction, conversion and production. If current policies remain unchanged, global plastic production is projected to almost triple by 2060, and annual emissions from fossil-based plastics to more than double, to 4.3 billion tons of CO2e.

On this trajectory, plastics would emit 67 billion tons of CO2e between 2026 and 2050. That is just over half of the remaining carbon budget for a 50% chance of meeting the 1.5°C target, and 27% of the budget for a very good chance of limiting the overshoot to 1.7°C. None of this is hidden; emissions are quantified, the projections are published and the industry’s expansion plans are public, yet plastic remains on the margins of climate negotiations.

EJF is calling on governments at COP31 to recognize plastic for what it is: a major driver of climate change that must be tackled at its source, by cutting production.

The petrochemical industry’s bet on plastics

As the world electrifies transport and power, the fossil fuel industry is using plastic as its hedge against electrification. With oil demand for transport and energy expected to decline, petrochemical companies are pivoting to plastics to keep oil and gas flowing. The plastics sector could account for 20% of global oil consumption by 2050, up from around 12% today. The top fifteen petrochemical companies received an estimated US$590.8 billion in financing between 2019 and 2025, and the sector was propped up by state subsidies estimated at over $80 billion in 2024 alone, almost half of which came from Saudi Arabia.

A test of resolve for the plastics treaty

A total of 145 countries from every continent have expressed support for provisions to reduce plastic production during the global plastics treaty negotiations. Rwanda and Peru have proposed a global target to cut plastic production by 40% by 2040 against a 2025 baseline. Modelling cited in the briefing shows that a 40% cut in high-ambition countries, joined by China and emerging producers, could reduce global emissions from plastics by as much as 21% compared with business as usual.

But negotiations have reached a deadlock, driven by a small group of oil- and plastic-producing states, including Saudi Arabia, Russia, Iran and China, with the United States recently aligning with their position. At least 234 fossil fuel and chemical industry lobbyists attended the latest round of talks in Geneva — more than the combined delegations of the EU and its 27 Member States.

Final Thoughts about Plastic and our Leaders

The truth about plastics is perverse. Plastics are widely used around the world due to ease of manufacturing, low cost, stable chemical properties, and good water resistance. They have infiltrated every part of the environment and human life cycle. More than 16,000 chemicals are used in manufacturing plastics, and at least 4,200 of these have been identified as hazardous to human health and the environment. Thousands more chemicals used in plastic production have never been tested. Many of these chemicals migrate out of plastic packaging into our foods, drinks, household products, and bodies.

Researchers have come up with many possible solutions to plastic pollution, as well as ways to mitigate microplastics in the environment. But will governments have the wherewithal to stand up to the powerful fossil fuel lobby that is behind plastic propaganda?

Carolyn Fortuna, PhD, is a longtime journalist and researcher for CleanTechnica who frequently writes about climate science, renewable energy, and environmental communication. Since joining the publication in 2016, Fortuna has authored nearly 1,900 articles focusing on ecojustice, clean technology adoption, and the intersection of science and public policy. Please follow Carolyn on Substack: https://carolynfortuna.substack.com/.

#5829. From Social Leasing to Social Fleets: A Proposed Law Lends Credibility to the Goal of One Million Affordable Electric Cars

Social leasing could soon change in scale, name, and business model. The bill introduced by MP Christine Arrighi aims to create a public service offering one million “low-cost electric vehicles” (ALM). Scheduled for December 3rd in the National Assembly, its examination is expected to place at the heart of the debate a now crucial question: how to make social leasing sustainable and widespread?

Several presidential candidates have made reaching one million vehicles (or even more) one of the hallmarks of their campaigns¹. While this convergence is excellent news, the battle of figures says nothing about how to achieve this goal.

The current system cannot simply be scaled up twentyfold. Its cost per vehicle, intermittent financing, imperfect social targeting, lack of a guaranteed solution after three years, and the resale risks borne by dealerships are all structural limitations. The 2026 edition also revealed its dependence on Renault and Stellantis, which can at any time restrict volumes to favor more profitable traditional sales.

The proposed law changes its approach. Sponsored by MP Christine Arrighi, it draws inspiration from the proposals of T&E France and Bernard Julien, stemming from their study on the future of social leasing, published in June 2026.

In this new model, the government would directly purchase the vehicles at an average net cost of €15,000. These vehicles would be kept for twelve years in a public fleet and leased successively to one or more households for an average monthly rent of €150 before being resold. The day-to-day management of the fleet would be delegated to groups of public or private operators within the local areas.

The initial investment to build the fleet would be financed by borrowing, with a maximum outstanding amount of €7.8 billion in 2035. Lease payments and resales, supplemented by €350 million in annual public support (equivalent to the current social leasing budget), would cover service costs and repay the debt within eighteen years. By then, the system would be self-financing, and the state would own one million cars worth approximately €10 billion.

“This model is much more beneficial for public finances,” explains Diane Strauss, director of T&E France. “It requires an initial funding effort, but it eventually pays for itself. Instead of subsidizing at a loss, we build up an asset that generates its own revenue. This is what makes the move to a million vehicles financially viable.”

In addition to providing long-term protection for low-income motorists against rising and volatile fuel prices, the proposal represents a genuine industrial planning policy. The announcement of a public order for two million vehicles over twenty years (including fleet renewal) would indeed give the French automotive sector the visibility necessary to invest and prepare for the future.

“This will also allow us to direct our production towards the vehicles that households truly need: affordable, fuel-efficient, repairable electric cars, produced in France and Europe,” concludes Léo Larivière, automotive transition expert at T&E France. “This proposal must now open a substantive discussion among all stakeholders. It is high time to prepare the next steps for one of the rare examples of policies that reconcile climate, social justice, and industrial planning.”

¹ Raphaël Glucksmann and Marine Tondelier aim for one million vehicles. Jean-Luc Mélenchon proposes a public fleet of 1.4 million cars by 2032, and François Ruffin raises the target to three million. Gabriel Attal and Dominique de Villepin also advocate expanding social leasing, without specifying a target.

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.
