#5828. Data center slowdown would raise risks for utility investments

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

An estimated 191 GW of planned data center capacity faces new risk after the midterm elections, with exposure concentrated in Texas, Ohio and Georgia, according to a report from Orennia.

Utilities could be left holding the bill for billions in infrastructure spending committed in anticipation of artificial intelligence data centers that are delayed, downsized or canceled, industry observers and experts told Utility Dive.

Opposition to data center construction has crossed traditional party lines, while Democratic gains in the midterms are adding pressure on earlier-stage investments across key markets. Together, these dynamics raise the stakes for project approvals and utility cost recovery, they say.

“From a financing perspective, prolonged uncertainty will increase the cost of capital, reduce available leverage and make investors more hesitant to commit significant development dollars before power is secured,” said Andrejka Bernatova, managing partner of investment firm Dynamix Capital Partners, which invests in energy infrastructure.

Even in regions that theoretically have enough capacity to accommodate new large loads, projects may struggle to secure a credible path to power on the timelines they need.

“Interconnection, permitting, and financing all matter, but they are part of the same execution question of whether the team can get reliable power to the site and actually deliver,” Bernatova said. “A customer commitment or a large announced capacity number does not answer that by itself.”

Ultimately, Bernatova expects investors to favor markets that offer certainty around power availability and timelines, potentially eroding the competitive advantage of markets like Texas, which until recently attracted substantial data center investment.

Over the summer, Gov. Greg Abbott, R, announced a pause on new data center interconnections pending an audit of the queue. In the midterms, he lost his bid for reelection to Democrat Gina Hinojosa, who has pledged to maintain pressure on the industry after taking office next year.

Nearly all of the 46 municipal ballot measures tracked by Bloomberg that addressed data center development resulted in restrictions, moratoriums or bans.

That unfavorability extends to a substantial share of the development pipeline. An estimated 191 GW of planned data center capacity faces new risk, with exposure concentrated in Texas, Ohio and Georgia, where incoming Democratic governors campaigned on pausing or restricting construction, according to a report from energy analytics firm Orennia that was shared with Utility Dive.

Texas accounts for nearly half of the pre-construction in development in the U.S., so a statewide approval freeze would have an outsized impact on the national buildout, but the “impact of delayed or unrealized data center build-out on power prices varies widely across technologies and markets,” Orennia said.

If 20% of projected data center demand in the Electric Reliability Council of Texas region fails to materialize, solar and wind generators could see their average wholesale revenue fall 38% and 37%, respectively, according to the report. Implied heat rates, a measure of the economics of gas-fired generation relative to fuel costs, could decline 39%. In the same scenario, two-hour battery storage arbitrage spreads could fall below new-build break-even requirements.

In PJM, delaying half of the planned data center buildout by two years could reduce four-hour battery storage arbitrage spreads by 16% and lower solar and wind capture prices and implied heat rates by about 12% through 2030, the modeling suggests.

The election results are unlikely to produce sweeping federal AI legislation but could lead to congressional hearings and greater transparency requirements, according to D.C.-based research and advisory firm Capstone. Its analysts expect much of the policy debate to take place at state utility commissions and local siting boards.

Despite a proliferation of large-load tariffs over the past year, safeguards may still leave customers exposed in the event of a slowdown, some say.

“Existing tariffs and regulatory frameworks likely won’t fully protect customers because they were designed for a different century,” Louisa Eberle, principal at the Regulatory Assistance Project, warned in a written statement.

Eberle also called for reforms to utility business practices “to turn this data center surge into updated infrastructure, cleaner energy and a better deal for average customers.”

The push for lower electricity bills is prompting policymakers to look more closely at how utilities use existing grid infrastructure.

Richard Caperton, vice president of policy at SPAN, a maker of smart electrical panels, anticipates greater emphasis on data center projects that can be served without major new grid investments.

“I’m really thrilled to see governors like Abigail Spanberger [in Virginia] campaign on affordability and then embrace utilization as the solution,” he said.

In April, Gov. Spanberger signed legislation requiring Dominion Energy and Appalachian Power to report how effectively they use existing grid infrastructure. The State Corporation Commission can use those metrics to establish improvement targets and evaluate utility spending.

“Winning candidates who emphasized lowering electricity prices are already looking for ways to deliver on their promises quickly,” Caperton said. “I expect that will lead to more policies and programs that boost grid utilization being ratified and implemented in early 2027. How those policies affect data center development and utility investments will vary by state.”

Stephen Rose, CEO of infrastructure software provider Render Networks, said he expects to see a “pause and necessary shakeout” in data center development.

“Fully permitted projects with real capital will keep moving,” Rose said. “The speculative end takes the hit: developers land-banking without firm power, and the contractors who staffed up for projects that won't break ground.”

Rose cited AEP Ohio's large-load tariff, approved in July 2025, which requires new data centers above 25 MW to pay for at least 85% of requested capacity for up to 12 years, with exit fees and financial guarantees. The utility’s data-center pipeline subsequently fell by more than half from more than 30 GW to about 13.

Rose expressed concern, however, that a broad slowdown wouldn’t affect hyperscalers as much as it would the businesses and people that build data centers. He recalled delays in Louisiana's Broadband Equity, Access and Deployment program in 2025, where service providers bore the costs of equipment and crews while waiting for approvals.

“When timelines stalled, the hit didn't land on big tech balance sheets. It landed on local drilling, civil and fiber contractors,” Rose said. “A data center slowdown hits the same people first, and Texas risks the same fallout.”

Permitting hurdles could make data center developers more selective about where they build and under what terms.

Andy Roehr, managing director in the tax advisory firm Baker Tilly’s energy and infrastructure practice, sees developers as more likely to redirect capital than abandon projects – though that could lead to a winnowing of queues in some jurisdictions.

“A developer is typically evaluating three, four or five sites at any given moment, so when permitting, regulation or local opposition makes one location difficult to read, the project is far more likely to be delayed or redirected than canceled,” Roehr said.

Roehr said states and municipalities competing for projects that can no longer move forward elsewhere could find themselves in a “bidding war.”

"The most interesting thing I am seeing is that these moratoria are creating a developer’s market since they have the assets, tax revenues and jobs that communities want or need,” he continued.

Some developers may shift to “less optimal sites where projects can get done at an effective price point and time-to-market, but the regulatory and legislative environment is less hostile,” he said.

Changes in expected demand could also lead to repricing across the supply chain, from gas turbines and processor racks to construction costs.

Another uncertainty is the possibility of federal permitting reform, which could make it easier to build certain kinds of energy projects.

The Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, which advanced in the Senate last week, faces a difficult path to passage following the midterm elections, analysts say.

Democrats are split over the proposal, with some expressing concerns over changes to environmental protections in the bill and urging party leaders to wait until next year when the Democrats will control both the House and Senate to pursue their priorities.

Roehr said in his view, the BAAJA takes an “all of the above” approach and would be as meaningful to solar as to nuclear or natural gas. But what the industry needs most, he said, is stability.

“Developers, investors and host communities need certainty to keep committing capital and local resources to these projects,” Roehr said. “These are 20-, 40-, even 60-year investments, and if the environment keeps whipsawing then jobs don’t appear, tax payments don’t appear and capital moves on — there will be no winners.”

#5824. Solar Insure Launches 20-Year Commercial Battery Warranty, Expanding Protection for Solar & Storage Projects

Launching at RE+ Las Vegas, November 17–20 | Visit Solar Insure at Booth C4190

Commercial property owners invest in solar and battery storage to manage energy costs and strengthen resilience. One question can shape that investment from the start. Who pays when equipment needs repairs years after installation?

Solar Insure today expanded its SI-Commercial Suite with two new warranties. SI-Commercial Battery is a 20-year limited warranty for commercial battery storage systems, and SI-Commercial Total covers solar and battery storage under one warranty. Together with the existing SI-Commercial Solar warranty, these additions allow Certified Providers to offer protection tailored to each project, whether it includes solar, battery storage, or both. Solar Insure protects more than 100,000 systems nationwide.

“Commercial projects are built for the long term, and their protection should reflect that,” said Ara Agopian, CEO of Solar Insure. “By expanding our commercial warranty suite, we’re helping solar companies offer protection that fits the systems their customers are investing in. It’s about giving property owners greater confidence not just in the installation, but in the years of ownership that follow.”

A failed inverter or declining battery capacity can lead to costs an owner did not budget for. Even when a manufacturer covers replacement equipment, the owner may still be responsible for labor and service costs. The expanded suite helps Certified Providers explain which repair expenses are covered so customers can evaluate their potential repair costs alongside system performance and installation price.

“Commercial battery owners are making a long-term investment, but some of the most significant expenses can emerge years after installation,” said Dean Chiaravallotti, Chief Revenue Officer at Solar Insure. “A battery replacement or unexpected repair can change the financial picture of a project. When providers can explain how they address those costs before the customer signs, they help buyers make a more informed investment decision. That’s the value we’re bringing to commercial storage.”

Solar companies must become Solar Insure Certified Providers to offer SI-Commercial warranties. Certification includes a review of the company’s qualifications, business practices, and commitment to customer service.

For qualifying claims, Solar Insure pays covered repair costs directly to the Certified Provider or service provider performing the work. If the original installer is no longer available, Solar Insure helps coordinate next steps, which may include identifying another qualified service provider. When a property changes hands, coverage transfers to the new owner at no cost.

“A lot can change over the life of a commercial energy system. Properties change hands, service providers change, and equipment eventually needs attention,” said Chiaravallotti. “Property owners need to know what happens w          hen those changes occur. Our goal is to give them a clearer path forward, with protection and support that extend well beyond the original installation.”

Solar companies can contact Solar Insure to learn about becoming a Certified Provider. Commercial property owners can speak with a Solar Insure Certified Provider about which warranty fits their project.

Solar Insure is a leading provider of solar and storage warranties, focused on protecting long-term system performance and elevating accountability across the renewable energy industry. Through rigorous provider certification and governance, Solar Insure delivers durable protection that residential and commercial installers can stand behind.

#5819. SolarEdge unveils U.S.-made optimizer for C&I solar

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 17, 2026

SolarEdge’s new Duo Power Optimizer promises to simplify commercial and industrial solar installations with 50% fewer optimizer units, independent module-level power tracking, and enhanced safety features.

The commercial and industrial (C&I) solar market is getting a new hardware option aimed at making installations simpler without sacrificing system performance. SolarEdge Technologies has unveiled its new Duo Power Optimizer, a U.S.-manufactured solution designed to deliver module-level energy harvesting and monitoring while cutting the number of power optimizers in half.

Announced November 16 ahead of RE+ 2026 in Las Vegas, the new product introduces a 2:1 architecture that allows one optimizer to connect two solar modules. Notably, each module retains its own independent maximum power point tracker (MPPT), which preserves the module-level performance benefits associated with the traditional one-to-one optimizer configurations.

For developers, EPCs and installers managing large-scale rooftop and distributed generation projects, the value here lies in fewer components to purchase, wire and install, with no trade-off in module-level optimization.

Simplifying installation without sacrificing performance

SolarEdge’s optimization tech addresses the challenge of conventional string inverter systems, which is that an underperforming module can affect the output of an entire string. But by allowing each module to operate independently, the system limits the impact of shading, degradation or performance differences between panels.

The Duo brings demonstrates that capability in a two-module configuration. By reducing the number of optimizers by 50% compared with one-to-one designs, SolarEdge is targeting an opportunity to streamline balance-of-system requirements and reduce the installation labor.

SolarEdge’s U.S.-made Duo Power Optimizer connects two solar modules while maintaining independent module-level power tracking for commercial and industrial solar installations. Credit: SolarEdge

The hardware also incorporates design changes intended to improve jobsite efficiency. Each optimizer features two input pairs: a shorter connection for the nearest module and a pre-extended connection for the adjacent module. The arrangement reduces excess wiring and eliminates the need for field-made jumpers.

The configuration also accommodates modules with different orientations, making it particularly relevant for east-west and dual-tilt racking applications.

Beyond installation efficiency, SolarEdge is emphasizing electrical safety as a central feature of the new product.

The Duo extends the company’s Sense Connect technology to its input and output connectors, monitoring connector temperatures to identify developing issues before they potentially escalate into overheating or electrical arcs.

Also, the optimizer introduces what SolarEdge describes as an industry-first approach to arc fault detection: every optimizer acts as an individual arc sensor. Rather than relying exclusively on detection at the inverter, the system distributes monitoring throughout the array, allowing potential arc faults to be identified closer to their source.

SolarEdge says the technology provides coverage across strings with up to 3,280 feet of total cable length between the inverter and optimizer. It can also help pinpoint problems to the optimizer area, potentially making troubleshooting and maintenance more efficient.

Manufactured at SolarEdge’s Florida facility, the Duo is designed to meet U.S. Domestic Content and Foreign Entity of Concern (FEOC) requirements, according to the company. SolarEdge says its domestic-content eligibility levels are consistent with those of its previous U.S.-manufactured power optimizers, subject to applicable tax guidance and project-specific requirements.

For SolarEdge, the Duo represents an effort to combine three priorities that are becoming more and more important in commercial solar: installation efficiency, system-level safety, and domestic manufacturing.

The technology does not fundamentally change the value proposition of module-level optimization. Instead, it looks to make that architecture more economical and easier to deploy at scale.

SolarEdge will showcase the Duo Power Optimizer at RE+ 2026.

#5818. CA Governor Talks Climate Action And Punches Nazis On European Tour

Tesla CEO and former California resident Elon Musk has been taking quite a bit of heat over his Nazi-adjacent rhetoric, compounded in recent weeks as reports continue to surface that he has manipulated Starlink military satellite service to Ukraine again, enabling Russian President Vladimir Putin to extend his war of aggression into other states of the former USSR. Why? Who knows! Regardless, another powerful California resident has been gearing up to push back against both Elon Musk and Vladimir Putin…

…If you guessed that someone is California Governor Gavin Newsom, run right out and buy yourself a cigar. California would be the fourth-largest single economy in the world if it was a nation unto itself. It offers living proof that decarbonization is a driver of economic growth, not a drag, and Newsom is happy to bring that achievement to the attention of the world.

Newsom has been eyeballed as a contender for the Democratic nomination in the 2028 Presidential election cycle. If so, he is off to a roaring start. Gavin chose the COP 31 climate talks in Türkiye to raise his profile on the international stage, while throwing down the renewable energy gauntlet against Putin and also taking jabs at Elon Musk.

Ukraine has considerable fossil energy resources that Putin can fold into the Russian economy (and his allies’ pockets), which explains why Putin launched an attempt to take Ukraine by storm in  February of 2022. Ukraine also has considerable renewable energy resources that it can bring to bear as an aspirational member of the European Union. With that in mind, it’s no surprise to see longtime Putin ally and fossil energy fan Donald Trump, and America’s shadow President Elon Musk, align with Russia against the interests of Ukraine.

It’s not just Ukraine. All of the former Soviet Union states are at risk, including members of NATO, the North Atlantic Treaty Organization. I visited NATO member and former satellite state Estonia on a clean tech tour after Putin invaded Ukraine, to get an up-close look at the tiny but innovative nation’s outsized influence on the renewable energy transition in Europe and beyond (here’s another example). Humming in the background of a round of site visits and presentations was an ongoing conversation about Estonia’s strategy to fend off Russia.

One manifestation of the defensive posture came this week, when Estonia announced the closing of border crossings, effective December 1, after accusing Russia of orchestrating an arson attack on one of its defense firms. The independent Ukrainian news organization Hromadske is also among those reporting that Estonia has been moving government services into remote digital platforms, safe from Russian attacks on data centers and other critical infrastructure.

Now let’s take a look at Governor Newsom’s all-but-in-name campaign launch this week, where a stopover in Poland illustrates how he intends to conflate anti-fascism with the renewable energy transition over the next two years — in other words, saving the planet and punching Nazis, too.

Considering that Elon Musk has spent a considerable sum of money to vault President Trump into the White House for a second term in office while also stirring up Nazi-adjacent sentiment in Europe and elsewhere through his “X” social media platform, Newsom’s highly public visit to the POLIN Museum of the History of Polish Jews in Warsaw was particularly ripe with symbolism. Among other historical exhibits, the museum commemorates the the Warsaw Uprising of 1943, known as the first — and for a long time the only — major urban battle against Nazi occupation during World War II.

After laying a ceremonial wreath at the Monument to the Ghetto Heroes at the museum, Newsom deployed his @CAgovernor account on X to underscore what appears to be the unifying theme of his campaign, calling the museum a “powerful reminder of fighting against the darkest forces.”

“The museum is a powerful reminder that we have to remain vigilant to protect what we value even in the darkest of times,” Newsom also elaborated in a press statement.

Newsom also used the occasion of the visit to sign a new climate partnership with Poland. The Memorandum of Understanding covers pollution reduction, energy efficiency, grid modernization, energy storage, and climate resiliency as well as renewable energy.

“Energy security, economic strength, and a livable climate are not competing goals. They are one mission, and no nation or state can achieve it alone. California and Poland come from different histories but share the same responsibility: to protect our people, power our economies, and leave future generations a safer world. This partnership puts that responsibility to work,” Newsom emphasized in a public statement that supports the running theme of fighting fascism with decarbonization.

Poland already has a running head start on renewable energy through its Baltic Sea offshore wind resources. Other clean tech sectors are on the move as well. To kickstart the MOU, Newsom met with the leading Polish battery firm Impact Clean Power Technology. “Impact designs and builds batteries for electric and hydrogen buses, trucks, rail and industrial vehicles, as well as for mobile robotics, with a particular focus on humanoid robots,” Newsom’s office explains.

“This place is all about entrepreneurial innovation, and it’s about imagination, the secret sauce,” Newsom adds, referring to Impact’s rapidly expanding plans for the future.

Poland was also the scene of the America-Ukraine Clean Energy Forum in Warsaw, providing another opportunity for Newsom to draw the connection between anti-fascism and the renewable energy transition. Referring to the new MOU, Newsom told participants at the form that the energy transition represents an “opportunity to think anew and act anew.”

“It’s the opportunity to begin with a different mindset. It’s an opportunity to be more resilient in every way, shape or form. That’s why clean energy should be front and center, self-evident. It’s a strategic imperative. Period. Full stop,” Newsom emphasized (emphasis added).

In a press release recapping the event, Newsom’s office also noted that the Governor told his audience that American voters “spoke loudly in the midterm elections,” in a reaction to “the Trump administration’s rampant corruption and embrace of Big Oil.”

“The subtext, he said, was that clean energy was subconsciously on the ballot, too,” the press release continued.

Newsom was not done yet. On November 16, he announced a new contribution of medical aid to Ukraine from California, including a new field hospital, more than 1,300 additional hospital beds, and millions of bandages among other supplies.

He also toured Ukraine in person, an extended trip that underscored the role of innovation and technology in Ukraine’s ability to withstand Russian attacks, despite efforts by Donald Trump and Elon Musk to undermine its defense.

In response to Musk’s repeated manipulation of SpaceX satellite services, Ukraine has already developed a workaround in the form of its own drone-based guidance systems, as capably demonstrated just yesterday by the destruction of a key Russian military asset, the Crimea Bridge.

Thoughts? Before you drop a note in the discussion thread, note that Musk’s SpaceX venture has emerged as a new, leading consumer of natural gas in the US. The startup will need much more of the same to make good on its plans for launching 30 rockets per day from its new “Starbase” in Louisiana. So much for saving the planet…

#5817. US to install 36% more wind turbines in 2025 than previous year

The United States is expected to add more than 7 GW of wind installations in 2025, a 36% increase over the previous year, and the five-year outlook remains unchanged quarter-over-quarter from previous forecast, according to the “U.S. Wind Energy Monitor” report released by Wood Mackenzie and the American Clean Power Association (ACP).

Amid a challenging market environment, the report shows that the United States is on track to add 46 GW of new wind capacity from 2025 to 2029, with total projected volumes unchanged quarter-on-quarter from previous forecasts. However, timing has shifted, as 2026 and 2027 will deliver significant gains, at 10.7 GW and 12.7 GW, respectively, as more assets advance through the development pipeline.

While Q3 installations came in 23% below forecast at 932 MW, the market has shown progress with 3.8 GW queued for Q4 2025 — representing 52% of the year’s total expected capacity. This back-loaded installation pattern is consistent with typical project-commissioning timelines.

U.S. turbine order intake has rebounded to pre-One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) levels quarter-over-quarter, supported by 2+ GW of firm commitments in Q3, the biggest intake in the region in the last nine months and a 79% quarter-over-quarter increase. However, true visibility remains limited as OEMs increasingly withhold project details and much qualifying “start-of-construction” activity occurs through off-site component manufacturing.

The market will then see a noticeable drop off, as 2029 weakens meaningfully quarter-over-quarter following project cancellations and inactive designations for late-decade capacity, driven by permitting and broader development challenges.

“The U.S. power market is facing mounting strain after a decade of flat demand, with utilities committing to 160 GW of large-load additions. This represents a significant opportunity for wind energy, which benefits from strengthened economic fundamentals and a compelling business case driven by its competitively low LCOE,” said Leila Garcia da Fonseca, director of research at Wood Mackenzie. “However, turbine costs remain elevated due to tariffs and mid-term wind growth will depend on resolving permitting and policy uncertainty.”

Power demand growth through 2029 is expected to average around 3% compared to just 0.7% over the previous decade, with data centers accounting for approximately 59 GW of the 90 GW total peak demand growth. This surge in baseload demand positions wind as a natural fit to meet rising power needs.

The five-year capacity outlook remains unchanged at 39.8 GW of added capacity quarter-over-quarter. The 2025-2027 pipeline is fully committed with all projects having turbine orders in place. More than 60% of the three-year capacity outlook has been commissioned or is under construction.

Activity is led by western states, such as Wyoming, New Mexico and others, that will account for 34% of activity in this time period. Major projects driving the outlook include Pattern’s 3.5-GW SunZia project in New Mexico, which will position the developer as 2026’s top installer, and Invenergy’s 998-MW Towner Energy Center in Colorado, the largest single project expected in 2027.

The market continues expanding geographically, with Arkansas bringing its first utility-scale onshore wind project online through Cordelio’s Crossover Wind.

The repowering market remains strong, as Wood Mackenzie projects that 18 projects will drive 2.5 GW of capacity additions in the next three years.

Wood Mackenzie expects the offshore installation pace to slow in Q4 2025 due to harsh winter weather conditions, pushing remaining capacity into 2026. Despite these near-term adjustments, Vineyard Wind has demonstrated strong execution, connecting 15 turbines in Q3 and delivering 200 GWh through nine months.

“U.S. offshore wind shows diverging momentum,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Projects under construction with COD estimated for 2026 continue to hit key milestones, but post-2027 developments face potential delays amid constrained wind turbine installation vessel capacity, driving delays and contract terminations.”

The offshore sector is also experiencing significant financial pressure.

The report highlights that uncertainty around tariffs is a threat to projections. Wood Mackenzie projects that tariffs will drive up turbine costs in 2026, before moderating in subsequent years. In total, U.S. onshore wind capex is projected to increase by 5% through 2029.

“U.S. wind turbine pricing is experiencing unprecedented uncertainty as conflicting market and regulatory forces interact,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Domestic manufacturing overcapacity relative to permitted project volumes, particularly after 2028, would normally place downward pressure on prices. Despite this, onshore wind costs are expected to continue rising due to tariff exposure on raw material inputs and subcomponents.”

#5816. Europe’s Inland Barges Need a Cargo Plan Before an Electric Fleet Plan

Coal throughput at Rotterdam rose 15.3% in the first nine months of 2026. Crude oil and petroleum-product volumes also increased. An inland vessel operator looking only at this year’s cargo market could see decades of work ahead. But an engine, battery conversion or replacement vessel bought now may still be operating in 2050, when the mix of cargo travelling through European ports and rivers will be different.

Rotterdam’s latest throughput release explains the coal rebound through recovering coking-coal movements, expensive natural gas and power-market conditions. Meanwhile, Eurostat reports that EU inland-waterway freight fell 3% in 2025 to 118 billion tonne-kilometres. Coal and crude-petroleum freight rose that year, while agricultural freight contracted. These changes are reshaping the services that ports, vessel owners and customers need to buy.

The Central Commission for the Navigation of the Rhine counted 6,224 dry-cargo vessels in Rhine countries in 2025, down from 6,410 in 2023. Smaller vessels account for much of the decline. Tankers moved the other way, rising from 1,400 in 2022 to 1,477 in 2025. The numbers describe a fleet already adapting to changing cargo and operating economics.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing on the inland barges Europe will need in 2050 uses a cargo-first model to work from the freight task to the future electric fleet. The underlying 98-page Inland Freight Electrification report is available to read.

A vessel owner has two connected capital decisions. The first is whether the freight service will still have customers through the useful life of the asset. The second is which propulsion and charging system can perform that service economically. Starting with equipment and then finding a market for it risks spending scarce investment capital on the wrong vessels.

I modelled a large European industrial port corridor from a 2025-style baseline to 2050. The central scenario assumes bulk cargo falls from 57.8 million tonnes to 34.4 million tonnes, a decline of about 40%. Wet bulk accounts for much of the loss, falling from 34.5 million to 15.0 million tonnes. Dry bulk declines from 23.3 million to 19.4 million tonnes. The model is a transparent scenario for investment decisions across this type of corridor, rather than a forecast of Rotterdam’s exact future cargo volumes.

The largest change is fossil-linked cargo. Fossil fuels and refinery streams fall from 19.0 million tonnes to 1.5 million tonnes, while coal and other solid fossil fuels fall from 2.5 million tonnes to zero. Combined, those reductions account for about 20 million tonnes of the scenario’s 23.4-million-tonne net decline. Any operator whose principal customers depend on moving coal or petroleum faces a different replacement decision from one carrying grain, construction minerals, chemicals or scrap.

Steel illustrates what cargo substitution means. The model reduces imported iron ore and other virgin iron units from 4.5 million tonnes to 3.0 million tonnes, while scrap and circular feedstocks rise from 1.8 million to 3.5 million tonnes. The industrial function continues as the materials moving to and from factories change. Existing barges and terminals may find new work, although parcel sizes, loading systems and routes will also change.

Biofuel feedstocks create a second trap. In the central scenario those movements peak near 5 million tonnes in 2030 before falling to 1.5 million tonnes in 2050. A temporary freight boom can support profitable operations without supporting a new vessel or terminal investment with a thirty-year life. The durability of the contract matters as much as the volume carried this year.

Cargo tonnage alone cannot size an electric fleet. The model translates commodities into powered vessel-equivalents using parcel sizes, routes, loaded calls and productivity. It starts with around 1,150 powered vessel-equivalents and ends with 664, a reduction of 42%. Dry cargo falls 17% in tonnes but 43% in powered vessel-equivalents because the modelled freight service changes. The starting 1,150 powered equivalents represent about 1,234 physical hulls after counting unpowered barges moved by pushers. Battery conversion decisions concern powered units; terminal and cargo-handling decisions also concern the additional hulls.

That distinction is particularly important for pushed formations. A pusher provides propulsion for several cargo barges. Converting one powered unit does not necessarily mean replacing every physical hull. Counting all hulls as propulsion conversions would inflate the equipment and financing requirement.

Low Rhine water levels bring a different constraint into the same calculation. A barge can remain on the register yet carry less freight per journey when draught is restricted. Cargo owners may have to buy more voyages, use different hulls or shift some freight to rail and road. Route depth, seasonal load factors and terminal access belong in the service model alongside cargo demand.

Ports should begin with a living cargo and route register showing which freight is declining, which remains, and which changes its material inputs. Vessel owners can connect that register to remaining hull life, annual utilization, cargo contracts and the cost of electric conversion. Utilities and terminal operators can then size grid connections, charging facilities and battery-container pools against vessels that will run on bankable routes. Infrastructure developed around dependable freight customers has a stronger prospect of earning a return.

Europe has good reasons to electrify established, high-utilization inland freight now. Battery-powered vessels are already serving container and bulk routes. The commercial opportunity is to invest in routes with surviving customers and electrify the services they will continue to buy. Expanding an electric fleet before measuring that freight task puts the order of decisions backward.

The complete TFIE Strategy Briefing works through the cargo substitutions, fleet denominator and investment decisions. The open Inland Freight Electrification report includes route-energy modelling, battery-pool sizing, terminal requirements and programme economics. TFIE Strategy also undertakes scoped corridor and fleet assessments for ports, operators and cargo owners.

#5815. DOE-funded transmission projects could boost East-West transfer capacity 440%

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The latest funding announcement is for a 1.8-GW, high-voltage, direct current transmission line in Wyoming, an area rich in wind resources and slated for major grid expansion projects.

The U.S. Department of Energy has issued a $2.8 billion conditional loan commitment to finance a 1.8-GW, high-voltage, direct current transmission line in Wyoming being developed by Grid United — the latest in a series of funding decisions that could add about 7 GW of transfer capacity between the Western and Eastern interconnections.

In 2021, the DOE said a collection of back-to-back converter stations enabled just 1,320 MW of electricity to flow between the two networks. Increasing transfer capacity could save money and boost grid reliability and resilience, it says.

The Wyoming Intertie project would run about 106 miles between a converter station in Wyoming’s Platte County and an existing substation in Carbon County, linking the Southwest Power Pool and the Western Electricity Coordinating Council. The area around the project is rich in wind resources.

Also, that part of Wyoming “sits at the confluence of major grid expansion projects in WECC and SPP, which makes it a particularly advantageous location for a grid-to-grid connection,” Ashley Kang, a Grid United spokesperson, said in an email.

Grid United expects to complete the permitting for the project in 2029 and begin construction in 2030. The company plans to bring the project online in 2032 or 2033, depending on its procurement of needed HVDC equipment, according to a company fact sheet.

The Houston-based transmission company is developing three other projects that would also add lines between the Eastern and Western interconnections. They include the 3-GW North Plains Connector project between North Dakota and Montana; the 1.8-GW Three Corners Connector between Oklahoma and Colorado; and the 1.5-GW Pecos West project between SPP’s footprint in Texas and WECC in New Mexico.

In separate funding decisions, DOE has recently agreed to help finance the Three Corners and North Plains connector projects. Taken together, the department has agreed to help fund about 6.6 GW of East-West transmission infrastructure by Grid United.

DOE has also agreed to help fund a 490-MW expansion project between Colorado and Texas. The project, planned by Xcel Energy, could be expanded to a total of 2 GW.

The DOE's draft 2026 National Transmission Needs Study found that average price differentials along the seam between SPP's eastern footprint and the Western Interconnection ranged from $18/MWh to $33/MWh from 2012 to 2023.

The Wyoming Intertie project could produce $39 billion in cost savings for ratepayers, help meet growing demand and improve grid resilience during periods of high electric use and extreme weather, DOE said Tuesday. Closing conditions on the loan include Grid United obtaining full project right-of-way, contracts for capacity on the line, and federal, state and local permits.

The conditional, 30-year DOE loan would finance 80% of the project’s construction costs, reducing interest expenses by about $500 million compared with typical financing sources, according to Grid United.

The low-cost, federal loan can be assigned to utilities that may buy capacity on the transmission line, translating into savings for ratepayers, Grid United said.

Increasing transfer capacity between the Eastern and Western interconnections could reduce wholesale power costs, SPP said in August when its board approved a plan to study potential transmission additions between the regions.

#5814. ACC Approves Gas Plants, Rate Hike, and Allows Annual Rate Increase

Phoenix – Yesterday, the Arizona Corporation Commission voted to approve Salt River Project’s (SRP) proposed Marigold gas plant in Pinal County, and also approved Tucson Electric Power’s (TEP) proposed rate increase, as well as its proposed Annual Rate Adjustment Mechanism (ARAM) that would allow the utility to increase its rates every year with very little oversight. Yet again, the Commission’s decisions are at odds with the best interests of ratepayers, issuing rulings that collectively worsen air quality, raise costs, and limit transparency.

SRP’s gas-fired Marigold power plant is a part of the larger Marigold Energy Center, which would include solar and battery storage. But the 675-megawatt gas plant exposes customers to volatile fuel prices while worsening air quality in a county that already experiences some of the state’s most degraded air, and fails to meet federal air quality standards. The Commission did not adequately analyze the plant’s environmental impacts before approving SRP’s Certificate of Environmental Compatibility (CEC) application and approved it without any opportunity for public comment before the Commission.

The ACC also approved a significant rate increase for TEP, approving a 10% surge that customers will see reflected in utility bills starting in December. The utility had initially proposed a 14% increase. The decision comes as TEP’s parent company, Fortis, netted more than $1.7 billion in 2025 across its portfolio of utilities. The Commission also approved the utility’s adoption of “formula rates,” enabling TEP to fast-track rate increases every year without a public rate case, with limited oversight by the Commission and limited opportunities for public participation. However, Commissioner Lea Márquez-Peterson filed a proposed amendment in the case to deny approval of TEP’s formula rate mechanism.

In September, Sierra Club published a report documenting various decisions the Commission has leveraged against the best interests of ratepayers despite the fact that Arizonans experience some of the highest electric bills in the country, with some of the most planned gas in the country.

“Today’s decisions continue a pattern Arizonans have become all too familiar with,” said Sierra Club Grand Canyon Chapter Director Sandy Bahr. “The ACC has shown us time and again that it will continue to prioritize utility profits and polluters over the community it is supposed to protect. Rising energy costs, climate, and clean air are top of mind for Arizonans. The ACC clearly isn’t considering this as it continues to approve rate hikes and gas plants that pollute the air, and threaten groundwater.”

#5813. How National Grid, Eversource are using VPPs to solve local-level constraints

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Northeast’s virtual power plant foundations are stronger today than they were a decade ago, but executives say utilities still need more visibility into distribution-system loads and resources.

National Grid and Eversource have both built robust, multi-device DER portfolios spanning multiple customer classes, Caldwell and Eversource vice president Tilak Subrahmanian said.

Eversource’s portfolio includes about 100,000 thermostats and 25,000 commercial and industrial customers across a territory that includes Greater Boston, ISO-New England’s biggest load zone, Subrahmanian said.

National Grid has about 100 MW of DER load in New England and more than 350 MW in upstate New York, respectively representing about 2% and 5% of regional system peak, Caldwell said. National Grid’s residential portfolio is mostly thermostats, but stationary storage and managed electric vehicle charging are increasingly meaningful, he added.

Eversource feels fortunate to operate in a region where state regulators provide time, funding and encouragement for utilities to “test hypotheses” about alternatives to traditional poles-and-wires investments, Subrahmanian said. That allows Eversource to explore use cases where distributed resources offer economic and system benefits over more capital-intensive infrastructure projects — which isn’t always the case, he added.

“It’s a learning process … [with] behind-the-meter devices, we have to be very specific about what problem we’re trying to solve,” he said. “We are cautiously optimistic, but a lot of it needs to play out still.”

National Grid and Eversource both participate in ConnectedSolutions+, a souped-up version of the long-running New England VPP program that offers higher incentives for distributed resource hosts on constrained distribution feeders.

Eversource announced two CS+ pilots earlier this year: one focused on areas served by three substations in Greater Boston and another focused on a single substation in southeastern Massachusetts. The Boston-area program aims to shift load away from demand peaks, while the southeastern Massachusetts program is intended in part to soak up excess distributed solar generation during the middle of the day.

At least one provider of low-cost home batteries, Haven Energy, is targeting customers of both utilities with deeply discounted energy storage subscriptions in areas where batteries deliver clear grid or resilience benefits. Earlier this month, National Grid said it would partner with Haven to provide up to 125 income-qualified households with no-cost battery systems as part of a pilot program it calls BackupBattery+.

“The value of a battery to the grid can be fundamentally different even one mile apart,” Haven Energy CEO Vinnie Campo told Utility Dive in August.

With increased DER adoption and regulation allowing for flexible cost recovery, the Northeast’s VPP foundations are far stronger today than a decade ago, when Caldwell was new to National Grid, he said.

Back then, “nothing ever really penciled, [it] was really hard to do … now, nearly a decade later, everything seems to be clicking. [There is] a sea change in how utilities see these things as practical tools for solving challenges,” Caldwell said.

More capable DER orchestration software is also unlocking potential, Diamond said. Traditional demand response events typically begin “firm” and then lose effectiveness as customers drop out, she explained. But this summer, across 70 dynamic load-shaping events for 12 different utilities, EnergyHub was “able to deliver consistent load shape” with up to 90% consistency for the entire duration, she said.

“[That] is huge for utilities, especially if you are trying to hit a peak hour — it’s really hard to do that if you don't have that consistency,” Diamond said.

Eversource is eager to gain more visibility into the position, sizing and behavior of loads and distributed energy resources on its system so that it can dial in location-specific solutions, Subrahmanian said. It only takes a few unmanaged EVs to overload the small, poletop transformers that remain common in parts of Eversource’s territory — infrastructure whose inevitable replacement can perhaps be deferred by a targeted managed charging program, he said.

Right now, Eversource can’t even confidently say which of its customers have air conditioning, but its ongoing advanced metering infrastructure rollout should improve the situation soon, he added.

“We will be in a much better position in a year,” Subrahmanian said.

#5812. Louisiana Communities Rally Against Meta Gas Expansion

BATON ROUGE, Louisiana — Today, weeks after a national election revealed a country fed up with high prices and data centers, Louisiana residents and organizations rallied in downtown Baton Rouge against gas plants for a massive Meta data center. Marching and rallying with signs and megaphones, the group urged the Louisiana Public Service Commission (PSC) to reject Entergy’s proposal to build seven expensive, polluting gas plants for Meta’s Hyperion campus.

Entergy wants PSC approval to build seven additional gas plants for the Meta data center, which will need more energy than a city four times as large as New Orleans. The utility doesn’t want to implement the common-sense customer protections requested by numerous groups intervening in the case. Sierra Club has been urging rejection of this proposal for months because it presents a “substantial cost” that Entergy’s existing customers may be required to bear.

Due to Gov. Jeff Landry’s “lighting speed” initiative and a previous decision by a majority of PSC commissioners, the agency is rushing this proposal through and planning to provide only one opportunity for the public to speak out against this proposal: a December meeting when commissioners are scheduled to also vote on the proposal minutes later. Much of the analysis Entergy has submitted to the PSC has been shielded from public view by NDA restrictions.

In response to the PSC’s upcoming decision, Louisiana residents and Sierra Club staff, issued the following statements:

“It’s clear by now that Meta and Entergy don’t care much for normal Louisianans, but the PSC has a mandate to do just that. Instead they have denied  us a meaningful opportunity to speak on a project that will impact our state for generations,” said Margie Vicknair-Pray, project coordinator for the Sierra Club and a Louisiana resident. “Commissioners must know that the people will not sacrifice Louisiana for one big, risky, expensive, polluting experiment. They must vote no on this outrageous corporate favor.”

“Any claims that Meta is fully paying its own way are false,” said Darryl Malek-Wiley, a senior field representative for Sierra Club and a Louisiana resident. “These seven gas plants will guzzle our water, pollute our air, – and customers will be on the hook to pay if Meta walks away. We’re here to tell commissioners that we are done footing the bill for big tech – especially considering the extreme lack of transparency surrounding this project.”

In its testimony to the PSC urging rejection of Entergy’s proposal, Sierra Club called for:

In negotiations, Entergy showed little willingness to consider or agree to any of these stipulations.

Separately, in Meta’s application to the LDEQ, it wants to modify its existing permit to add 44 fossil-fuel water boilers that will operate and emit pollutants around the clock. Because this would send total pollution for the permit beyond the minor-source permit limits, Meta said that – instead of applying for a major source permit as required by the Clean Air Act – it simply wouldn’t count emissions from the previously approved 31 diesel generators when those are operating during emergency hours.

Sierra Club has urged LDEQ that it must consider emissions from those diesel generators running during emergency operations.

#5811. NextEra-Dominion merger hearings kick off with focus on scope

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The case is “simply not the one to litigate the cost of service [or] who’s paying their fair share ... including the data centers,” said Joe Reid, an attorney representing NextEra and Dominion.

The proposed merger between Dominion and NextEra, announced in May, would create the largest regulated electric utility in the world, with a 130-GW large load pipeline. Clean energy groups, local Virginia officials and leaders of the state’s executive branch have raised concerns about the potential impact of the deal on rates and data center development.

McGuire Woods partner Joe Reid, representing the joint petitioners NextEra and Dominion, said the case “is simply not the one to litigate the cost of service, and nor is it the case to litigate who's paying their fair share and ensuring everybody pays their fair share, including the data centers.”

Reid noted that the proposed acquisition is a “consequential combination, but its size does not change the requirements of the Transfers Act or the analysis required to determine whether the statute's requirements have been met.”

SCC Chair Kelsey Bagot asked Reid if the commission “should not consider longer-term benefits in evaluating this proposed transaction,” and Reid replied that the commission “should consider all the evidence that is in front of it.”

Nate Benforado, a senior attorney with the Southern Environmental Law Center, argued that the Transfers Act is less narrow than the joint petitioners claimed.

“The analysis that the Utility Transfers Act requires is broad,” Benforado said. “Petitioners must prove that this transaction will not impair or jeopardize adequate service at just and reasonable rates … This is not simply a ‘do no harm standard’. This looks at virtually everything a utility does.”

Will Reisinger, representing the Piedmont Environmental Council, pointed out that the statute provides that “before approving this merger, the commission ‘shall be satisfied’ that adequate service to the public at just and reasonable rates will not be impaired or jeopardized.”

“That standard, satisfied, is subjective,” he said. “This is not a matter of calling a ball or a strike. This decision is 100% within your judgment, within your discretion.”

Will Cleveland, the attorney representing Google, said that the company plans to present testimony from energy economist Carolyn Berry, “who will testify that the minimum demand charge approved as part of Dominion's 2025 biennial rate case discourages large loads like data centers from taking advantage of their statutory right to retail choice.” Dominion has said “they don't think this testimony is relevant to the case,” Cleveland said, but Google disagrees.

Amazon’s attorney, Brian Greene, said that that company would want to see Dominion address two issues if the commission were to approve the transaction: “One is reforming the large load interconnection queue, and second is addressing the need for new capacity through bring-your-own options,” he said.

Greene said that Amazon proposes that “if the transaction is approved, Dominion be required to reform its study process through a cluster study approach that evaluates projects together and looks for coordinated system-wide solutions … Approving the merger without reforming the interconnection queue would be like putting more power behind the same undersized wire.”

#5810. Mission Solar Energy puts supply chain transparency in the spotlight at RE+ 2026

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 18, 2026

With new high-efficiency residential and commercial modules and an integrated supply chain spanning polysilicon to power conversion, Mission Solar Energy is making a case for U.S. manufacturing, domestic content and FEOC-compliant sourcing.

As the solar industry continues to navigate shifting trade policies, domestic content incentives, and supply chain scrutiny, Mission Solar Energy is bringing a message of manufacturing strength and transparency to RE+ 2026. The San Antonio-based manufacturer showcased its latest commercial and residential modules while highlighting the advantages of its relationship with fellow OCI Holdings subsidiaries OCI TerraSus and OCI Power.

The announcement puts the spotlight on a growing priority for solar developers, installers and distributors: knowing exactly where components originate and having the documentation to prove it.

The three companies collectively cover significant portions of the solar value chain. OCI TerraSus produces high-purity, solar-grade polysilicon in Sarawak, Malaysia; Mission Solar designs, engineers, and assembles modules in San Antonio, Texas; and OCI Power manufactures inverters and power conversion systems in South Korea.

According to Mission Solar, all three companies are compliant with Foreign Entity of Concern (FEOC) requirements and are not classified as prohibited foreign entities. The integrated structure offers customers access to a traceable supply chain backed by OCI Holdings, a global clean energy group headquartered in Seoul.

For an industry that’s becoming more and more focused on sourcing eligibility and domestic manufacturing credentials, that positioning could prove commercially important.

On the product front, Mission Solar is highlighting two modules aimed at different market segments.

For commercial and industrial (C&I) applications, the company is featuring its 595W N-Type Bifacial 144HC module. The dual-glass design captures sunlight from both sides, potentially increasing energy production without requiring additional project footprint. Designed and assembled in the United States, the module carries 11.5% domestic content, meets FEOC requirements and supports front-side loads of up to 5,400 Pa.

Residential installers will get a look at the 440W N-Type 108HC rooftop module, which Mission Solar says will be the first of its kind on the market with 12% domestic content. Building on an existing 6% domestic-content version, the module combines N-type cell technology and a multi-busbar design to deliver efficiency of up to 23%. It is also approved on residential financing platforms including LightReach and GoodLeap.

Looking further ahead, Mission Solar plans to preview two next-generation G12R modules, with commercial launches anticipated in late 2027. The company will also display a lower-domestic-content U.S.-produced C&I module option expected in 2027.

Although module performance is still critical to the industry, supply chain credibility is increasingly part of the overall purchasing decision.

As developers evaluate project economics, tax credit eligibility and sourcing risk, manufacturers that can offer transparent documentation alongside competitive technology may have an advantage.

#5809. Trump admin ‘pauses’ leases for five offshore wind projects already under construction

The U.S. Dept. of the Interior has immediately paused the leases for all large-scale offshore wind projects under construction in the United States due to “national security risks identified by the Dept. of War in recently completed classified reports.”

“The prime duty of the United States government is to protect the American people,” said Secretary of the Interior Doug Burgum. “Today’s action addresses emerging national security risks, including the rapid evolution of the relevant adversary technologies, and the vulnerabilities created by large-scale offshore wind projects with proximity near our East Coast population centers. The Trump administration will always prioritize the security of the American people.”

The Dept. of the Interior claims that the blade movement and reflective towers create “radar interference called ‘clutter.'” Allegedly, this is a national security risk.

Dominion Energy released the following statement in response to the pause issued for the Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind project:

“The Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind Project (CVOW) is essential for American national security and meeting Virginia’s dramatically growing energy needs, the fastest growth in America. This growth is driven by the need to provide reliable power to many of America’s most important war fighting installations, the world’s largest warship manufacturer, and the largest concentration of data centers on the planet as well as the leading edge of the AI revolution.

Stopping CVOW for any length of time will threaten grid reliability for some of the nation’s most important war fighting, AI and civilian assets. It will also lead to energy inflation and threaten thousands of jobs.

CVOW is American-owned and benefits all of our Virginia customers. Our customers are paying for the project after a careful review of project costs and benefits by Virginia state regulators in 2022. These same state regulators, along with numerous federal agencies, oversee our cyber and physical security program, which is among the strongest in the energy industry.

The project has been more than 10 years in the works, involved close coordination with the military, and is located 27 to 44 miles offshore, so far offshore it does not raise visual impact concerns. The project’s two pilot turbines have been operating for five years without causing any impacts to national security.

CVOW enjoys bipartisan support and is within months of generating a massive 2,600 MW to support the fastest growing part of America’s energy grid. This growth serves the largest concentration of critical infrastructure in the world.

Virginia’s All-American, All-Of-The-Above-Energy Plan requires a range of power generation assets, including natural gas, advanced nuclear, and renewables. Virginia needs every electron we can get as our demand for electricity doubles. These electrons will power the data centers that will win the AI race, support our war fighters, and build the nuclear warships needed to maintain our maritime supremacy. Virginia’s grid needs addition of electrons, not subtraction.

We stand ready to do what is necessary to get these vital electrons flowing as quickly as possible.”

#5808. Biofuels in the EU Post-2030 Renewable Energy Framework

A deep-dive exploring the impacts of potential EU biofuels policy scenarios

The discussion around the future of the Renewable Energy Directive (REDIII) is underway as the European Commission devises what role renewable energy will play in transport in the post-2030 energy framework.

As the political priorities of the EU focus on energy independence and domestic industry, there is a renewed push for more first generation food and feed crop biofuels in the European transport energy mix. But these biofuels can have adverse impacts on the climate, environment and food supply chains.

To address these concerns, we have modelled three different policy scenarios to demonstrate the consequences of varying outcomes and their potential impact on the climate, environment and food security.

Our findings show that the current cap on first-generation biofuels set by the Renewable Energy Directive (RED III) can allow 72% more food- and feed-crop biofuels to be used in the EU transport energy mix, compared to what the bloc consumed in 2024.

When accounting for indirect land use change, even at current consumption, emissions from first generation biofuels already reach nearly 43 MtCO2e, about 23% more than the fossil fuels they replace.

When safeguards take the same form as stipulated in RED III and other key transport regulations, food- and feed-crop biofuel volumes, land-use area and emissions could all potentially double by 2040, emitting an equivalent to more than 5 million diesel cars.

In an unfavourable scenario, whereby the cap on first generation biofuels is harmonised at 7% across Member States’ transport sectors, volumes, land use and emissions would almost triple.

In a worst case scenario, where the cap on first generation biofuels is expanded beyond 7%, the negative consequences would be far heavier. We have used 10% as an example: volumes, land use and emissions could be four times today’s levels in 2040. Land dedicated to biofuels could reach 17.7 Mha, 18% of the EU arable land, an area larger than Greece, and emissions would exceed those of fossil fuels by 33 MtCO2e.

For advanced and waste biofuels, which are limited in supply, we show that the road sector is currently overconsuming available feedstocks, which should be directed towards sectors that cannot be electrified, particularly aviation. Furthermore, recent cases of fraud have highlighted the risks of relying on third country exports, making the case that all palm oil derivatives should be phased out and that effective safeguards should be put in place to allow for the most sustainable feedstocks.

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5807. The grid will fall short of AI’s power needs. These 6 resources can close the gap.

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

No single option is fast, firm and large enough on its own, writes Simon Irish, CEO of Terrestrial Energy.

Simon Irish is CEO of Terrestrial Energy, a developer of small modular nuclear reactors.

Most planning for artificial intelligence’s electricity demand starts with a forecast and asks whether it is right.

I would start somewhere else: Assume the grid will fall short, then ask how to close the gap — and how fast.

The evidence supports that starting point. McKinsey & Company estimates the U.S. will need 30 GW to 55 GW of additional capacity by 2030 as data center demand grows and aging plants retire. PJM Interconnection, the largest U.S. grid operator, has already seen the impacts of this trend. Its latest capacity auction fell about 6.6 GW short of its reliability requirement and cleared at the price cap.

Forecasts can be wrong, and requested load can be speculative or duplicative. But the PJM result is a market outcome, not a forecast. The useful question is which tools can close the gap, by when and at what cost.

The first four options below address serving data centers through the grid. The final two consider generation at or near the data center, with an optional grid connection. These categories overlap; on-site generation is a delivery strategy, not a generation technology, and can use multiple technologies.

Flexible demand. Duke University’s Nicholas Institute estimates the grid could absorb 76 GW of new load if that load accepted curtailment averaging 0.25% of annual consumption, in events of about 1.7 hours. It is the fastest option because it needs no new generation. It makes better use of capacity already on the grid.

But that capacity is finite, and beyond it, further load growth requires new supply. There is also a practical hurdle; to date, many data centers have shown little willingness to curtail AI workloads, because the value of computing far exceeds the cost of power.

Existing nuclear plants. Increasing output at operating plants through uprates and restarting closed plants can add reliable electricity supply, while extending operating licenses keeps existing plants running. A U.S. Department of Energy initiative targets 5 GW of added capacity at existing nuclear plants by 2029, and DOE has closed a $1.9 billion loan to restart Iowa’s Duane Arnold plant, returning 615 MW to service.

The limit is opportunity: Few operating plants remain candidates for significant uprates, and restarts are underway at nearly every plant where one is practical.

Natural gas. Gas offers firm power and a familiar path, but gas turbine supply has become the constraint. GE Vernova’s gas turbine backlog and slot reservations reached 116 GW at midyear 2026, and the company has said a heavy-duty turbine ordered today will not arrive until 2031. Buyers also take on fuel-price exposure and decades of emissions.

Wind, solar and storage. These resources can be deployed quickly and at scale. Their limit is that data centers require dependable power around the clock, so matching that requirement takes substantial overbuilding, energy storage and transmission infrastructure to deliver the same capacity as a firm resource.

On-site generation. Building a power plant at a data center can bring electricity online sooner by avoiding some grid upgrades and connection delays, where permitted.

However, it does not speed up equipment deliveries. An on-site gas turbine plant faces the same turbine shortages as one supplying the grid. Reliable operation also requires sufficient on-site backup capability or access to grid power during outages and maintenance. If a project relies on the grid, developers need clear terms defining that access and its cost.

Advanced nuclear energy. Advanced reactors offer firm, clean power and can be built as a fleet. They can serve data centers directly, with an optional grid connection. They will not close the gap this decade, and their real test is cost at fleet scale.

No single option is fast, firm and large enough on its own. Three principles follow.

Flexible demand, uprates at existing nuclear plants, and new wind, solar and storage can help meet near-term electricity needs.

Natural gas and on-site generation can help fill the gap while longer-term resources are developed, but only if equipment deliveries, permits and grid interconnection align with the data center’s schedule.

Advanced nuclear energy can contribute in the 2030s and beyond.

Utilities must plan now for both natural gas and advanced nuclear energy. DOE’s long-term modeling indicates 550 to 770 GW of additional firm, clean generating capacity could be needed by 2050, with advanced nuclear energy providing about 200 GW.

Infrastructure built to meet an immediate shortfall should also have a useful role later. A gas plant could provide firming capacity or operate during periods of peak demand as longer-term supply becomes available.

Make flexibility the price of speed. Utilities can offer earlier interconnection to customers accepting curtailment under defined, metered and enforceable terms. For data center operators, that is a choice between some power sooner and firm power later. Customers that can curtail load may get power sooner; those that cannot must wait for firm supply. This converts flexibility from an assumption into a contract and rewards developers whose flexibility is real.

Secure the long-lead items for firm, clean power now. Buyers who reserved gas turbine production slots early secured earlier deliveries; those ordering now may have to wait until 2031.

Firm, clean power has its own long-lead items, including fuel supply and equipment manufacturing capacity. Utilities and data center operators can sign reservation or offtake agreements that give suppliers the demand signal to build capacity before it is needed.

These principles also address the forecast debate. Some investments — flexibility, uprates and transmission — remain valuable across demand scenarios. Others are options worth buying, such as reservations for firm supply in the 2030s and beyond.

And some are bets to avoid: irreversible commitments sized to a demand number that may not arrive. If forecasts prove too high, the cost is the price paid to preserve an option. If they prove right and no one bought the option, the cost is the kind of shortfall PJM has already seen.

Planning for a shortfall is not pessimism. Of all the inputs AI needs, reliable power may prove the scarcest. The race to scale AI is a race to deliver power. Utilities, regulators and developers need tools that arrive on time, hold their value if forecasts change, and keep electricity reliable and affordable for existing customers.

#5805. New York state is short electric resources beginning 2033: ISO

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

New York will face an approximately 225 MW statewide resource deficiency, with the need for power resources growing to 1,850 MW by 2036, the state’s grid operator said Wednesday.

New York ISO’s 2026 Reliability Needs Assessment reveals the state’s increasing reliance on emergency operating procedures to maintain grid stability, highlighting the imminent resource shortfall. The ISO said it expects to call on demand response, voltage reductions, operating reserves, load curtailment and other measures, approximately 3.5 days next year, with that total growing to 21 days by 2036.

“New York’s reliability needs are no longer a distant concern,” Zach Smith, senior vice president of system and resource planning, said in a statement. “New generation and transmission resources take years to plan, permit, and build, so the reliability needs identified in this report must be addressed today.”

Along with the statewide resource deficiency, the report also identified local reliability needs. New York City is projected to be deficient by approximately 400 MW in 2032, growing to approximately 1,850 MW by 2036, while the Lower Hudson Valley deficiency grows from approximately 130 MW in 2032 to approximately 1,950 MW in 2036.

“While transmission investments can help address local reliability needs ... they cannot resolve the statewide resource shortfall,” the ISO said. “A combination of new generation, repowered resources, energy storage, dependable demand reductions, flexible large loads, and additional transmission will be required to maintain reliability over the next decade.”

NYISO expects demand growth of about 1,000 MW from manufacturing facilities and 1,700 MW from data centers will contribute to the shortfall. “The age and availability” of the state’s generation fleet is also a growing reliability concern, the grid operator said.

Approximately 4,600 MW of fossil generation will be more than 70 years old by 2036, according to the report. The assessment “accounts for this risk by projecting approximately 2,600 MW of generation as unavailable by 2036,” it said.

The ISO said it will continue to monitor the situation quarterly, reporting on the issue through its short-term reliability processes.

Once the 2026 RNA is approved by the NYISO board of directors, the grid operator said it will request updates to the base case “that may reduce or eliminate the identified reliability needs,” including changes in the status of generation and transmission projects, demand forecasts, demand response resources, large load assumptions and other system developments.

“If reliability needs remain after those updates, the NYISO will solicit and evaluate market-based solutions, regulated backstop solutions, and alternative regulated solutions” through the development of a 10-year reliability plan, the operator said.

#5804. Fuel constraints could challenge grid this winter, despite resource improvements: NERC

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Some areas in the Midwest, Southeast and New England could face resource shortages if transmission and pipeline constraints occur during periods of extreme weather, the reliability watchdog said.

Electric demand is growing, but “there has also been strong growth in resources,” Mark Olson, manager of reliability assessments, said Wednesday in a call discussing the outlook. “In many ways, the industry is on better footing going into winter this year,” he said.

However, Olson added that “historically, winter is the toughest operating period for the North American electric grid.”

While some models show the El Niño weather pattern can bring milder-than-average conditions that are a help to a constrained power system, “the reality is that extreme storms with ice and abnormal cold temperatures can lie within those averages,” Olson said.

“We're talking to our industry stakeholders about the need for extra vigilance. El Niño is a variable that adds to uncertainty for the winter,” he said.

Peak winter electric heating demand often coincides with higher residential gas usage, potentially leaving single-fuel generators that don’t have long-term gas contracts unable to show up when their generation is most needed, NERC officials said.

In the Northeast Power Coordinating Council’s Québec and Maritimes regions, “the supply can become strained during extreme winter peak demand,” NERC Senior Engineer Bob Tallman said on the call. “New England, as we've identified in the past several years, is assessed as elevated [risk] due to the constrained or limited gas pipeline infrastructure in that region.”

In the SERC Reliability Corp. East area, resources can fall short of extreme peak demand “during morning and low solar output periods of the day,” Tallman added.  And in the Southwest Power Pool and Midcontinent Independent System Operator territories, NERC identified subregions “where there are localized transmission constraints and it could be difficult to import into those areas if there was a wide-area winter event limiting local generation and increasing their reliance on imports.”

#5803. Almost 90% of Europe’s Battery Production at Risk from Franco-German Car Deal

Europe has huge potential for battery plants but calls to weaken or delay the Industrial Accelerator Act and carmaker demands to weaken the EU’s CO2 standards, risk handing Europe’s battery value chain to foreign competitors

The Franco-German car deal will secure just 13% of Europe’s planned battery cell production in 2030, new T&E analysis shows. Weak Made-in-Europe rules, combined with weaker emission reduction targets, risks handing over the market for battery production to Europe’s competitors, says T&E.

The IAA introduces local content requirements to ensure scarce public subsidies and corporate tax incentives anchor homegrown EU manufacturing. 100 GWh of cells and 250 GWh of cathodes could leak abroad because of weak Made-in-Europe rules, the analysis shows — equivalent to 1.5 million battery packs.

At the same time, Europe’s battery industry faces a double blow from the EU Parliament’s proposals to weaken emission reduction rules which will cut cell demand by 247 GWh or approximately 3.7 million EU made EVs between 2027 and 2032 — almost three times the annual capacity of Europe’s largest battery plant, LG Energy Solution in Poland.

Franziska Grüning, Raw Materials Officer at T&E, said: “The Franco-German deal is a disaster for Europe’s battery industry. Securing a full European battery value chain, from mining to production, and the high value jobs it will provide, should be a top priority for Europe’s leaders. Instead, they are debating how far to weaken rules that would secure domestically produced batteries. Weak local content incentives for batteries in the IAA will send a terrible political signal for the future of Europe’s battery industry. Homegrown companies struggling to cross the valley of death should benefit from an IAA demand boost.”

With many cell factories only just beginning to ramp up, most EU production expected by 2030 comes from plants that have not yet entered production. The future of factories such as Lyten (formerly Northvolt) in Sweden, PowerCo Salzgitter in Germany, or Verkor Dunkirk in France, could be jeopardised by this deal.

The EU’s battery industry must not go the same way as its solar panels industry, says T&E, which calls on the EU to reject the Franco-German deal proposal on batteries, and stick to the timelines and battery requirements of the EU Commission’s proposal. In addition, a strict Made-in-EU scope, or at most a narrow list of trusted partners of the EU27 plus the European Economic Area (EEA) and the UK, must be introduced, and there should be no further weakening of the car CO2 standards.

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.
