#5802. PJM urged to delay base capacity auction until data centers cut from forecast

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

State ratepayer advocates and the grid operator’s market monitor say holding the auction in December as planned threatens grid reliability and could hit consumers with billions in unnecessary costs.

The request from the ratepayer advocates comes amid nationwide concern about energy affordability and data centers, as reflected in the midterm elections. The letter says that given “the urgency of the approaching auction,” its signatories request a written response from PJM’s board no later than Nov. 23.

In PJM, affordability concerns have been driven by a recent spike in capacity prices caused by growing data center load forecasts. PJM’s current capacity market rules effectively prevent data center companies from paying for their own power supply and infrastructure costs.

PJM recognizes the financial impacts rising electricity bills place on households and businesses, Jeffrey Shields, a spokesman for the grid operator, told Utility Dive in an email.

“The December auction includes an established price cap, providing an important safeguard against how high prices can rise,” he said. “The system urgently needs capacity to meet rising demand. Postponing the auction this close to its execution could destabilize the market and increase the risk of power interruptions when customers need electricity most.”

Electricity demand is growing faster than new power supplies can be built, according to Shields.

“While decisions about whether data centers and other large electricity users can connect to the grid rest with policymakers and utilities, PJM must plan for the demand those decisions create.” Shields said. “That means moving forward with securing the power we need while continuing to advance solutions that address the challenges of growing demand and the pressure on electricity costs.”

So far, including existing and forecast data center demand in PJM’s capacity market has increased capacity costs by $29.4 billion for all customers, according to Monitoring Analytics’ Nov. 12 report that called for the auction to be postponed.

PJM has responded to pressure from states, federal regulators and consumers by proposing a two-part plan to meet data center demand by facilitating bilateral contracts outside its normal capacity auctions, and also holding a backstop procurement for large loads that do not have bilateral contracts. It is also pursuing reforms to its rules for interconnecting large loads and generating supplies. The status of these efforts is pending.

The market monitor’s concerns were amplified by Exelon’s Nov. 3 complaint at FERC that challenged PJM’s three-year forward auction, partly over the uncertainty in forecasting data center load and the resulting billions in potential excess capacity costs, the ratepayer advocates said.

The advocates noted that PJM plans to publish the final “planning parameters” for the upcoming auction, which may remove canceled or delayed data center projects from the auction’s demand curve, on Nov. 19.

However, the market monitor’s recommendation is not simply to update PJM’s load forecast, they added.

“It is to fundamentally change how forecasted data-center demand is treated in the capacity auction so that existing consumers are not required to subsidize speculative or uncommitted data center demand,” the advocates said. “The delay would allow PJM to implement the IMM recommendations, subject to FERC approval and appropriate reliability safeguards.”

A “statement of principles” endorsed in January by PJM and the governors of its 13 states emphasized that large-load forecasts must be “real and verified,” the ratepayer advocates said. “Proceeding with an auction that incorporates both real and speculative data-center demand, despite the [market monitor’s] findings, would undermine that commitment and expose consumers to billions in potentially avoidable costs.”

The letter was sent to PJM by the Office of the Ohio Consumers’ Counsel, the Delaware Public Advocate, the West Virginia Consumer Advocate, the Maryland Office of People’s Counsel, the Pennsylvania Office of Consumer Advocate, the New Jersey Division of Rate Counsel, the Illinois Attorney General’s Office and the Illinois Citizens Utility Board.

#5801. The Return Of Project Hail Mary … And Next Steps For Artemis, Too

Word has just dropped that the mega-hit sci-fi film “Project Hail Mary” has returned to the big screen for the holiday season with its nail-biting tale of science, adventure and friendship. Catch a brand new Hail Mary trailer on YouTube while the action heats up here on Earth, including solicitations for the next class of astronauts, the formation of the new US Space Academy, and next steps for the Artemis mission to the Moon.

On November 7, NASA (the National Aeronautics and Space Administration) began taking applications for the next class of astronauts. The deadline is 11:59 PM ET on December 3.

NASA received more than 8,000 applications during the last round of astronaut recruitment, in 2024. Only 10 candidates were selected — but don’t be discouraged. Just look at the payoff as described by NASA:

The next generation of astronauts will train to operate on the lunar surface, live and work beyond Earth, and take on missions that will push the boundaries of human exploration farther than ever before.

“The next generation of astronaut candidates could help write history while inspiring future scientists, engineers, and explorers,” adds Scott Tingle, chief astronaut and chair of the NASA Astronaut Selection Board. Interested? Apply here.

If you blinked, you missed the formation of the new US Space Academy. However, the folks over at the Louisiana Economic Development agency were on the ball. On November 12, LED announced that Louisiana has thrown its hat into the ring for the privilege of hosting the the forthcoming Space Academy.

“The Space Academy is a proposed NASA-supported federal academy that will educate the next generation of astronauts, engineers, scientists, and leaders for America’s space programs,” LED explains.

If all goes according to plan, the first Space Academy class will occupy Louisiana State University in New Orleans with a roster of 300 students in the fall of 2028, with room for a total of 4,000 — or more — to follow.

If you’re wondering why Louisiana thinks it has a good shot at hosting such an endeavor, Louisiana Governor Jeff Landry summarized the case in a press statement. “The United States Space Academy belongs in a state that builds the rockets, supports our nation’s military and is now home to the world’s largest spaceport,” Landry enthused.

As described in an LED press release, the state’s qualifications include hosting “America’s Rocket Factory,” aka the Michoud Assembly Facility in New Orleans. The facility is owned by NASA and managed by the Marshall Space Flight Center, which among other projects has just released a new album of “baby star sounds.”

The idea of a dedicated center for space education and training bubbled up from the White House just a few months ago, in August. NASA promptly jumped on it, inviting the governors of all 50 states to compete for the new facility.

Louisiana is determined to beat them all to the punch. With LED in the lead, collaborators on the state’s Space Academy proposal include Louisiana State University, the City of New Orleans, the business organization Greater New Orleans, Inc., and the New Orleans subsidiary of the leading utility Entergy among others, with all of the state’s US Senators and Representatives enlisted in support.

“A dedicated state fund, the Louisiana U.S. Space Academy Fund, will cover startup needs and independent operating infrastructure, lowering the federal investment needed to launch and sustain the Academy,” LED adds, noting that two other schools — Southern University at New Orleans and Tulane University — are also lending their support to the LSU campus.

LED and its partners are leaning heavily on the presence of the Michoud Assembly Facility to leverage its claim to host the Space Academy. As LED notes, Michoud has been front and center in crewed space flight for 65 years, and it sits only about nine miles from the LSU campus

“Michoud built the Saturn V first stages for Apollo, every Space Shuttle external tank, and the core stages for the Space Launch System rockets that power NASA’s Artemis missions,” LED explains.

“The National Center for Advanced Manufacturing at Michoud develops the composite and metallic manufacturing processes behind that hardware,” the agency adds.

LED Secretary Susan B. Bourgeois also chipped in her two cents. “Louisiana is building one of the most consequential aerospace ecosystems in the country, and the U.S. Space Academy would give us the opportunity to build the talent pipeline alongside it,” Bourgeois said.

“Cadets would learn alongside leaders in government and commercial and space, including NASA’s Michoud Assembly Center and Stennis Space Center, SpaceX Starbase Louisiana, Michoud tenants including Boeing, Lockheed Martin, Textron Systems, and Vivace,” LED elaborates, also taking note of the presence of the Naval Information Warfare Center among other resources.

“Greater New Orleans anchors the middle of America’s southern space corridor, which runs from Kennedy Space Center at Cape Canaveral to Johnson Space Center in Houston and SpaceX’s Starbase in South Texas, with Marshall Space Flight Center in Huntsville to the north,” LED adds again for good measure.

As for the Artemis mission, as of October NASA seemed pretty optimistic about the chances for sending people to the Moon sooner rather than later. A new schedule calls for an Artemis III test flight in Earth orbit next year. The flight will include shakeouts of docking and other systems between NASA’s Orion spacecraft and lunar landers from both from SpaceX and Blue Origin.

“Four astronauts will fly aboard Orion, launched on NASA’s SLS (Space Launch System) rocket, for a planned 14-day mission in Earth orbit to reduce risk ahead of the Artemis IV moon landing mission,” NASA explained.

“The Artemis III mission will unfold in sequence,” NASA explained. “Blue Origin will launch a Blue Moon lander test article into Earth orbit, SLS will launch Orion and its crew, docking with the Blue Moon lander test article, then SpaceX will launch a Version 3 Starship lander test article while Orion will undock from Blue Moon and dock with Starship.”

Assuming that all goes off without a hitch, 2028 will see US astronauts on walking on the Moon, presumably before anybody else gets there.

The last time US citizens set foot on the Moon, millions of Terrestrials witnessed the fuzzy, flickering action on live TV. If you were among them, share the experience in the discussion thread What was it like? Did everyone cheer? Or did somebody say something random such as “I need a cigarette,” and is that the moment that burns especially bright in your memory? Asking for a friend…

Image: Ryan Gosling stars as the unwilling but brilliant astronaut in the hit sci-fi film “Project Hail Mary,” which returned to the big screen this week for the holiday season (screenshot courtesy of Amazon).

#5800. Grid operators need data center ride-through rules that protect reliability

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The first facilities built to Texas’ new standard are about to come online, proving that firm, predictable regulation can protect reliability without slowing development, says ON.energy’s Alan Cooper.

Alan Cooper is CEO of ON.energy, a developer of power infrastructure.

On July 22, a mechanical failure forced a 230-kV transmission line in Northern Virginia offline. Within milliseconds, grid protection systems cleared the fault, as they are designed to do. The event would not have been a particularly noteworthy story, had it stopped there. It’s what happened next, though, that pointed to a growing challenge for grid reliability.

In response to the fault, data centers that had been drawing nearly 4 GW of energy, close to 4% of PJM’s load, collectively disconnected and switched to their on-site backup generation. The move sent voltage and frequency spiking across the system, a technically wonky but threatening result that caused power flickers as far away as Chicago. Thankfully, the prevailing grid conditions were strong enough to absorb the shock of the fault, so the grid held. But if the same event had hit Texas or even Northern Virginia at a time of higher demand, it could have caused a widespread outage. In West Texas, the Electric Reliability Counil of Texas estimates that a loss of as little as 3.2 GW could threaten grid reliability.

The power sector is not unfamiliar with the need for things hooked up to the electricity grid to “ride through” disturbance events. In 2023, the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission told the North American Electricity Reliability Corp. to develop requirements that inverter-based supply resources like wind and solar facilities must follow to ensure they “ride through,” i.e., stay online, during certain disturbances. And this time around, NERC, FERC and several grid regions have been quick to identify a similar need for large loads to adhere to analogous standards.

In an Oct. 14 presentation to ERCOT, Dominion Virginia confirmed that the disturbance fell within the ride-through parameters that have now been finalized or proposed by NERC and two grid operators (ERCOT and PJM Interconnection). They concluded that if those requirements had been in place, the event would have been avoided.

Clear ride-through standards protect the grid, and they protect data centers’ ability to keep connecting to it. The open question for grid operators and regulators isn’t whether to create them, but rather which facilities the rules should cover. Some proposals would exempt projects that aren’t likely to draw grid power for years. Texas took a firmer line and in doing so, gave other operators and regulators an effective blueprint to follow.

The Public Utility Commission of Texas approved ERCOT’s ride through rule and it took effect in August. ERCOT made three choices that are key to making it work.

It drew the applicability line at energization. Facilities that energize roughly 18 months or more after ERCOT’s first market notice must comply with the rule. That exempts projects that are nearly finished, with equipment already ordered or even installed, and it applies to projects that exist only on paper, which can still change their electrical design for a small share of what a retrofit would cost later.

It measured the risk in exempt capacity within a cluster. ERCOT studies ride-through risk in its quarterly stability assessments. Those studies identify the clusters of large load that could trip together and the rule limits the amount of exempted load — i.e., the amount that is not equipped to ride through disturbances within each cluster — to 3,200 MW per single fault. Its latest results flag North Texas and Central Texas as active risk areas, with far West Texas being monitored.

It made the risk transparent and predictable. ERCOT watches each load cluster’s combined consumption in real time. When a cluster’s actual demand reaches about 2,500 MW, a mitigation plan kicks in: each utility and scheduling entity gets a “not to exceed” limit based on its share of recent peak use, and ERCOT keeps the authority to curtail non-compliant load. Because ERCOT publishes the study results and updates them over time, developers can see how much headroom is left long before they hit the limit. This transparency gives them time to decide whether to install ride-through capability, change their plans, or accept the risk of curtailment.

The result is that existing data centers keep running, and projects under construction generally avoid a forced redesign or need to buy different equipment. Other grid users stay protected. The first facilities built to the new standard will connect in the coming months.

In general, regulators and regional grid operators across the country are right to be careful about borrowing from ERCOT. Its isolated grid and energy-only market make much of its experience hard to apply elsewhere.

This isn’t one of those cases. A data center’s response to a voltage dip is the same in Virginia, Ohio or Texas, and so is the physics of thousands of megawatts disconnecting at once. NERC, PJM and other grid operators should adopt the core tenants of the Texas approach:

A single large-scale event would change the conversation overnight. Instead of debating exemption criteria, policymakers would be questioning if data centers can remain connected. Clear standards now are the best way to ensure the industry has a path forward.

The impact of a major event wouldn’t stop at the fence line: a multistate outage would affect the communities where data centers are concentrated and working to become good grid citizens. Proposed exceptions to ride-through requirements leave the door open for exactly the kind of event that consumers and policymakers are concerned about.

None of this protection is theoretical or depends on a technology breakthrough. Equipment to keep data centers connected through grid faults is commercially available today and already running in the field. The first facilities built to Texas’s new standard are about to come online, proving that a firm, predictable regulation can protect reliability without slowing market development. All too often, reliability problems are left lingering until there’s an emergency.

Large load ride through concerns can be solved now. The only thing missing is clear, consistent rules that protect grid reliability while providing certainty to the industry.

#5799. Smarter Cooling, Stronger Reliability: Advancing Wind Turbine Gearbox Performance

As the wind energy industry matures, operators and service providers are increasingly focused on maximizing turbine availability while controlling long-term maintenance costs. Although major drivetrain components often receive the spotlight, one overlooked system can have an outsized impact on turbine reliability and performance: gearbox oil cooling.

Heat exchangers play a critical role in maintaining proper oil temperature and viscosity within wind turbine gearboxes. When cooling systems become clogged with airborne debris, dust, or organic material, oil temperatures rise, lubrication performance declines, and component stress increases. Over time, these conditions can contribute to reduced gearbox efficiency, elevated maintenance demands, and avoidable downtime.

“Wind operators today are balancing increasing performance expectations with the realities of aging fleets and rising maintenance costs. Technologies that improve cooling efficiency and reduce service exposure can have a meaningful impact on long-term turbine reliability and operational profitability,” according to Mike Erickson, HYDAC’s Market Manager for the Wind Industry.

Traditional “panel cut” heat exchanger designs have long been susceptible to clogging due to their tightly packed fin structures. In many wind environments — particularly agricultural, dusty, or high-pollen regions — debris accumulation can severely restrict airflow. Oil leaks can further compound the issue by trapping contaminants within the exchanger core, making cleaning more difficult and labor intensive.

This operational challenge has driven the need for more resilient cooling technologies designed specifically for real-world wind farm conditions.

HYDAC’s new “Square Wave” heat exchanger design represents a significant advancement in addressing these reliability concerns. Engineered to allow debris to pass more freely through the exchanger fins, the design minimizes clogging while improving cooling efficiency under demanding operating conditions.

The importance of effective cooling cannot be overstated. Wind turbine gearbox oil typically operates within an optimal temperature range of approximately 45°C to 65°C. Even modest temperature increases can significantly impact oil viscosity. According to field data, a 12°C rise in oil temperature can reduce viscosity by nearly 28 percent — affecting lubrication quality and accelerating wear on critical drivetrain components.

Field trials have demonstrated measurable operational benefits from the Square Wave design. During a one-year trial on GE 1.x turbines, the upgraded exchanger consistently reduced clogging and lowered maintenance requirements compared to traditional panel cut cores. Operators reported reduced up-tower cleaning time and fewer temperature-related cooling faults.

Field validation across multiple turbine platforms has further reinforced the operational advantages of the Square Wave design. In one case study involving legacy 1 MW-class turbines operating in the Pacific Northwest, a major renewable energy operator installed pilot sets of Square Wave heat exchangers as part of a cooling performance evaluation. Installation teams reported the upgraded exchangers integrated seamlessly into the existing platform with no retrofit complications.

After initial operating data demonstrated improved cooling consistency, the operator expanded the evaluation program with additional units. Long-term SCADA analysis later showed turbines equipped with Square Wave technology operate approximately 20 to 25 percent cooler than comparable turbines using conventional heat exchanger designs under similar operating conditions. The data also highlighted another important advantage: the Square Wave cores continued to maintain airflow and cooling efficiency in debris-heavy environments where traditional exchanger designs became increasingly restricted over time.

The successful deployment also generated interest from additional wind service organizations seeking retrofit-ready cooling upgrades that could improve turbine reliability while reducing maintenance burdens across aging fleets.

“What operators are increasingly recognizing is that small improvements in thermal management can create meaningful gains in turbine reliability, maintenance efficiency, and long-term asset performance,” said Erickson. “The field data we’re seeing continues to validate the value of designing cooling systems specifically for real-world wind operating environments.”

More recently, long-term SCADA data from Mitsubishi platforms further validated the technology’s performance. After two years of operation, turbines equipped with Square Wave heat exchangers operated approximately 19 to 25 percent cooler than reference turbines using standard exchanger designs under comparable operating conditions.

For wind asset owners and service providers, the implications are substantial: Improved thermal management supports healthier oil viscosity, more stable gearbox operation, and potentially longer component life. At the same time, reduced cleaning frequency and fewer cooling-related service events translate directly into lower operational expenditures and increased turbine availability.

As the industry continues to prioritize lifecycle optimization and energy production efficiency, innovative subsystem upgrades such as advanced heat exchanger technology are becoming increasingly valuable. Rather than waiting for costly failures or performance degradation, operators are seeking practical retrofit solutions that improve reliability without requiring major system redesigns.

“Effective thermal management is critical to protecting gearbox health and maintaining oil performance over time. By addressing one of the most common causes of cooling degradation — debris accumulation — operators can improve uptime while reducing unnecessary maintenance interventions,” said HYDAC’s Erickson.

For wind stakeholders evaluating ways to improve gearbox reliability, reduce maintenance exposure, and enhance turbine performance, cooling system optimization deserves greater attention. Proven retrofit technologies like advanced heat exchanger designs can offer a practical path toward improved uptime and operational efficiency without requiring major system overhauls.

As fleets age and performance expectations continue to rise, collaboration between operators, service providers, and technology partners will be essential to building more resilient wind assets for the future. Now is the time to evaluate whether existing cooling systems are supporting — or limiting — long-term turbine reliability goals.

“HYDAC’s goal is to help wind owners and service providers solve practical operational challenges in ways that create long-term value across the entire asset lifecycle. Innovations like the Square Wave heat exchanger are designed to improve reliability, simplify maintenance, and support greater energy production over time.”

Don’t let cooling system limitations impact turbine performance.

Proactive cooling optimization can help operators reduce maintenance exposure, protect critical drivetrain components, and maximize energy production across the asset lifecycle. To learn how HYDAC’s Square Wave heat exchanger technology is helping wind operators improve reliability and lower operating costs, connect with our wind energy specialists today or visit: https://www.hydac.com/en-us/industry-solutions/renewable-energy/wind-power/

#5798. Last Chance to Get It Right: The Industrial Accelerator Act Will Make or Break Europe’s Battery Industry

How the Franco-German auto deal, a wide Made-with-EU scope, and carmakers abandon Europe’s battery value chain to foreign competitors.

There is a growing consensus that Europe must reduce its reliance on Chinese cleantech, especially batteries

The IAA marks a major shift in EU industrial policy. It introduces Union content incentives to ensure scarce public subsidies and corporate tax incentives are used to support European manufacturing. This demand-side pull is crucial if the energy transition is to deliver high-value industrial jobs and lessen critical geopolitical dependencies. There is a growing consensus that Europe must reduce its reliance on Chinese cleantech, especially batteries. But several proposals currently before co-legislators risk severely undermining that overarching goal. Diluting battery criteria in the IAA means giving up on a strategic value chain for Europe, leaving it to China only.

T&E’s assessment shows that only an IAA with a calibrated timeline, robust battery requirements and a strict geographical scope will deliver substantial benefits for Europe. Without secured demand, announced industrial capacity will simply not materialise.

The Franco-German automotive deal from 16 October 2026 is a major blow for Europe’s battery industry as it severely dilutes incentives for batteries. It suggests battery sourcing requirements for eligible vehicles of 10% in 2028-2029, 20% in 2030-2031 and 70% from 2032 on. This is far below what the market already does: over 50% of cells in electric vehicles (EVs) sold in the EU are already Made-in-Europe. By 2030, the deal would only secure 13% (62 GWh) of Europe’s announced cell production: a further 206 GWh would likely find European buyers, though with no guarantee, while 220 GWh would find no demand at all. Even in 2032, the 70% would still not secure a substantial share of potential cell capacity (only 45%).

Another major risk is applying a wide Made-with-EU geographical scope to battery criteria. Compared to a strict EU-only scope, including free trade agreement (FTA) partners puts around 100 GWh of cell demand and 250 GWh of cathode active materials (CAM) demand at risk of leakage in 2030. Limiting the scope to trusted partners only helps to a limited extent. Since Europe can produce enough cells to meet demand under a Made-in-EU IAA, adding partner countries risks leaving EU production without offtakers: even with trusted partners only, a third of potential EU cell supply could be affected. For CAM, around 30% of expected EU supply could be left without a buyer under a trusted-partners scope, rising to 50% under an extended one.

The midstream, CAM and their precursors (pCAM), is the weakest link in Europe’s battery value chain. Boosting this key segment and diversifying away from China is essential for resilience as it shields European industry from supply disruptions and export controls. Under a strict Made-in-EU scope, reaching IAA CAM requirements is technically and commercially viable by 2030, requiring only limited sourcing from trusted partners (33 GWh cell-equivalent). From 2032, EU production can cover demand from EVs under support schemes. A Made-in-EU IAA would generate 323 GWh of extra EU CAM demand in 2032, around the output of six mid-sized EU CAM plants, bringing total EU-made CAM to 407 GWh (securing 94% of expected EU supply).

pCAM is just as vital for resilience and to establish local offtake for European refiners and recyclers. A 28% EU pCAM requirement in 2032 applied to a carmaker’s entire fleet sales (not only for vehicles eligible for public support) creates enough demand for expected EU supply to materialise and projects to reach Final Investment Decision (FID). Onshoring CAM and pCAM adds only a modest cost of $10.6/kWh for Nickel Manganese Cobalt (NMC) and $6.5/kWh for Lithium Iron Phosphate (LFP) batteries. This is a necessary sovereignty premium that will fall as the EU supply chain matures.

The car CO₂ standards are as critical to industrial delivery as the IAA. Even under a strict Made-in-EU scope, the European Parliament’s position as approved by the leading committee in November 2026 would sharply cut expected EV sales and reduce EU cell demand by 247 GWh between 2027 and 2032, which equals 3.7 million EU made EVs.

Finally, the proposed fleet booster mechanism fails to drive industrial onshoring and relocation if its compliance trigger is set below 85%. Carmakers’ efforts to lower that threshold aim at getting a stamp of approval on their sourcing strategy in China. A major risk for public subsidies ending up benefiting imported cars with Chinese batteries. Attempts to lower the Booster below a 85% threshold should be rejected, especially if combined with weaker battery criteria.

It’s key to reject the Franco-German deal proposal on batteries.

Battery criteria in this deal are too weak to create additional demand and undermine the IAA’s core purpose.

By Franziska Grüning, Raw Materials OfficerBrussels (EU), T&E.

Jordane Faizant, Analyst, FleetsBrussels (EU)

Jeanne Andre, Analyst, Raw MaterialsBrussels (EU)

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5797. Google signs 50-MW superhot geothermal PPA with Quaise Energy

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The agreement will support Google’s Oregon data center operations beginning as early as 2030, advancing development of a technology designed to access geothermal resources deeper underground.

Google's agreement with Quaise gives superhot geothermal an important commercial partner as developers seek to move enhanced geothermal systems from demonstration projects to utility-scale deployment.

“Geothermal energy remains largely underutilized. It supplies less than 0.5% of global electricity and is limited to just a handful of states in the U.S.,” Diane Hughes, senior vice president of marketing and communications at Quaise, said in an email.

Unlike conventional geothermal plants, which depend on naturally occurring underground hot-water reservoirs that are geographically constrained and rarely exceed 400 degrees Fahrenheit, enhanced geothermal uses engineered reservoirs to access heat in deeper rock formations.

Quaise is developing millimeter-wave drilling designed to ablate rock without mechanical contact. The company is designing wells to reach depths of up to 65,000 feet, where rock temperatures can exceed 932 degrees Fahrenheit and conventional drill bits cannot withstand the heat, according to Hughes.

“Superhot wells like these can produce up to 10x more power per well than what's currently in play and bring geothermal to places where it isn't viable or economical today," she said.

The agreement comes as technology companies increasingly seek firm power sources to meet data center demand. Google has signed nearly 35 GW of clean energy purchase agreements globally since 2010, including previous investments in next-generation geothermal, according to its most recent environmental report. Google says its three Oregon data center campuses are sourced with 83% carbon-free energy.

Google is also pursuing other emerging technologies, including advanced nuclear power and long-duration energy storage, as part of its efforts to achieve net-zero emissions.

Quaise said in its 2025 year-end update that it had drilled 118 meters through granite at its central Texas test site and is now working toward a depth of 1 kilometer.

#5795. At $26,000, Would You Have The Last Laugh In This 1987 Corson Ferrari 512BBi?

Before you ask, no, today's Nice Price or No Dice 512BBi isn't actually a Ferrari. Instead, it's a Fauxrrari, one of 77 reputedly built by the Corson Motorcar Company on donor Pontiac Fieros. I know! You could have fooled me, too. Let's see whether buying this faker would be a foolish move.

First, however, let's look at what went down yesterday. When SCCA began the Trans-Am Championship Series in 1966, it was positioned as a more international-style road course competition, in contrast to NASCAR's wide oval shenanigans. Over the years, the series extended to tracks as far away as Canada and Puerto Rico. Today, the series' primary sponsor, Pirelli, maintains that international flair, though the number of competitions has been reduced and is now limited to tracks within the Continental U.S.

Starting in 1969, Pontiac celebrated the series by introducing the WS4 Trans-Am handling and appearance package on the company's F-body Firebird. Almost immediately iconic, that model served as the Firebird's performance vanguard until the model's demise in 2002.

Yesterday, we looked at a fairly unique Trans-Am, a 1997 convertible with the added-output WS6 ram air induction package and the promise of a well-cared-for life. While the comments were peppered with declarations that it wasn't the kind of car many of you would want to own, they also noted that the $13,000 asking price seemed in line with the description. Ultimately, that earned the Trans-Am a solid 66% 'Nice Price' win.

In the 1970s, the ubiquitous VW Type 1 Beetle served as the basis for many a home-built kit car. Dune buggies, classics, and swoopy sports cars emerged from the burgeoning industry, standing in for the original utilitarian sedan bodies. By the 1990s, however, the Bug was no longer available in sufficient numbers, and was reaching classic status on its own. That sent kit car companies to seek another platform to bend to their will.

That platform proved to be Pontiac's mid-engine Fiero, with its plastic body panels and monocoque steel chassis making the perfect canvas for all sorts of reimagining or imitation. It's that latter idea that we're looking at today.

That's right, today's 1987 Corson Ferrari 512BBi isn't the real deal. Ha, ha, fooled you!

Instead, this is a copy of that classic Pininfarina-penned Italian sports car, wrapped around the Fiero's mundane mechanicals and interior. And hey, it looks pretty convincing at first glance.

Built by Phoenix, Arizona's Corson Motorcar Company, the Fiero-based Ferrari homage was, in fact, far too close to the original in looks. That saw Ferrari successfully sue the faker-maker, resulting in the cessation of production and, rumor has it, the destruction of the company's molds. Something south of 80 Fieros were converted before the axe fell, including 5.7-liter V8 conversions and a small smattering of open-top Spiders.

This car, based on a 1987 Fiero, still has the stock 2.8-liter, 140-horsepower Chevy V6 sitting sidesaddle behind the driver, mated to a Getrag 5-speed manual. According to the ad, the engine has been updated with a "Custom exhaust" that the seller says "sounds great." Other notable features include a sunroof, cruise control, working A/C, and power locks. The (possibly fake) two-piece wheels look to be in great shape and are fitted with BFG tires that have plenty of life in them. An additional set of spinner-style center caps will accompany the car in the sale.

Looking at the car gives you an uncanny-valley feeling. From some angles, it's difficult to tell it from a real Berlinetta Boxer. From others, most obviously from the side, its proportions are sufficiently off to make the Fiero foundation obvious.

Like the real car, both the front and rear clips open fully for access to the spare and engine on either end. That likely made the fiberglass molding a lot easier as well. Other oddly real-appearing elements include the tail lamps, front grille and fog lamps, and the iconic orange turn signal panels on the nose. All the—questionably legal—badging is also present.

Inside the Corson, it's all Fiero, all the time. A Ferrari-badged steering wheel has been fitted in place of the original, along with a wood-veneer kit for the dash and console. Also, this must have been a GT or Formula back when it was nothing more than a Fiero, as it sports the ancillary gauge pod atop the center stack. Everything, inside and out, appears to be in great shape. The car shows 78,716 miles on the odometer and will come with a clean, obviously Fiero, title.

According to classic.com, the current average selling price of a real Ferrari 512BB is $290,242. The cheapest sale that site shows on record was $188,770, meaning even the deals aren't within the range of mere mortals. That's why cars like this Corson exist: to let the riff-raff like us get a taste of what exclusivity might be like.

At $26,000, this Corson 512BBi is a far cry from what a real car would cost, and it serves the added purpose of probably ticking off those who can actually afford a real edition or two. Who wouldn't want that?

A more important question is: who would want this fake Ferrari? What do you think? Is there a market for such a car, and does that $26,000 asking price put this car in play in that market? Or is that just too much for a game of "let's pretend"?

Buffalo, New York, Craigslist, or go here if the ad disappears.

#5792. $1199 — Anyland Rev Pro: An Electric Dirt Bike With a Wheelie Assist Built for Beginners

When I was a kid, I loved to ride. Riding a bike, for me, is synonymous with childhood, in fact. We used them to get ourselves places, but we also just used them to entertain ourselves. Inevitably, this led to popping wheelies, building ramps and trying jumps, and doing all sorts of other stuff my Mom just didn’t want to know about, no matter how much I wanted to brag. As an adult, I still love to ride — as the famous saying from Animal Farm goes, “four wheels good, two wheels mo’ bettah!” (I’m paraphrasing, a lot, but you get the point).

So when Anyland hit us up to do a review of their new Anyland Rev Pro, an all-electric dirt bike capable of 34 mph and assisted wheelies, the inner child in me jumped at the chance.

Wheelies look effortless until you try one. Anyone who has learned on a dirt bike knows the usual lesson plan: too little throttle and the front wheel never lifts, too much and you’re flat on your back. Anyland, a startup electric dirt bike brand, created the Rev Pro as an entry-level electric dirt bike with a controller that lifts the front wheel to a preset angle and holds it there, so a new rider can focus on balance instead of juggling throttle and brake.

The system, which Anyland sells as WELIXON and says it co-engineered with controller maker Moxin, offers three angle settings: 25°, 35°, and 45°. Riders start at the lowest angle and move up as their balance improves. According to Anyland, the controller delivers continuous, smooth power to keep the front end at the chosen angle, which is designed to reduce the risk of a “loop out,” where the bike flips backward, one of the most common ways wheelie learners get hurt. Once you’re ready, you can switch the assist off, and the Rev Pro behaves like a regular throttle-controlled electric dirt bike for practicing manual wheelies.

Underneath the trick, the Rev Pro is a lightweight, mid-drive electric dirt bike. Anyland lists:

The speed modes set the first speed caps as low as 12 mph, and matter for the audience Anyland is targeting: teenagers and adults who are new to dirt bikes. Starting in the lowest mode lets a rider build skills before moving up, and gives parents a way to keep a first-timer in a gentler setting.

The Rev Pro arrives in a compact box and includes all the tools you’ll need to set the bike up. My friend and I (two untrained professionals) did it in about 40 minutes:

In short, the ride is really fun. I found the suspension incredibly responsive and comfortable, the acceleration smooth and quick, and the riding position much more comfortable than I anticipated. It’s been raining non-stop where I live, so while I got this bike a few weeks ago in the mail, I haven’t had a chance to get it up onto the trails yet, so stay tuned for that. Testing it on the dirt sidewalks and running over curbs shows me it will likely be a great addition to my weekends.

A wheelie assist doesn’t make any dirt bike risk-free. Wear a helmet, gloves, and protective gear every time, and start in the lowest speed mode at the 25° setting. Practice using the rear brake, supervise younger riders, and stick to private land or designated off-road areas. Check your local rules before riding anywhere else, since many electric dirt bikes aren’t legal on public roads.

Anyland’s Black Friday and Cyber Monday price starts as low as $1,199, and prices have varied, so check the current price before you buy.

For a startup brand in a crowded field of lookalikes, the Rev Pro’s pitch is simple: a beginner-friendly bike built around one clever feature. If you’ve always wanted to learn a wheelie without the bruises, it’s worth a look.

#5790. Six new ways to simplify solar installs: Top Rack Technology brings mounting innovation to RE+ 2026

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 18, 2026

From smarter waterproofing to 360-degree rail adjustment, Top Rack Technology is putting installer efficiency, rooftop flexibility, and cost control at the center of its latest product lineup.

As the solar industry continues to look for ways to reduce installation costs and streamline residential projects, mounting hardware is getting a fresh dose of innovation. Top Rack Technology, Inc. (TRT) is making that case at RE+ 2026, where the solar racking manufacturer is showcasing six new mounting solutions designed to simplify rooftop installations, improve flexibility, and help installers work more efficiently.

Its latest lineup reflects a practical approach to product development: addressing everyday installation challenges while keeping system performance, aesthetics, and affordability in focus.

Six Products, One Common Goal: Better Installation Efficiency

Among the new offerings is TRT’s Full-Coverage Skirt System, which targets an important consideration in residential solar: appearance. By concealing exposed array edges, the system delivers a cleaner, more finished look that can help installations better complement residential properties.

The new TRT-G2 Bracket takes aim at installation simplicity and competitive pricing. Designed around practical field requirements, the bracket can be paired with TRT’s ButyLok waterproofing component, creating an integrated attachment and roof-sealing solution.

Waterproofing is particularly important in rooftop solar, where roof penetrations are still a critical consideration for installers and property owners alike. ButyLok introduces a shoe-like component containing pre-applied, gel-like butyl material. When compressed by the G2 bracket, the material spreads across the intended sealing surface, potentially reducing manual sealant application and simplifying the installation process.

TRT is also introducing SAVRIX, another residential attachment option developed with manufacturing efficiency, installation practicality, and cost competitiveness top of mind.. Meanwhile, the RF-2 Rail Fastener is designed to simplify rail-to-bracket connections.

Top Rack Technology’s FreePivot Bracket features a 360-degree rotatable rail fastener and built-in height adjustment to accommodate varying rooftop conditions and simplify solar panel installations. Credit: Top Rack Technology

But perhaps the most versatile addition is the FreePivot Bracket. Its 360-degree rotatable rail fastener allows installers to accommodate attachment points that aren’t perfectly aligned. Built-in height adjustment helps compensate for uneven roof surfaces, while its all-metal construction supports durability. The one-orientation design and single-socket installation concept are also intended to reduce tool changes and keep crews moving.

Beyond the six new products, TRT is showcasing its established portfolio, including the TRT 01 Rail-less Base System, TRT 02 Rail-Base System, and TRT 03 Tile Hook System.

The company is also highlighting its Online BOM Design Tool, which supports project setup, structural design inputs, module layouts, engineering verification, and automated bill-of-materials generation. Connecting digital planning with physical mounting solutions could offer installers and distributors a more streamlined path from project design to field execution.

Installation hardware doesn’t need to be flashy to make a difference. Improvements in attachment flexibility, waterproofing, assembly time, and aesthetics can translate into meaningful operational advantages.

#5789. Mercedes-Benz's Point-To-Point Assisted Driving System Is Tomorrow's Co-Pilot In Training

One of my lingering concerns with any self-proclaimed autonomous driving system is losing control. Far too many times, I've fought lane-keeping assist through highway construction zones, but one automaker might have found the sweet spot: Mercedes-Benz is starting to roll out Point-To-Point Assisted Driving for its MB.Drive Assist Pro advanced driver-assistance system. The feature is impressive for a "Level 2++" system and lets drivers nudge their vehicles through precarious situations that would leave similar Tesla systems flustered.

MB.Drive Assist Pro relies on an extensive suite of eight cameras, 12 ultrasonic sensors, a mid-range radar, four corner radars, and, of course, a software stack co-developed with Nvidia. The semi-autonomous system doesn't rely on a cloud connection, leveraging an onboard high-performance computer capable of up to 254 TOPS, or 254 trillion operations per second. Mercedes has even grander ambitions in its sights with continued development of its SAE Level 3 MB.Drive Pilot system, eventual release of an SAE Level 4 system, and a robo-limousine ecosystem.

Full disclosure: Mercedes-Benz flew me out to San Francisco, California, and put me up in a hotel on the Embarcadero to experience its latest MB.Drive Assist Pro offerings and the bumper-to-bumper traffic over the Bay Bridge.

Point-To-Point Assisted Driving does what it says on the box. A driver can select a point in the navigation system and their MB.OS-equipped vehicle will drive them there. Over an hour's drive through the hilly, chaotic streets of San Francisco, I sat in the driver's seat of a Mercedes-Benz GLC EV as it safely navigated unprotected left turns and waited at stop signs for pedestrians to cross intersections. Sadly, the software hasn't quite got the California roll down yet.

The system operated exceptionally well, but it's not flawless. I had to intervene twice during the drive. First, the GLC misjudged the distance between a stopped bus and an upcoming intersection on a two-way street, and attempted to pass in the oncoming lane before sighting there wasn't enough room to slot in front of the bus. Second, the GLC detected a phantom stop sign and gradually stopped in the middle of the street.

This is where Point-To-Point Assisted Driving's best fallback came into play: you behind the wheel. In both situations, I was able to easily drive my way out without deactivating the Point-To-Point drive. The system encourages nudging. If your car comes to a stop at a non-existent intersection, you can simply tap the throttle and the car will continue on. You can also nudge the steering wheel to keep your distance from parked cars or puddles near pedestrians. Mercedes refers to the feature as cooperative steering.

While the Pro level receives point-to-point assisted driving, Mercedes also introduced hands-free highway driving for the mid-tier MB.Drive Assist option. MB.OS-equipped vehicles can operate semi-autonomously at speeds up to 85 miles per hour. The system also uses proactive lane changes so cars can maneuver into ideal lanes to approach exits and pass slower traffic; drivers can trigger lane changes themselves by using their turn signals. It honestly makes cruising down the interstate a breeze. I only had a slight scare when I got off I-880 and the car attacked the exit lane near the speed limit as traffic crawled in the adjacent lane. The software can be far more confident than a human, who would be wary of an impatient driver suddenly pulling into the exit lane.

It should be noted that both MB.Drive Assist and MB.Drive Assist Pro aren't truly hands-free, requiring the driver to touch the steering wheel occasionally. A typically green symbol on the dash will turn red, and if the visual warning is ignored, an audible beep sounds. Then, if there's still no contact with the wheel, the car will pull over and the Point-To-Point Assist will be soft-locked. The feature will only unlock and become available if the car is restarted.

It might sound extreme for Mercedes to lock a driver out of their vehicle's semi-autonomous feature if they ignore system warnings, but it's out of an abundance of caution. The German automaker wants to ensure that it has the safest software on the road, going above and beyond Euro NCAP and U.S. NCAP standards. On an empty dockyard in Alameda, Mercedes engineers set up a dummy-filled test range to showcase its suite of ADAS safety features.

Mercedes vehicles could react to pedestrians in traffic at a moment's notice. Automated emergency braking was still effective at highway speeds and avoided motorcycle riders darting from blind spots across the vehicle's nose. The software suite can even judge with steering away would be safer than braking. MB.Drive Assist comes with impressive creature comforts, like a 360-degree parking camera, rim protection warning, and parking and reverse assists.

These safety features are standard as MB.Drive on all MB.OS-equipped vehicles. MB.Drive Assist is optional on 2027 CLA, GLB, GLC, GLE and C-Class, as well as standard on the S-Class, GLS, and the AMG GT 4-door Coupe. MB.Drive Assist Pro is currently optional on CLA, GLC, GLE, GLS and S-Class, and the company claims that availability will be wider for the 2028 model year.

Mercedes has yet to reveal how much the optional subscriptions will cost vehicle owners. The automaker is rolling out the new systems in December with an early access program available to selected participants in California, Stuttgart and Munich, before nationwide rollouts next year through an over-the-air update.

#5788. Sierra Club Submits Comments Urging SEC to Preserve Shareholder Rights & Climate Accountability

WASHINGTON, D.C. — On Thursday, Sierra Club submitted two comment letters opposing the Securities and Exchange Commission’s (SEC) proposal to rescind Rule 14a-8, which allows shareholders to put proposals addressing climate risks and other corporate issues before fellow investors for a vote, and amend Rule 14a-4(c), which governs how companies may exercise discretionary voting authority over shareholder proposals. The Sierra Club submitted both a technical comment and a grassroots comment, reflecting concerns from over 1,100 of its members and supporters.

Together the submissions urge the SEC to withdraw its proposed changes and preserve investors’ ability to raise concerns directly with corporate management and press companies to address material risks, including climate-related risks, that can affect their long-term financial interests.

Jessye Waxman, Campaign Advisor on Sierra Club’s Sustainable Finance Campaign, released the following statement:

“Climate change and other systemic risks have real consequences for companies, investors, and the broader economy. Shareholder proposals give investors an important way to bring those risks to management’s attention, push for meaningful action, and hold companies accountable when they fail to act. The SEC’s proposals would weaken those tools, making it harder for investors to protect their long-term financial interests and leaving corporate management with less accountability for the risks its decisions create. The SEC’s proposals would shield corporate management from meaningful shareholder scrutiny, while undermining the very investor protections the Commission is charged with upholding.”

Rule 14a-8 establishes when public companies must include qualifying shareholder proposals in the materials they send investors ahead of shareholder meetings. The SEC’s proposed repeal would leave decisions about shareholder proposals to state law and individual companies’ governing documents, eliminating a federal framework that investors have used for more than 80 years.

Sierra Club’s technical comments explain that material risks, like climate change, can both have significant financial consequences for companies and create systemic financial risks that extend across investment portfolios and the broader economy. Shareholder proposals give investors a way to bring these concerns to company leadership and fellow shareholders, including by surfacing risks that management may overlook or have limited incentives to address. The letter emphasizes that, for decades, this process has served the broader market by helping investors collectively identify and address financially material risks.

The letter warns that replacing a consistent federal framework with varying state laws and corporate bylaws could make shareholder engagement less predictable and push disputes over proposals into costly litigation. It points to the increase in shareholder lawsuits challenging proposal exclusions during the 2026 proxy season as an indication that disputes do not disappear when the SEC steps back from administering the process; instead, they can shift to more costly and uncertain avenues.

The submission also opposes proposed amendments to Rule 14a-4(c), which would expand company management’s discretionary authority to vote shareholders’ proxies on proposals excluded from company proxy materials, unless shareholders affirmatively opt out. Sierra Club’s letter warns that these changes could allow management to vote on proposals that investors have not had an adequate opportunity to evaluate.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

Sierra Club has 374 posts and counting. See all posts by Sierra Club

#5787. GM Prepares For Life Without CEO Mary Barra

Good morning! It's Thursday, November 19, 2026, and this is The Morning Shift, your daily roundup of the top automotive headlines from around the world, in one place. This is where you'll find the most important stories that are shaping the way Americans drive and get around.

In this morning's edition, General Motors is trying to figure out what its world without Mary Barra in it would look like, over 1,500 Rockstar employees are voting on whether to unionize as Grand Theft Auto 6 goes live, German automakers are staring down the barrel of a work stoppage as a labor fight takes hold and Ford has started shipping the battery cells it's building in partnership with China's CATL.

If you'd like a recap of the latest auto news sent to your inbox each weekday, you can sign up for Jalopnik's free Morning Shift newsletter here.

Mary Barra has been the CEO of General Motors for nearly 13 years at this point. For some, it's hard to think of modern GM without her, but with her 65th birthday just a month away, folks within The General are clearly starting to do so. The automaker is working with an executive search firm to look for possible outside candidates to succeed Barra as it prepares for a future that'll come once the highest-paid Big Three CEO hangs it up. Don't worry, current GM employee, the company is also looking at internal candidates.

Barra has apparently been laying the groundwork for months to hand over the keys to the company. She's already GM's second-longest tenured CEO — beaten out only by its first CEO, Alfred P. Sloan, Jr., who was in the position for 23 years. Still, her tenure is about twice that of the average modern CEO of an S&P 500 company, and she's the first woman to run a global automaker. So far, GM hasn't indicated publicly when Barra could step away from her post. From The Wall Street Journal:

GM has historically groomed chief executives from its own ranks, and internal candidates remain in the mix. Contenders have included the company's finance chief Paul Jacobson; Sterling Anderson, a high-profile Silicon Valley hire who serves as Chief Product Officer; and Mark Reuss, a 40-year GM veteran and Barra's No. 2, according to separate people familiar with the matter.

Barra took over as GM was still rebuilding after its 2009 bankruptcy and has led the Detroit automaker through trade wars, the global pandemic and tumultuous forays into electric and autonomous vehicles.

GM's stock, up 25% from a year ago, has risen about 136% since she took the reins, and the company has returned tens of billions to investors through sweeping buybacks.

Barra took over with a mandate to shake up the automaker's notoriously hidebound corporate culture. Her GM has emerged as a smaller but more profitable automaker anchored by its lucrative U.S. truck business.

GM is selling millions fewer vehicles around the world than it was a decade ago, after retreating from unprofitable foreign markets while slashing low-margin sedans and other slow sellers in the U.S. Yet it is generating record levels of cash and turning bigger profits despite fewer sales.

The company has spent the past decade trying to pivot away from its roots as a traditional car manufacturer and instead remake itself into a software-driven tech enterprise—a push marked by a string of Silicon Valley hires, an expanding California hub and heavy bets on autonomous driving and digital services.

Of course, not all of Barra's gambles have worked out. GM lost billions on its Cruise robotaxi division before pulling the plug, and its original electric vehicle plans have been a bit of a disaster that cost the automaker $6 billion in write-downs. WSJ also notes that several of the high-profile tech hires it made saw relatively short tenures.

Barra and GM are clearly playing things close to the chest. Just a few months ago, the CEO was asked if she's considering stepping down, and she told Fortune magazine that she remains energized.

"I still feel like I can contribute and continue to build the foundation," she told the outlet.

We'll see how long she continues, but the $48.2 million in realized compensation she made in 2025 certainly helps.

Unless you're at Patrick Star's house, you're aware that Grand Theft Auto 6 launched at midnight, and it's one of the biggest video game releases ever. In preorder sales alone, GTA 6 generated over $1.6 billion in revenue, and that number is sure to climb as folks buy it in the coming days. Even with all that immense amount of money, workers at Rockstar, the studio behind GTA, don't think they're getting their fair cut. That's why over 1,600 Rockstar employees in the UK are voting on whether to form a union, and if it wins recognition when the ballot closes on November 26, the Rockstar Games Workers Union would be the largest in the UK gaming sector.

In an op-ed for The Guardian, Rockstar animation programmer and union representative Nestor Uriarte said the studio's reason for a unionization push is because of a decade-long realization that "to tackle the industry's mounting challenges we need to come together." He argues that union recognition would help safeguard the working conditions of developers who built GTA 6 over the past decade. From PC Gamer:

"Behind these numbers are thousands of people whose talent and hard work make the games possible. With a recognized union, we can ensure everyone, across every department, sees a fair share of the success we contributed to," Uriarte said. "Our games are industry-leading. Our wages, conditions and bonuses should be too."

Working conditions at Rockstar produced one of the early flashpoints in the advancing push for games industry unionization over recent years, sparked by employees' descriptions of its "death march" crunch culture and allegations of sexual assault. While the studio's taken steps towards a healthier workplace in the years since, employees have reported continuing concerns around gender pay gaps, crunch, and nebulous bonuses conditions.

Coinciding with the union vote, a Glasgow tribunal is weighing testimonies in the ongoing legal battle around Rockstar's alleged union-busting and firing of employees who had been involved in organization. Meanwhile, just days before a launch that's projected to earn landmark revenue, Rockstar parent company 2K reportedly laid off a third of the employees at XCOM and Civilization developer Firaxis.

"It says a lot about the industry that even my colleagues and I, working on the biggest game of all time, are worried about getting laid off," Uriarte said. "But the truth is that workers across the sector are helpless to resist the waves of redundancies crashing around us, until we get organised."

"We've heard from colleagues who are nervous that the union is an outside troublemaking force that will threaten the company," Uriarte said. "But we're not outsiders. We are hundreds of studio employees who live for Rockstar, its output, and the players. We want to see it succeed."

Uriarte said that recognition would also provide developers with a greater ability to negotiate the rapid proliferation of AI in game development, which represents an existential threat to workers in an industry where one third of the labor force has been laid off in the past two years.

Audi and BMW are among the German automakers that are facing 24-hour strikes this week after workers approved industrial action. It's the latest escalation in an intensifying nationwide dispute over pay, benefits and working hours at a time when automakers are already under pressure caused by weak demand, high costs and stiff global competition. Employees are seeking greater flexibility in labor costs and working hours, and the IG Metall union is pushing for higher pay and commitments to project investment and jobs at domestic factories. From Bloomberg:

IG Metall will halt production Friday at Audi's main Ingolstadt plant and BMW factories in Munich, Dingolfing and Landshut, it said Thursday. Workers at sites operated by Robert Bosch GmbH, ZF Friedrichshafen AG and other industrial firms in Bavaria are also set to walk out for a full day.

In northern Germany, IG Metall has already secured backing for 24-hour strikes at selected plants across Bremen, Hamburg, Mecklenburg-Western Pomerania, Schleswig-Holstein and northwestern Lower Saxony.

Major companies in the region include Mercedes-Benz Group AG, which operates a large vehicle plant in Bremen, as well as aircraft manufacturer Airbus SE and shipbuilder TKMS AG & Co. KGaA, though the union hasn't identified which sites will be targeted.

The union has offered to allow companies to deviate from collective agreements in exchange for commitments to invest in German plants and secure products and jobs, according to IG Metall. Employers haven't taken up that proposal, the union said.

IG Metall is demanding a 5% pay increase and higher apprentice wages in Bavaria, and it claims that employers have proposed no permanent increases for the first 20 months, following by a 1.3% raise. That's, uh, less. They're also looking for greater scope to cut special payments and extend working hours.

This wouldn't be the first such action. Over 118,000 workers participated in an earlier wave of short strikes in Bavaria through last week. The 24-hour walkouts are the first such escalation by IG Metall during a wage round since 2018.

As I was just saying, solidarity forever.

Ford has started producing its lower-cost battery cells at a new plant in Michigan that are meant to be used in its upcoming Fathom electric pickup truck. The central Michigan facility is owned by Ford, but it uses technology licensed from Chinese battery maker CATL, which hasn't sat right with some lawmakers and officials.

The prismatic-shaped cells made at the Michigan plant have a lithium-iron-phosphate (LFP) chemistry, and they're going to head to Ford's plant in Louisville, Kentucky, to be fitted to Fathom prototypes. From Reuters:

Several automakers are transitioning to LFP chemistry because it ​costs less than nickel-based batteries and is considered more durable.

US ​Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy wrote in a September letter to ​Ford CEO Jim Farley that his agency was "deeply alarmed" by Ford's reliance on ‌licensed ⁠technology from CATL at its plant in Marshall, Michigan

Ford called the letter a "wrongheaded attempt to capture headlines," and touted its development of a US battery plant while other automakers continue to ​import batteries from ​China.

Ford's Alan Clarke, ⁠vice president of advanced development projects, told media this week that working with CATL was the ​fastest way to build affordable electric vehicles in ​the US. "CATL ⁠being the biggest in the world, it means that they're the best supplier to get us that technology and help us learn ⁠how ​to produce LFP cells," he said.

Seemingly undeterred by the Trump administration's foolishness, Ford says it will be able to make 40 cells every minute at the battery facility, and a spokesperson for the company expects them to still be eligible for a federal battery manufacturing tax credit.

#5786. Novel ADC Could Expand Treatment Options in Ovarian Cancer

— Torvutatug samrotecan may benefit patients across folate receptor alpha expression levels

Updated results from the FONTANA trial presented at the annual European Society for Medical Oncology congress showed promising activity with the investigational antibody-drug conjugate (ADC) torvutatug samrotecan (torvu-sam) in patients with previously treated ovarian cancer, including those with low or absent folate receptor alpha expression.

In this exclusive MedPage Today video, Funda Meric-Bernstam, MD, of the University of Texas MD Anderson Cancer Center in Houston, discusses the findings and how torvu-sam could expand treatment options for a broader patient population, including its potential role alongside existing therapies and in future treatment settings.

Following is a transcript of her remarks:

FONTANA is looking at torvu-sam, which is a folate receptor antibody-drug conjugate. This is an antibody-drug conjugate that targets folate receptor and has a topoisomerase payload. This trial had presented at ESMO 2025 where the dose escalation had been shared, and this was updated data in that we had further follow-up on our dose optimization, and we'd also included a cohort of patients that had ultra-low or null expression of folate receptor. So these were results from 156 patients that had been previously treated and now getting this antibody-drug conjugate.

The class of topoisomerase ADCs I think are really emerging as an exciting therapeutic opportunity. And we were pleased to see the activity in the folate receptor expression-high cohort where the objective response rate was 61.7%. But also we enrolled patients that had folate receptor expression low, and in that patient population, the response rate was 57%. We also enrolled a group of patients that had shorter follow-up, but the objective response rate in the folate receptor expression ultra-low or null was 48%.

So overall, this was also notable that these responses were quite durable. And we reported in the folate receptor-high group, their durability was 10.2 months, with the folate receptor-low group being 8.3 months. So overall the PFS [progression-free survival] in the high group was 9.7 months and the low was 7.6 months. Taken together to suggest that really this is an active agent that has no new safety signals, adverse events consistent with the topoisomerase ADC mechanism of action.

These are different-mechanism-of-action ADCs. Mirvetuximab [soravtansine (Elahere)], of course, has a different safety profile as well, but notably patients need to have folate receptor expression tested and they need to have 75% tumor cell positivity for that monotherapy activity. So here, the fact that we saw activity in the low cohort, which had 25% of the tumor cells positive, as well as the ultra-low/null cohort, that really makes it a lot more promising because feasibly we could offer this therapy even without testing and a lot larger patient population will be able to benefit from this agent.

Currently, ongoing clinical trials are looking at torvu-sam in comparison with mirvetuximab in patients that are fully receptor-high, also comparing torvu-sam in patients... in fully super-low with standard chemotherapy, as well as looking at torvu-sam in the maintenance setting.

This is a really exciting time. We're learning more about how we can better target cell surface antigens, and I think topoisomerase inhibitors really have a great opportunity to be impactful in a much larger patient population.

#5784. Pacific Power Begins New Customer Protections Against Data Centers

Regulators approve new agreement to ensure data centers pay for their own energy costs

SALEM, Oregon — The Oregon Public Utility Commission has approved an agreement between advocates and Pacific Power. Now, Pacific Power customers have a strong suite of protections against data centers’ impact on Oregonians’ energy bills.

Starting Jan. 1, 2027, new Pacific Power data centers will be held responsible for their own energy costs. These new rules will only apply to future data centers and existing ones making significant investments, which regulators identified as the most pressing challenge to address. Right now, the utility has very few existing data centers on its system.

“With a long line of data centers waiting at Pacific Power’s door, I’m confident that we have the right protections at the right time,” said Bob Jenks, Executive Director of Oregon Citizens’ Utility Board. “We were able to stop more extreme impacts on power bills just in time.”

Data centers over 100 MW will also have to pay a small surcharge to fund energy efficiency upgrades for low-income households. This includes funding for deep home retrofits, lowering energy use across the grid while saving customers on monthly bills.

“Many of the households we serve are already struggling to afford their energy bills and risk having their utilities shut off. Data centers will help fund weatherization, energy efficiency upgrades, and the critical home repairs that often prevent our clients from accessing these improvements in the first place,” said Charity Fain, Executive Director of Community Energy Project. “Requiring data centers to cover the costs of the energy infrastructure they need, rather than passing those costs on to residential customers, is an important protection for low-income households, seniors, and people with disabilities.”

Under this order, if there is a power emergency, Pacific Power can prioritize critical energy use for other customers, like renters and homeowners, by curtailing service to data centers. Increased electric demand by data centers strains the grid and increases the potential for blackouts during heat waves or hard freezes.

“This is a big win that puts Oregon at the forefront of managing data center growth the right way,” said Joshua Basofin, Oregon Clean Energy Program Director at Climate Solutions. “It protects Oregonians from rising costs and strengthens grid resilience. Data centers should not harm our ability to access essential electricity to power our lives and keep us safe all year.”

Regulators also ruled that new data centers can only be connected to Pacific Power’s grid if there is enough emissions-free electricity available to serve them. Under HB 2021, Pacific Power is required by law to serve all customers with 100% emissions-free electricity by 2040. This new rule will help manage costs for other customers outside of data centers during the transition to clean energy.

“We have long known that clean energy is the most affordable form of energy,” said Ben Brint, Senior Climate Program Director at Oregon Environmental Council. “This ruling is a win for data center accountability, and for Oregonians across the state — especially frontline communities already bearing the brunt of climate impacts and rising energy costs. It will save ratepayers money in the short and long run, allocating costs appropriately while advancing Oregon’s 100% clean energy goals.”

This change also comes at a time when many Oregonians are calling for broader changes to how the state handles data centers. Impacts on data centers are poised to be an issue again at the Oregon State Legislature in 2027.

“The POWER Act demonstrated the people’s power to fight back against the rising energy rates and ecosystem harms that large data centers inflict,” said Emily Bowes, Policy Strategist & Acting Director at Sierra Club Oregon. “In 2027, alongside new and returning climate champions, we will continue to fight for a clean energy transition in Salem and statewide.”

In 2025, the Oregon Legislature passed the POWER Act to help utilities rein in data centers’ added costs to Oregonians’ energy bills. Last fall, Portland General Electric began the work to roll out this new law in practice. In January, Pacific Power started a similar process for its customers. The next step is to address the few existing data centers on Pacific Power’s system, starting as early as summer 2027. It is unclear if the final phase will include rate adjustments.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.
