#5782. 2027 Genesis G80 And GV80 Hybrids Launch In The U.S. With 347 HP And Much Better Fuel Economy

We have been eagerly awaiting the arrival of Genesis' first full-on hybrid option for a while, as it's been one of the only luxury brands not to launch such a powertrain. The GV80 Hybrid was finally shown off in Korea in August, and now on Thursday at the Los Angeles Auto Show, Genesis has revealed that midsize crossover in U.S.-spec form and held the world premiere of the the even cooler G80 Hybrid sedan, too.

The GV80 and G80 Hybrids will share the same powertrain, which pairs a turbocharged 2.5-liter inline-4 gasoline engine with a 7-speed automatic transmission, a single electric motor and a battery pack of unknown size. It's Hyundai Motor Group's first hybrid system with a liquid-cooled battery, and the system produces a combined 347 horsepower while returning Genesis-claimed efficiency gains of 24% in the GV80 and 30% in the G80, compared to the regulas gas-only models. Official fuel economy estimates for these two hybrids are not available just yet, but Genesis says both the GV80 SUV and G80 sedan should return a maximum driving range of over 500 miles on a tank of gas.

Genesis says its turbo hybrid system was "engineered specifically to highlight the brand's longitudinal, rear-wheel-drive-based architecture," and the liquid-cooled battery allows for a higher maximum discharge output when compared to air-cooled batteries. We've already sampled a version of Hyundai Motor Group's turbo-hybrid powertrain in the segment-topping Hyundai Palisade Hybrid. Jalopnik's own Andy Kalmowitz praised that version of the powertrain for its seamless transitions between electric and hybrid operation, which bodes well for the Genesis system and its upgrades.

In Genesis form, this hybrid setup produces 347 horsepower and 391 pound-feet of torque, which is 18 more horses and 52 more lb-ft of torque than the Palisade's Hybrid system. It's also 47 horsepower more than the G80 and GV80's base turbocharged 2.5-liter four-cylinder powertrain, and it matches the torque output of the top twin-turbo 3.5-liter V6, which Genesis says helps give it "responsive acceleration from a stop."

Both the GV80 and G80 Hybrids will offer buyers adjustable regenerative braking settings, a nice features that is not always found on traditional hybrids, and it has a Smart function that automatically adjusts regenerative braking based on driving conditions. They also have a Stay mode that allows some auxiliary functions like the climate control and multimedia functions to operate without starting the gas engine.

Both the GV80 Hybrid and G80 Hybrid sedan get a redesigned front grille with a new 3D look, new wheel designs, and new exterior colors. The GV80 has new 19-, 20-, and 22-inch wheel designs developed specifically for the Hybrid, and three new exterior colors: Seychelles Beige, Cardiff Green, and Brunswick Green Matte. It also will come with four new two-tone interior color combinations, real wood trim options, and upholstery quilting patterns.

The G80 Hybrid reflects a bigger change when compared to its gasser siblings: it's actually 5.1 inches longer overall than other G80s. Though Genesis doesn't explicitly say where the G80 Hybrid's extra 5.1 inches goes, it's almost certainly a 5.1-inch wheelbase stretch, since that's the same change that was made to the 2027 Genesis G80 Electrified in other markets. (That car was quietly canceled in the U.S. last year due to slow sales, if you'll remember, so we haven't gotten the LWB G80 until now.) That means it will have a limo-like wheelbase of 123.6 inches, or just 1.6 inches shorter than the flagship Genesis G90 sedan. The G80 Hybrid also gets new 18- and 20-inch wheel designs, and new exterior paint options that are inspired by locations and natural elements from around the world like Wadden Gray, Milos Blue, and Barolo Red.

These new hybrids will provide Genesis buyers with a third powertrain option on both cars, and they'll each be sold in the usual array of trim levels. Standard features include a 27-inch OLED gauge cluster and infotainment screen, and an array of standard active safety features. Genesis hasn't released official North American pricing or availability for either hybrid yet, but we expect pricing to fall between their base turbocharged four-cylinder powertrains and the more expensive twin-turbocharged V6, so somewhere around $70,000 for both models.

#5781. 2027 Hyundai Elantra Feels Too Nice To Be Treated Like An Economy Car

For the most part, economy cars are treated as fairly disposable pieces of equipment meant mostly for getting folks from Point A to Point B. They're not exactly treated with much care during their lives, and when they're used up, they're scuttled off to the nearest junkyard. More likely than not, that same fate will befall the 2027 Hyundai Elantra and 2028 Elantra Hybrid, and I don't think that's fair. Top to bottom and front to back, this little sedan is just far too nice to deserve a fate like that.

As with the redesigned Tucson, Hyundai put its entire back into its eighth-generation compact sedan, giving it a distinct and decidedly upmarket look, a premium-feeling interior, a cutting-edge brain with stellar interior tech, and a hybrid powertrain that'll get over 50 mpg, all while maintaining the idea that "affordability is standard," according to Olabisi Boyle, Hyundai's Senior VP of product planning and mobility strategy. While I haven't driven it yet, the slightly larger new Elantra seems mighty impressive when it comes to the design, feel and specs.

Full Disclosure: Hyundai paid for my food and transportation so I could check out the new Elantra and Tucson at a warehouse in Jersey City, New Jersey.

The first thing about the new Elantra that will jump out at you about is undoubtedly how it looks. It's the latest example of Hyundai's "Art of Steel" design language that we've previously seen on the China-only Ioniq V, Boulder concept and recently revealed Tucson. Hyundai also told me that the Elantra's design pulls from the N Vision 74 concept, and even though this is a totally different kind of car, you can certainly see those cues, especially in the wide fenders of the rear three-quarter section. The high-performance N version of the Elantra has already been spied, too, and it's going to look sick as hell.

The Elantra is slightly bigger than it used to be; 3.4 inches longer and 1.2 inches wider, to be exact, with 1.2 inches added to the wheelbase. Hyundai says the majority of that extra space goes to the second row, with gains in legroom (1.1 inches), headroom (0.7 inches) and shoulder room for rear-seat occupants, and now folks who accidentally had a kid will be able to better fit a rear-facing car seat.

Despite the extra size and funky angles, the 2027 Elantra still cuts through the air with ease, achieving a 0.26 coefficient of drag. Apparently, H-shaped daytime running lights and taillights are very aerodynamic. Who knew? The wedgey front end, also reminiscent of the N Vision 74, doesn't hurt either, and neither do the flush-mounted door handles.

It all helps explain why the Elantra is a real fuel economy winner whether you're looking at the entry-level gas motor or the optional hybrid. Fitted with 16-inch wheels, the 147-horsepower 2.0-liter inline-4, with the help of a CVT, will return up to 35 mpg combined, according to Hyundai. The 155-hp hybrid's 1.6-liter four-cylinder is mated to a six-speed dual-clutch transmission and 1.49-kWh battery, and Hyundai says it's going to achieve up to 53 mpg combined, which puts it firmly in "really, really good" territory. There's no word on other performance metrics right now, but I wouldn't expect anything too exciting, if I'm honest. The hybrid also comes with a Stay mode that lets you run accessories off the hybrid battery rather than the ICE motor when stationary for longer periods of time.

While the exterior is full of hard edges and sudden cuts, the interior is a far softer, more inviting place, one that wouldn't look out of position on a car that costs far more than the Elantra's currently unannounced price would suggest. Hyundai calls the furniture-inspired design ethos "Curve of Upholstery," and it's supposed to be all about comfort, simplicity, and minimizing driver distractions. It not only looks pretty thanks to interesting styling and materials choices, but it also feels nice to the touch with solid build quality.

Hyundai says Elantra is a "software-defined vehicle," which is pretty uncommon for an internal-combustion-powered car. Mostly Hyundai's spokespeople talk about its Pleos Connect operating system and its capabilities, like next-gen navigation (and AI assistance crap), and it explains why the interior's focal point is the 12.9-inch infotainment touchscreen in the center of the dash. The left section of the screen serves as a driver display showing speed and other pieces of vehicle information, while the rest of it is a regular infotainment screen with configurable widgets for media, maps, and everything else you can imagine — including things you can't imagine, since Pleos allows owners to download apps created by others.

Beneath the center screen are dual wireless 15-watt Qi 2.0 chargers and 100-watt ultra-fast USB-C charging ports for your many important devices, and you can run audio from said devices into an optional 12-speaker Bang & Olufsen audio system. Not too shabby.

Then, behind the steering wheel is a slim 9.9-inch display that is meant to keep the driver's field of vision uncluttered and only display critical information like speed, as well as radio, map directions or a few other things via widgets. We first saw both of these screens in the Tucson, and they make the Elantra feel far more tech-forward than a usual compact sedan would. Adding to that tech-forwardness is Hyundai's SmartSense suite of driver-assistance systems, which includes the classics like lane-keeping assist, lane centering, adaptive cruise control, forward collision avoidance and blind-spot monitoring.

At launch, the gas-only Elantra will come in SE, SEL Sport, and Limited flavors. As you may imagine, features and materials get nicer as you move up the range, but the 12.9-inch screen, adaptive cruise control, push-button start and a few other niceties are all standard from the get-go. Coming a bit later (no word on how much later) will be the 2028 Elantra Hybrid, which will come in SE Blue, SEL Sport, and Limited trims. It's the same deal as the gas car, but there's even more as standard, including dual-zone climate control and Stay mode.

I know that right now the Elantra is shiny and new, with its covers just pulled off at the 2026 LA Auto Show, and soon it'll face the sorts of abuse that all budget-friendly cars do. Before that happens, enjoy the Elantra and what it has to offer the world, because it's not very often an automaker swings for the fences on a cheap vehicle like this. Yes, vehicles like the Corolla, Civic and Mazda3 are very solid, but they're never exactly inspiring — certainly not from a design perspective, at least.

The eighth-generation Elantra elevates the segment, and it might force other automakers to rethink their budget vehicles as well. It certainly looks polarizing from the outside, and it won't be for everyone, but it's also distinctive, and that's something that is very hard to come by on a budget. If you're the type of person who appreciates that, plus a comfortable interior and tech that punches well above its weight class, then it's best you start getting ready now, because the Elantra is set to hit U.S. dealers in early 2027.

#5779. Should All Adults Be Screened for Lipoprotein(a)?

— Recent trial findings have provoked pushback of guidelines issued earlier this year

As a drug targeting lipoprotein(a), or Lp(a), failed to prevent cardiovascular events in a closely-watched clinical trial, some experts are questioning recent national guidelines recommending that all adults be screened for it.

Back in March, a collaboration of 11 cardiology-related medical societies strongly recommended, for the first time, that all adults have their blood tested for Lp(a) -- also referred to as "Little a" -- at least once.

Higher scores, the committee said, reveal patients at much higher risk of developing atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease, incentivizing more aggressive management strategies, such as further lowering higher LDL cholesterol, HbA1c, body mass index, and other risks. The guidelines followed the European Atherosclerosis Society's similar recommendation in 2022 and the European Society of Cardiology's reinforcement in 2025.

But many prominent physicians say they are confused or are outright opposed to adding such a universal test. They argue that Lp(a), a heart disease risk marker known for years, is genetically inherited and not affected by LDL, diet, exercise or weight loss, and stays roughly the same through life.

They say the relationship to one's actual risk is unclear because Lp(a) may not necessarily be the cause of disease, but rather, may be related to something that is.

And there's not much one can do about a high score beyond what doctors normally recommend to reduce heart disease and stroke risks in their patients.

On top of that, CMS is allowing regional variation in whether Medicare covers the test, meaning that some seniors will have to pay for it out of pocket, depending on where they live.

"A lot of people are going to be very unnecessarily worried about a useless test that we should not be ordering or recommending in the first place," Rita Redberg, MD, MSc, a preventive cardiologist at the University of California San Francisco and former editor of JAMA Internal Medicine, told MedPage Today.

"It's certainly not going to make a difference in people once they pass age 50. You should be on a healthy lifestyle program no matter what your 'little a' is," Redberg said.

James Stein, MD, a cardiologist and emeritus professor at the University of Wisconsin in Madison, who has written several Substack blogs opposing universal use of the Lp(a) test, told MedPage Today the guidelines have "done a real disservice to the entire medical community by recommending widespread population screening because it creates a lot of anxiety."

"It also leads to a large downstream test cascade ... most of which is not indicated at all, and some of which actually puts you at additional risk for more problems," Stein said.

Then why did the committee make this recommendation?

"In general, there's been a 3-to-5-year push to do more Lp(a) testing, part of which is because there's a bias towards action. Let's do more. Let's prevent more disease," Stein said. "Part of it is because there's been some intellectual seeding, getting all of us ready for the Lp(a) lowering drugs, which a majority of people thought would show reduction in cardiac events."

In the HORIZON trial, the antisense oligonucleotide drug pelacarsen reduced Lp(a) levels, but it failed to reduce major cardiovascular events. That was a huge disappointment to researchers who had high hopes the drug would dramatically shrink cardiovascular death, strokes, and emergency cardiac interventions. If it had, it would have provided an important tool in the fight against cardiovascular disease.

But now, clinicians may find themselves at a loss for what to do about patients with a high Lp(a).

Stein remembers a recent clinic when three patients with no other cardiac risk factors "came in crying. Each one brought in a letter (showing their high scores)."

A woman in her early 70s had an extremely high score, above 350 nmol/l, with no family history and no other risk factors, and one of her children also had a high score, Stein said.

"'I can't believe I gave this to my child,' she told me," Stein said. "Now she is a healthy woman who feels like she has a sword dangling over her head. She does not."

Jason Goldman, MD, immediate past president of the American College of Physicians and an internist in Coral Springs, Florida, prefers recommending a coronary calcium CT score rather than an Lp(a), and was glad that at least the committee mentioned that option in its new guidelines.

"I am less enthusiastic about saying 'Little a' needs to be checked for everyone once because we don't have a treatment to lower it and improve outcomes, but we do have a treatment to lower LDL and cardiovascular disease," Goldman told MedPage Today. "CT calcium, I think, should be ordered on everyone, when appropriate."

He wishes the committee would reconsider its recommendation, especially in light of the HORIZON trial.

At the same time, several doctors told MedPage Today they welcomed the guidelines in their practices.

Jordan Rennicke, MD, a family medicine physician in Johnson City, Tennessee, orders Lp(a) draws for all his patients. And a few times, scores for patients without any risk factors including family history of cardiovascular disease have come back particularly high.

He knows that can "open up a can of worms," but he told MedPage Today he "values the information tremendously because then I can have a more informed discussion with my patients."

Karl Nadolsky, DO, an endocrinologist in Holland, Michigan, also checks all his patients. "It tells us who is carrying an extra inherited risk" that other scores don't show.

Roger Blumenthal, MD, chair of the guidelines committee and a Johns Hopkins cardiologist, said anticipation of positive pelacarsen trial results had nothing to do with the Lp(a) testing recommendation.

He said there's "overwhelming evidence from a multitude of studies that Lp(a) is a strong causal risk factor for atherosclerosis and aortic valve calcification and aortic valve stenosis."

The European societies' recommendations were particularly influential, he told MedPage Today. "We felt there are so many people who get cardiovascular disease at an earlier age than expected, and one common denominator is that they have a higher Lp(a). We felt it is a very reasonable test to do one time."

"Maybe some people say, well, you didn't have to have it as your strongest recommendation, you could make it a class 2A, and I certainly can understand that. But our colleagues across the pond have said the same thing ... that it's a good way to identify people who need to improve their lifestyle habits, that they may be at higher risk of atherosclerosis and aortic valve disease."

"And maybe we can do a better job of motivating people to improve their diet and exercise, and if you lower cholesterol significantly, you seem to neutralize most of the risk associated with a higher Lp(a)," he said.

Blumenthal emphasized that the "committee's guideline is not a rule. We're not saying that you have to do that. It's a simple blood test and it lets me know whether I take after my uncle or aunt who had a stroke or a heart attack at age 50. Then yeah, sure. Let's check it."

Blumenthal said in his own practice, he and many Johns Hopkins colleagues typically do not go by the new guideline to order Lp(a) blood screening for everybody. In his own practice, he orders it for patients who want to know their score, or if there's obvious risk factors like family history.

"There's a lot of well-meaning people on all sides," he said. "Some feel like if you measure anything, you're going to necessarily alarm people. But our feeling is that most people would like to know if they may have a factor that increases their risk, and if it does, then maybe that'll be the extra motivation."

"I understand there are other points of view and concerns," he said.

#5775. Quadruple Agonist Reduced Weight Without Major Muscle Loss in Mid-Stage Trial

— Once-daily oral agent also showed favorable tolerability

NATIONAL HARBOR, Md. -- An investigational oral quadruple receptor agonist led to substantial weight loss with preservation of lean mass, a randomized phase II trial presented here showed.

By week 13, the two highest tested doses of NA-931 -- 150 mg and 120 mg -- led to reductions in mean body weight of 13.8% and 11.3%, respectively, compared with a reduction of 1.9% with placebo among adults with obesity or overweight and weight-related comorbidities (P=0.001 and P=0.002).

Participants receiving the two lowest doses -- 60 mg and 90 mg -- lost 5.5% and 8.7% of baseline body weight, respectively, according to Lloyd L. Tran, PhD, of developer Biomed Industries in San Jose, California, and colleagues.

The topline findings "support the potential of NA-931 as a first-in-class oral quadruple receptor agonist for weight loss, with promising efficacy and tolerability," Tran said at the ObesityWeek annual meeting, adding that the results also "suggest potential muscle preservation."

Using dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry (DEXA) measurements, which estimate lean soft tissue, including skeletal muscle, organs, and water (though changes do not directly establish shifts in muscle function), lean mass decreased by 1.7% over 13 weeks among participants treated with NA-931.

For context, longer-duration trials of the GLP-1 agonist semaglutide (Wegovy) over 68 weeks and the dual GLP-1/GIP agonist tirzepatide (Zepbound) over 72 weeks reported reductions in lean mass of 13.2% and 10.9%, respectively. According to a 2025 meta-analysis of 22 randomized trials of semaglutide and tirzepatide, including 2,258 patients, lean mass reductions made up roughly 25% of total weight lost.

"We believe we are entering an era of post-GLP-1 where there is a need for sustainable obesity management" that pairs meaningful weight loss with preserved muscle strength, Tran said. That preservation is particularly critical for older adults, he noted, as muscle mass naturally declines by roughly 3% to 8% per decade after age 30 and accelerates after age 60.

NA-931 is a small molecule that targets four receptors: insulin-like growth factor-1 (IGF-1), GLP-1, GIP, and glucagon. Because the compound readily crosses the blood-brain barrier, only a low dose is needed for efficacy, Tran explained, which may contribute to its favorable tolerability profile.

In the 150-mg NA-931 group, rates of gastrointestinal adverse events -- a common side effect of GLP-1-based therapies -- were only slightly higher than with placebo, including nausea (12.5% vs 10.3%; all cases mild), vomiting (8.3% vs 6.9%), and diarrhea (8.3% vs 6.9%). No participants in the 150-mg arm discontinued treatment.

By comparison, pivotal trials of semaglutide and tirzepatide reported nausea in 44.2% (vs 17.4% on placebo) and 24.6% to 33.3% (vs 9.5% on placebo) of participants, respectively.

This multicenter, double-blind trial enrolled 125 adults with obesity or overweight and weight-related comorbidities. Participants were randomized to receive once-daily oral NA-931 at 60 mg, 90 mg, 120 mg, or 150 mg, or placebo. Mean participant age across the groups ranged from 41.9 to 46.7 years. Most were female (62.5% to 66.6%) and white (62.5% to 68.9%). Mean baseline weight was 212 to 220.9 lb and mean body mass index was 34.4 to 37.8.

Patients in the 150-mg group also saw a modest reduction in HbA1c levels (-0.2%).

Tran said Biomed Industries is advancing NA-931 into phase III clinical testing.

#5774. Volvo Trucks Realizes Universal Dream Of Life-Size Tonka Trucks With New Tech

Remote control trucks are cool and all, but haven't you ever wanted to remote control something bigger? Realer? Well, Volvo Trucks has your number. The company just introduced a new feature that allows operators to remote-control their own heavy-duty vocational truck — so long as it is on a job site, that is, and going under five miles per hour.

Volvo's new tech, Remote Steer, is just that — a way to steer and control the company's new VNX heavy-duty truck from a gaming style control from outside the vehicle. It may sound like a party trick, but Volvo actually makes a good case for its use: Rather than relying on communication between a driver and a spotter for precision jobs, like aligning a trailer hitch or pouring concrete, Remote Steer allows one person to do both jobs. A worker can stand behind their truck, at the trailer hitch, and use the remote to back the truck up perfectly without any risk of anyone saying "No, no, my left."

It wouldn't be Volvo Trucks if safety wasn't first and foremost in mind. The operator is responsible for switching on the system in the cab and linking the cab to a VIN-specific remote control. The remote control is married to only one VIN number, so there can't be an switch-em-up which could result in everything from hilarious shenanigans to untimely death. There is also a very limited range on the controller, as well as emergency stop buttons on both truck and controller. You'll also need to have all the latest and greatest Volvo Trucks tech to make it work, like Volvo Dynamic Steering, I-Shift transmission, electronic braking system, and a Body Builder Module. If you have that stuff, you can get the module installed aftermarket at any time.

Will this truck replace workers on the job site? Probably not. Ideally, Remote Steer will be used on job sites as a way to help existing workers rather than as a way to replace them. If that's the use case the tech sees, it'll likely be a genuine boon to the folks who have to do precision maneuvers on their trucks all day long. Then again, you might not see it at all, as Volvo Trucks, while sold worldwide to the tune of 120,000 trucks last year, has a smaller footprint here in the U.S than competitors like Mack (which is now a part of Volvo Trucks) or Kenworth trucks.

Either way, we hope we get invited to this press drive. Five miles an hour sounds like peanuts and, on the road, it is, but you don't need much more to dig around in a life-size sandbox with your buddies. Volvo's next move should be expanding to front loaders, excavators and Volvo Trucks-branded microbreweries that allow you to dig around and drink an IPA at the same time. The money would print itself.

#5772. Tau Therapy Shows Continued Benefit at 2 Years in Alzheimer's

— Open-label extension data suggest diranersen's treatment effects may build over time

Diranersen, an investigational antisense oligonucleotide therapy that targets tau, continued to show benefits for 96 weeks, data from the open-label extension of the phase II CELIA trial suggested.

At 2 years, diranersen continued to slow clinical decline across measures of cognition, function, and disease progression, reported Lawren VandeVrede, MD, PhD, of the University of California San Francisco, at the Clinical Trials on Alzheimer's Disease (CTAD) conference in Boston.

A 60-mg dose twice a year showed the largest effects; with that regimen, participants had a 1.09-point treatment difference in Clinical Dementia Rating-Sum of Boxes (CDR-SB) score at 96 weeks, surpassing the 76-week CDR-SB score, VandeVrede observed. CDR-SB scores can range from 0 to 18, and a 1-point change is considered meaningful, he added.

Treatment effects were calculated by comparing 60 participants who received diranersen during CELIA's placebo-controlled phase and stayed on the same dose through 2 years with 115 people who were originally assigned to placebo and then moved into the study's open-label extension.

On the CDR-SB, the treatment effect grew from 26% at week 76 to 38% at week 96, VandeVrede said.

Other measurements of cognition and function also improved from 76 to 96 weeks, he added. "The increasing separation observed between 18 months and 2 years is remarkable," VandeVrede noted.

Safety findings through week 96 were generally consistent with data from the placebo-controlled period. The most frequent adverse events were procedural pain, post-lumbar puncture syndrome, headache, and confusional state. Most confusional state events were mild or moderate, occurred within 7 days of dosing, and resolved within 7 days of onset.

Diranersen targets microtubule-associated protein tau (MAPT) RNA. It is designed to reduce both intracellular and extracellular tau.

CELIA was a phase II dose-ranging study of diranersen in individuals with early Alzheimer's disease. It enrolled 416 participants with mild cognitive impairment due to Alzheimer's disease or mild Alzheimer's disease dementia who had not previously received anti-amyloid therapy.

The study evaluated three doses of diranersen administered intrathecally over the 18-month placebo-controlled treatment period: 60 mg every 6 months, 115 mg every 6 months, and 115 mg every 3 months.

The primary endpoint was an assessment of dose response for change from baseline on CDR-SB scores at week 76. All diranersen doses tested in CELIA slowed cognitive decline, but because the lowest dosage performed best, the drug missed its primary endpoint of demonstrating a clear dose response. The ongoing open-label extension study is evaluating the long-term safety, tolerability, and durability of diranersen's clinical benefit.

"CELIA gave the field its first evidence from a randomized trial that lowering tau may slow cognitive decline, and today's 2-year data suggest that benefit may build over time," said Laura Nisenbaum, PhD, interim chief science officer of the Alzheimer's Drug Discovery Foundation in New York City, who wasn't involved with the study.

"The lowest dose appeared to perform best, but its safety and clinical benefit will need to be confirmed in a larger phase III trial," she noted. "Importantly, diranersen also lowered tau without affecting amyloid, making it a promising candidate to pair with anti-amyloid therapies as the field works toward combination treatments that address the full pathobiology of Alzheimer's."

New data from the placebo-controlled treatment period presented at CTAD showed that, at 18 months, the twice-yearly 60-mg diranersen group had favorable memory, orientation, and executive function scores compared with participants on other doses. Biomarker analyses showed that diranersen reduced tau PET signals and lowered soluble tau biomarker levels.

#5771. AGCO Celebrates Construction Milestone at Olmedo Solar Project in Spain

OLMEDO, Spain, Nov. 19, 2026 — AGCO (NYSE: AGCO) today announced that Project Olmedo, the solar installation at the center of its first Virtual Power Purchase Agreement (VPPA), has completed construction and commissioning and is expected to begin delivering renewable electricity to the Spanish power grid in the coming months, pending final regulatory approvals. BRUC, one of the largest renewable energy groups in Spain, built the new solar installation in the northwest province of Valladolid.

The milestone follows AGCO’s September 2025 announcement of its 10-year VPPA with BRUC. The installation’s two solar projects, Los Silos and El Granero, span approximately 300 hectares (741 acres) and hold approximately 150,000 solar panels. AGCO’s portion of the installation’s annual production is expected to be approximately 150 gigawatt-hours (GWh) of renewable electricity a year, equivalent to the annual electricity consumption of approximately 40,000 European households, based on Eurostat data.

“Project Olmedo will give AGCO long-term price certainty for the majority of the electricity certificate cost in Europe,” said Tim Millwood, Senior Vice President, Chief Supply Chain Officer. “In volatile energy markets, securing that stability for 10 years strengthens the resilience of our supply chain while supporting new renewable generation.”

A VPPA lets a company support new renewable energy projects without taking direct delivery of the electricity they generate. Because AGCO’s agreement supported the construction of a brand-new installation, it will add new renewable capacity to the grid rather than drawing on existing sources, a principle known as additionality. The agreement also helps reduce AGCO’s Scope 2 greenhouse gas emissions from purchased electricity in its Europe/Middle East (EME) region and supports its commitment to reduce absolute Scope 1 and 2 emissions by 55% by 2033 from a 2022 baseline.

“Additionality is what makes this agreement truly meaningful,” said Roger Batkin, Senior Vice President, Corporate Affairs, General Counsel & Corporate Secretary. “We did not simply buy renewable electricity that already existed; we supported the construction of a brand-new renewable solar project. Accomplishing this project required over two years of involvement from many teams, functions, and areas of expertise across AGCO, along with technical advisory support from Schneider Electric and a strong partnership with BRUC.”

Reflecting AGCO’s Farmer-First strategy, Project Olmedo supports local farmers to further diversify their land use by leasing land for the project while maintaining the option for livestock to graze among the panels, maintaining the vegetation in place of machinery.

AGCO and BRUC leaders, joined by local community representatives José Emilio Escudero García, Mayor of Bocigas, and Ursino Vara Pérez, Mayor of Almenara de Adaja, marked the milestone with a ribbon-cutting ceremony at the site in November. Additional drone footage from Project Olmedo and the ribbon-cutting event are available on AGCO’s YouTube page.

For more details on AGCO’s sustainability initiatives, visit the company’s sustainability webpage and read the 2025 Sustainability Report. For more information about BRUC, visit the company’s website at brucenergy.com.

AGCO (NYSE: AGCO) is a global leader in agricultural machinery and precision agriculture technologies. Driven by a Farmer-First strategy, AGCO delivers value through its differentiated leading brands, Fendt™, Massey Ferguson™, PTx™ and Valtra™.  AGCO’s high-performance equipment and smart farming solutions, including brand-agnostic retrofit technologies and autonomous offerings, empower farmers to drive productivity while sustainably feeding the world. For more information, visit www.agcocorp.com.

#5770. I've Found General Motors' Next CEO: Me

Hello America. It's always nice to talk to you again. When I heard that General Motors' second-longest serving CEO and first woman to take the helm of a major automaker, Mary Barra, is approaching retirement age, I started waiting patiently by the phone. Since I've yet to hear from The General, I'll make my pitch plain as day: Now is not the time for upheaval in the upper echelons of power. What GM needs is another reassuring, no-nonsense brunette woman from southeast Michigan with a square jaw and steely eyes. The candidate GM can not afford to overlook is me, Erin Marquis.

So what makes me the ideal candidate to replace the irreplaceable Mary Barra? It's not just our shared love of leather jackets and skin care. Barra swooped in to GM leadership following the unmitigated disaster of the ignition switch scandal way back in 2014, to reassure us all in her flat midwestern accent that everything would Be OK.

She then went on a firing spree that shook loose a lot of complacency at the company, pulling the stock out of a nose dive to the relatively healthy company we see today. If you need a reassuring midwestern gal with a proven track record of getting things done, you'll be hard-pressed to find anyone better.

Barra's spiked heels are some pretty big shoes to fill. You might be wondering if I have the ankle strength to take this gig on. She's been a heavy hitter in the industry, bring GM back from the brink when others would have driven the historic company off a cliff.

A little bit about myself: When Barra took the job I'd just moved out of my cube at the Renaissance Center, returning to journalism after three years in the GM trenches with what the cool kids call a Creative Agency of Record. I headed up a revamp of the global website, GM.com, and then worked with five GM brands (GM mashed all the discontinued brands together into an acronym POSH — Pontiac, Oldsmobile, Saturn and Hummer — and treated them as a brand) to bring customers back into the fold.

This led to several, real-life important skills that I think makes me a perfect candidate:

You have my number GM. Do the brave thing, the right thing, and make the call.

#5768. Where To Find The Paul Walker 'Fast And Furious' Tribute Car In GTA 6

Thanks to his portrayal of Brian O'Conner in the "Fast and Furious" franchise, the late Paul Walker will forever be connected with car culture. What some non-car enthusiasts might not realize is that Walker was also a gearhead offscreen, with several vehicles from his collection fetching high-dollar amounts at auctions, such as his 2000 Nissan Skyline R-34 GT-R that sold for $1.357 million in 2023. O'Conner, whose character arc was cop-turned-criminal-turned-cop and criminal car guy, is behind the wheel of a bright green 1995 Mitsubishi Eclipse in the franchise's first entry.

Sadly, that tuner's life was about as short-lived as its 10-second race with Dominic Toretto, as it was destroyed during a drive-by shooting led by the film's antagonist, Johnny Tran. Gone, but NOS forgotten, the lime green loser continues to garner attention long after its 2001 silver screen demise. Recent sightings include the Fast & Furious Exhibit at the Petersen Museum and, as of last night, the finally released "Grand Theft Auto VI." Although you can't touch the display car, you can drive the game version.

The best part is this easter egg is not a mod that you buy or farm achievements to earn. There is no mission to complete. You just have to find it. Oh, and then break into a house to access the garage before unlocking the vehicle with a, um, slim jim or your elbow. Hey, but then it's yours to drive away with.

The only vehicle in the garage is the Eclipse, complete with "F&F" signature colors, distinctive graphics, and a massive clothes rack masquerading as a rear wing spoiler. However, some report not seeing the car during their first visit, needing to return later for it to spawn. At first glance, the Mitsubishi looks the part. The in-game Eclipse isn't the real McCoy, though, but rather a very, very impassioned dead ringer of the film fandom version, like so many other cars spotted in GTA 6.

For example, O'Conner's car had Toyo Tires decals while the GTA 6 version hawks Terada Tires. The massive wing of the real Mitsu is silver and black, while the animated rendition is green and black. Even the graphics, although the same colors, are splashed on slightly differently. The wheels are six-spoked, brushed-aluminum in GTA 6, while the film car featured seven spokes and was bright silver. Furthermore, the game vehicle feels a lot faster than what you'd get from O'Conner's decidedly non-turbo, naturally-aspirated 2.0-liter.

But it doesn't matter. GTA 6 is a vivid representation of real life without using actual names of places and things, so yup, this is Paul's car ... I mean, Brian's. Whatever. Find it and furiously drive it through the streets of Vice City.
